For Beatrice

ཎྞཱ་ 

PART ONE 

1 THE MUSIC of the organ, electrically brought to extraordinary volume, waged an unending struggle for victory with the noise of roller-skates, brought by youth to extraordinary volume, on the perfect floor of the rink. Exaggerated music versus a gigantic coffee-grinder. Neither ever won. To ears unused to the continuous sound in tremendous roller-skating rinks the effect was little short of stunning. When the signal 

All-Skate was replaced by Clear the Floor the noise dwindled. Dorothy at the organ lowered the volume. The floor 1

men with arms spread stood at the wide gaps in the rail, shunting the skaters from the skating arena into the lounging and practice spaces. Then when the arena was empty, from these positions the eight floor men in their blue and gray uniforms, blue caps, white gloves, skated in various geometrical courses to their routine position in front of the organ. Skated? They flew, swam, glided, leaped, danced, and were suddenly there. Young men around twenty, rarely over twenty-five. A stride ahead of this line of instructors, the three instructresses took their position. Trim, pretty young women in winter uniform of royal blue satin. Jaunty blue cap with gold visor. Gold collar, with a V of gold insertion plunging into the blue. Gold at the wrists. Briefest ballet skirt leaving legs bare down to ankles where high white buckskin shoes clamped to skates completed the decorative professional costume. 

Very slowly, in swaying uniformity, the group skated to the center of the rink. The organ was silent. From the opposite rail, the floor manager in uniform skated forward to face them. All stood a moment at rigid attention. The electric sign on the organ stage changed to Couples. The instructors and instructresses separated, two women, two men, to stand in a line in the center of the floor, others to find partners in the crowd, others to skate on guard against accidents. 

Dorothy played a Strauss waltz. The floor was taken by couples only. 

Laurel in the center of the floor closed her eyes. "Number 

Three," she thought, and instantly her mind, against her will, photographed the unvarying schedule (interrupted only on gala nights when exhibitions were given) that was maintained in the seven great rinks operated by the company:

EVENING SESSION 1. ALL-SKATE Theme Song: ROLLING AMERICA 2. ALL-SKATE 3. COUPLES-Waltz & Fox Trot 4. ALL-SKATE-Tango & Waltz 5. NOVELTY 6. ALL-SKATE 7. COUPLES 8. ALL-SKATE 

10. ALL-SKATE-Waltz & Fox Trot 11. LADIES ONLY-Waltz, Fox Trot, Tango 12. REVERSE-Fox Trot & Waltz 13. BRONZE DANCES 14. ALL-SKATE-Waltz & Fox Trot 15. NOVELTY 16. ALL-SKATE-Waltz & Tango 17. WALTZ COUPLES 18. ALL-SKATE-Fox Trot 19. COUPLES-Waltz & Fox Trot 20. ALL-SKATE-Waltz, Fox Trot, Tango 21. BRONZE, SILVER, AND GOLD MEDAL DANCES THE NATIONAL ANTHEM 

3

Laurel went from mechanical schedule to plan. All-Skate was next; in the inner oblong, reserved for dance practice, acrobatic skating and free style, she would go through some spins, while in the outer great oblong the crowds went round and round with the deafening noise of the fabulous coffeegrinder. Her exhibition in New Brunswick to-morrow night. 

Would the little shop have the crimson costume ready?

Would the organist-Ann was at times a little slow-get enough fire into Hungarian Dance No. 5, Laurel's newly selected music for her free style exhibition? Oh, a hundred doubts! Perhaps all precarious careers were balanced on doubts.

The organ stopped. Clear the Floor flashed on the electric sign on the stage. The couples streamed through the openings in the rail. Laurel skated backward to stand at the rail in front of the organ. Eddie shot to her side, braking with one dragged skate to an abrupt stop. Then Harry, Pete came on a series of whirls and jumps, deer-graceful. The others lined up. And Alice and June. They skated slowly forward to the center of the floor to face the main entrance of the cavernous building. The floor manager in identical uniform rolled out to meet them. The twelve stood rigid.

All-Skate was signaled. Dorothy began a popular tune on the loudly amplified organ. The hundreds swarmed onto the floor.

Laurel went to the cleared center marked by a black line painted on the floor, a hundred feet long, thirty feet wide. 

Her pupils, Eddie's pupils, were already at practice. Youngsters from seven years old to twelve; girls and boys up to eighteen; young women, young men; hardly any over twenty-five. Laurel executed a toe spin. She slipped, fell flat, to regain her feet in an instant, unsmiling, intent. A long

scrape of faintly reddened flesh appeared on her thigh so white below the blue satin rim of the brief skirt. Impatient at the stinging burn she stood straight for an instant and then in one lithe powerful movement began the spin. Infinitely faster than a ballerina could dream of, her slim body whirled; her arms, outstretched from her sides in an unbroken line, sliced the air. The speed of the turning was terrific, incredible to newcomers in the spectators' seats surrounding the arena, almost incredible to pupils who had gathered to watch. Laurel Easton, Silver Medalist of the 

ROLLING AMERICA chain of rinks. She came out of the spin with the barest necessity of slowing down, and abruptly was motionless, standing on one skate, with angled leg drawn up in prescribed position, the skate held inward up against her knee. Flawless spin and recovery. At once she executed another spin. And two more. Then she skated backward in a free style solo dance. 

A hand caught hers, tugged. "Skate with me, Laurel. Please.' "I'm practicing. "Please." 

Laurel shrugged and joined the circling tide of skaters, her body in instant unison with that of the child, one of her favorite pupils. Jean, ten years old, laughed and chattered loudly above the organ as she skated with her grave, adored head instructress of the rink. She reached up to pop a large caramel between the protesting lips. "Stop it, Jean. I don't want it." But no one could refuse this little girl, and so

Laurel found herself skating with jaws working, as she knew, most undecoratively. Jean was a pest, a bundle of insistence and a darling. Laurel knew that every employee in the rink, from skate mechanic to manager, made her a favorite. She had

that certain something of an all-time winner. Sweetness and spontaneity and generosity. In her little white ballet costume she was a fairy princess. And she knew it, and traded on her charm, and bossed everybody and had her way, and everybody was glad she had her way. Above all, for Laurel, Jean was becoming a remarkable child skater. 

Another hand, not as small as Jean's, but as confiding, caught Laurel. "Hello, Laurel." "Hi, Beatrice." 

Thirteen years joined ten, in flanking twenty-three. Beatrice's height was almost Laurel's, five feet five, with figure just coming on, with fine straight legs, and a lovely face ardent and ever alive. Her dusky hair, very like Laurel's, was a banner when she skated. The three swayed and moved on, making a picture spectacular with the white costume of Jean, the royal blue and gold of Laurel, the pale green of Beatrice. di- 

Laurel thought, "Two swell kids. Nucleus of my group show in February. I'm glad the management decided against a Christmas show this year. With my own exhibitions all over the chain I simply wouldn't have the time for group rection. Uncommon kids, these two. Their devotion is embarrassing at times, but in this mad circus life, it's grand to know that they're with me. All the way."

She sighed as she glided past Eddie skating backward in his routine of guard work slowing down the crowd. Always skating backward, weaving in and out of the skaters with fantastic skill, putting a hand on this man's shoulder, touching that boy, smiling reproof at a girl who was going too fast. A minute later she would come face to face with Harry, and then with Pete. All trained to a split-second judgment in

this holding the skaters to safe pace. Each with a whistle.

Some one falls. A whistle shrills. The nearest floor men race to the jumble. One picks up the fallen. Another stands behind to split the oncoming stream. The tiny whirlpool is smoothed. Courteous, watchful, good-natured, the floor men resume their interrupted positions in backward skating. The noise of the giant coffee-grinder fights with the music of the electrically amplified organ. The routine and training in the rinks of ROLLING AMERICA. 

Passing Eddie, Laurel sighed and wondered why, of late, he disliked her so. They were dancing partners in so many shows around the circuit. Two professionals intent on their work, fired with parallel ambitions. Eddie was a marvelous skater. Built on springs. As tireless as she. Exuberant in nature and conduct: there resemblance ended, for she knew that exuberance in her was lacking. Severe to herself, severe to her work. Perhaps too much so. But if one was to go ahead- "Why is Eddie against me? There isn't one real reason." She shrugged away the sigh. "Nerves, I suppose.

He's been skating furiously for too long without a break. 

Maybe I have, too."

Circling under the flood-lights the skaters seemed cut off from the shadowed spectators as much by the glare as by the iron rail. Only by looking consciously over the rail was recognition of individuals probable. A side-glance now confirmed Laurel's intuitive sense that the elderly man was in his place. His place? Well, it could be called that. Once or twice a week for the past month he had been seated in the same chair. In the narrowed world of the rinks-narrowed in the same sense as circus life is narrowed-he became noticeable. No one seemed to know him. No friend ever sat with him. And little by little the feeling grew in Laurel that his

interest was primarily in her. When she danced in Couples beneath the lurid play of color wheels, with the white floodlights extinguished, she was conscious every once in a while of his face staring out at her over the rail, and she supposed that he ventured to stare because of the dimmed illumination. When the full white blaze poured again to the floor his gaze was averted, but it was, if in short side-glances, still given to her, and as she skated beyond him she could sense it upon her figure. Once she turned and confirmed her sense. The session over, she would forget the man.

Quickly she had dismissed her early idea that he might be a talent scout from Hollywood, or night-club manager looking for a skating act. His presence was too aloof, too continual, for anything like that. No. He was just an unimportant enigma. Then, a few days later, there he would be again. 

Yesterday at the refreshment bar near his habitual seat she had found herself at his side. They were both drinking chocolate milk. He was, she saw, of medium height, probably in his fifties, a man well dressed, neat. He wore dark trousers, a gray-blue sports jacket. The neatness was of cleanliness rather than of any attempt to be fussy or dapper. He simply wore good clothing casually. His hair was smooth silver, his face slightly florid under a faint tan enduring from summer. 

His features had a coin-like regularity. His eyes were gray, changeable. All this was visible in the mirror behind the counter, and in glances as their shoulders actually touched under pressure of the crowd at their sides. 

Suddenly he spoke. "I enjoy your skating, Miss Easton.

Believe me, it is art. Not even in the Ballet Russe is gracefulness more deeply presented." "Thank you." She was conscious of her awkwardness in the face of his suavity, and almost childishly she sucked choco-

late milk through her straw. He knew her name. Of course.

On the billboard in the lobby. On programs. In the pamphlet newspaper printed by the management. Laurel Easton, head instructress of the ELIZABETH ROLLER ARENA. Then 

Beatrice came and begged her to skate, and she glided away from her awkwardness. 

And to-night here he was in his place and stealing glances.

She swung around the turn at the far end of the rink, came down the long stretch, and turned again. She looked at him. 

This time he smiled uncertainly. She frowned inwardly. How silly of the old man! If he wanted to talk to her about skating why the deuce didn't he do it? Surely by this time he must understand the maintained convention of the rinks. 

The instructresses were, in a sense, hostesses. Any one was free to ask them questions, to ask to skate with them. A free courtesy was always returned. The peculiar thing about this man was that he did not skate and rather obviously was not interested in the skating of others, except hers. Heavens! If he was just an elderly masher she would pin his ears back in a hurry. For all his unimportance he was getting to be a bit of an annoyance with his gazing, and now—a smile. 

Still, what he had said yesterday was really nice to hear. He might be an artist of some sort and genuinely impressed by her work.

Now she was directly opposite him, not over five feet away. She turned a little, smiled over her shoulder. She was quite unconscious of the quality of this habitual response. 

A cold smile, shallow on her lips, offered suddenly, and for persons outside the narrowly disciplined circle of her affection, as suddenly withdrawn.

The schedule followed its invariable course. Laurel refused to skate Trios with Beatrice and Jean, deciding to sit through

that romping, noisy, fast number. The floor men permitted increased speed in Trios, for there were fewer on the floor, falls were infrequent, and groups of three were more easily watched than the patchwork of general movement. Laurel sank into an easy-chair near the skate room, closing her eyes against the glare for an instant's rest. "Tired, Miss Easton?" 

Laurel shook her head. "I'm never tired."

She frowned when the man laughed outright and dropped into the chair next to hers. "That's such a boast. Youth alone could make it." "Youth alone could live up to it." "Hmm. That's quite fast. Your eyes are laughing at me." "Sorry." "I'm not. May I talk a little? I mean, of course, shoutabove the organ and the roar of wheels." "Yes. I'm resting this number." "Does one rest if one is not tired?" "Certainly. In ten minutes I'll be practising a routine for an exhibition. Jumps, spins, arabesques. Prize-fighters sit in their corners between rounds, even before the first round when surely they aren't tired." Laurel turned a little toward him. She liked his grin of admitted defeat. "Take your rest," he said, reaching into his pocket, offering her a cigarette from a tan leather case. "No, thanks." "You don't smoke?" "No. The dust in these rinks is bad. They put a mixture of plaster-of-Paris and pumice on the floor. Why add smoke to that? I give my lungs a break." "How many hours a day do you skate?" "Well, there is one session nightly. Seven-thirty to eleven-

thirty. Every night. Six nights a week for me. Mondays I'm off duty. And on Wednesdays, Saturdays, Sundays and holidays there are afternoon sessions, too. Two until five.” "Humph. Four hours of skating, every day, and on the others three additional hours." "Plus private lessons I give before sessions, or in the morning. But I suppose counting this rest that you charge me with, seven hours would cover it.' "How the devil do you stand it?" "I love it." 

"You must. This place, too. As for me, I'm in a madhouse.

Such noise, such fast movement even outside the circling rail. Every one streaks. To chair, or refreshment stand, or cloakroom. A wild dash. And the organ bellowing, or screaming above the grating wheels-" "Dorothy would like that!" "Apology to Dorothy. She does splendidly. She really does. 

But the music is a-a bit powerful." "Could a violin be heard?" "No," the man said cheerfully. "Well, so much for the overwhelming quality. But I do begin to see-if not to understand-the order. There is a precision, almost military, in the way you instructors from out there on the floor introduce each number. Night after night it is the same. And at the end of the session-the playing of the National Anthem. It is all quite correct and impressive, Miss Easton, above the general uproar." "It is run exactly that way in every one of the company's seven rinks. All are in New Jersey. ROLLING AMERICA is the trade name covering the chain." "I begin to see, too, that gaiety and youth are at flood-tide. 

It is no place for the old. Even the manager is a young man."

"Old persons skate here." "Yes. But they are the exception. And it seems to me that they require what is given. Extreme care and skill from the floor men. Falls are common. When youngsters fall they seem to bounce up again—” 

Laurel interrupted. "I see what you mean. But youth needs the watching, trained eyes of the floor men just as much. You must have noticed the instantaneous rush of floor men to a tumble. Skates can cut. A pile-up of oncoming skaters can be pretty serious. So Pete and Harry and the others, weaving their way slowly backward, through the faster crowd, politely slowing it down, fairly fly to a danger spot. I've seen it for years and still I wonder at it." "For years. 

"Four. I got my first skating job at nineteen; it was only a part-time arrangement. Some newspaper had sponsored a two-mile race on Long Island, near where I was living at the time. Outdoor race. On poor skates. I entered for the fun of it and the faint hope that I might win a fourth or fifth place prize. I was first. Then with the twenty-five dollars in prize money I bought some clothes-" Laurel stopped, suddenly realizing that she was telling too much to this stranger. 

He stared at her. “Don't think that,” he said gently. “I'm deeply interested.” "The newsmen managing the race told me of large roller rinks, gave me a medal, along with the money, and I applied for a job. I was taken on, as I said, for part-time work. For crowded sessions, holidays, all that. For six months I towed beginners around the rink, or taught them how to stand up in the spaces outside the rail. After my first excitement at

actually being in a rink, on a perfect floor, and getting paid for it, I found the work terribly dull. That everlasting slow rounding with some one clinging to me for support. As I grew better I went from rink to rink. Not in the ROLLING 

AMERICA chain. I looked at the head instructresses and made my vow." "You've kept it." "Yes. I worked hard. Practising in every snatched five minutes. Studying steps, elementary dances. Going on to jumps, acrobatic skating, free style, advanced dancing. 

Every one helped me. They are that way. Oh, there are the usual artistic and professional jealousies and feuds among us, but we help each other-always. Well, I worked on for months, got on full time employment. Then I could practise. 

I joined ROLLING AMERICA and last spring after taking two full tests, was made an instructress in this rink. Now I'm head instructress." "What comes next?"

Laurel's lime-green eyes looked straight at him with cold protest. "I don't know. I'd rather not talk before things happen." "You are wise. Then let me go on a bit. I was telling you that little by little I begin to understand how well this place is run. Except for the dust you mentioned, the place is spotless. And your Dorothy, playing so well up on that stage, is outstandingly decorative. Framed with the organ in that ornamental scarlet and gold shell." 

Laurel stood up abruptly. "Time for All-Skate. And my practice if I don't have to skate with any one.' "Good-by. It's been nice to talk and to listen." 

She nodded and moved out to the cleared floor and her

place in the introduction of the next number. Her limber body swayed in rhythm. Her firmly fleshed bare legs were white proofs of flawless coördination.

When Laurel left the rink the flamboyant illumination of the entrance had not yet been turned off. Electric colors, blue, gold, crimson, broken into a thousand flickering spots by tiny squares of glass, blazed across the huge concrete front of the building. Wheels of skates painted in electricity revolved in fires of prismatic glass. As Laurel glanced back from the corner where her bus would stop, the arena went gray, folding into the murky night. The bus lurched on, and 

Laurel sat with half-closed eyes, thinking of sleep. The stranger had been right. She was tired. To-night she would skip her leg exercises, that prescribed process of limbering derived to professional skaters from the invariable ritual of ballet dancers. She would not even cream her face. The dust of the rink could stay there. If a few pimples should come- 

Laurel opened her eyes, changed her mind. She would cream her face.

She slipped her key quietly into the lock of the front door of Mrs. Green's house. The hall was dark. A second key opened the door of her front room on the ground floor. She touched the switch, and two lamps showed a bedroom with adequate, if unattractive, furniture, single bed, two chairs, a table, a bureau. A clothes closet opened from the inner wall. Two windows faced the street. Shelter! A room of her own. Home? Home was the rink.

She undressed quickly, stood before the wall mirror over the bureau and removed make-up and, presently, impurities from her skin. A pale thin face appeared, with sharply chiseled features. Her eyes in shadow lost their lime-green color and seemed dark and hollow. Her hair brushed down to the

nape was dark brown. Under her nightgown her breasts formed rather small, round.

In a robe she went to the bathroom at the end of the hall, and returned, opened the window a few inches, and touched the room to darkness. Six paces to the bed. A slight shiver as the cold sheets penetrated thin silk nightgown. 

One knee was drawn up almost to her chin. The hollow of an arm pressed her breasts. Her nose burrowed into the pillow. She was asleep.

PRINCETON WAS white. The tolling bell on Nassau Hall was hushed in the smothering fall and from the brightly lighted dormitories undergraduates plunged ankle-deep on their way to supper, underclassmen to the dining halls of Commons, upperclassmen to the clubs on Prospect Street. Snowballs, yells, quiet plodding along the paths of the campus. The interior of the University Store was bright with books, stationery, sports clothes. 

Dr. James Saunders, Director of the Neville Conservatory of Music a half-mile north of Princeton, was turning the leaves of a new anthology of poetry offered by a friend. "It seems excellent," the clerk said. "Have you "Yes." read it?" "So have I. In proofs. And you are correct. It is excellent. 

Wrap it, please. The snow is no friend of book covers. Put it down to my account."

Dr. Saunders turned to the man who had just come to his side before the counter. "Hello, Easton. Can you lend me snowshoes?"

Austere, kind, knowing thoroughly not only his beloved conservatory where he had dwelt for forty years, but also a great proportion of Princeton University's faculty, Dr. 16

Saunders smiled. He regretted mildly that this instructor appeared startled. Too bad. Too bad. The fellow just seemed not to get along in any orderly progress. Half-way through the fifties, still an instructor, still a stranger to Neville warmth, after ten years. A mere holder of a berth. Obviously without ambition. He would, Dr. Saunders mused, have been let go, except for the satisfactory results with eager students who clearly liked the man and his methods. Well, music was a peculiar field. The man's record before coming to Neville established a hard worker at two schools of music, a conscientious teacher. His personal life seemed exemplary, save for the mark of the recluse. No social life at Neville touched him outside the routinary obligatory functions. He lived alone in Princeton, in a three-room apartment on Nassau Street where Neville students often visited him in conference, departed, humming and satisfied. His punctuality was known. He was neat, quiet, pleasant and withdrawn. 

One serious criticism only against him, Dr. Saunders recorded: in the past two years or so Easton had attended fewer and fewer performances of music at the conservatory, or on the Princeton campus where Neville men were warmly welcome. Famous string quartets, soloists, orchestras were important in Princeton-Neville life, and the faculties of the two old institutions, one so large, one so small, were always generously represented, but Easton, at these centers of stimuli in his own realm, was becoming an infrequent listener. A strange lapse. "Imagine my absence from a Spalding concert! Or any other. Yes, yes. It is almost a dereliction from-" Dr. Saunders shrugged. The man did his work. His students progressed. The head of his department seemed satisfied. So?

"I say, Easton. Some men are coming to my rooms this evening. With their wives. Quite informal. Would you care to drop over? The Harvard organist is coming. I think you'd find him interesting. Come along.' 

The clerk handed the wrapped book to Dr. Saunders. "Thank you, Doctor. Thank you very much. But I'm leaving town right after supper. By bus. Quite roundabout to 

Elizabeth. Thirty-five miles. I'm sorry." "Some other time, then. Sorry you'll miss the organist. 

Quite a chap, I'm told. Good-night."

Charles Easton, a new mystery novel tucked under his arm, stood on the step of the store for a moment before plunging into the snowstorm. Already the tall gaunt figure of the noted professor of music had been swallowed by the silent white hosts. The famous bell on the campus tolled the hour with muffled voice. The walk from the University 

Store to Nassau Street was but a couple of hundred yards and on the corner of Nassau and Witherspoon Streets was the old building in which, on the second floor, Charles had his bedroom, sitting-room, kitchenette and bath. To-night he prepared coffee, toast, a salad, and ate sparingly even of this light spread on his living-room table. A mounting excitement captured him. This mere bite of supper. Yes. Yes. But late to-night in Elizabeth he would find a restaurant, order steak, good vegetables, good dessert, and opposite him-oh, it had to be! It would be!-would be seated Laurel. With wily tongue or frank begging he would persuade her to take a late supper with him after the session was over. That young woman he had been surely, slowly approaching during the past month. Slowly? Charles muttered, “In outward fact, yes, slowly. But inwardly I've been caught and held with dismaying speed." The mutter hushed in thought. With each

visit to that damned rink his desire to know her had increased in geometrical progression. 

On the first visit a shocking answer to curiosity. What a cold, inadequate word, curiosity! No. It had been more than that. When a student had idly told of skating one night in the ELIZABETH ROLLER ARENA, of the graceful, attractive head instructress, Laurel, he had felt something more than curiosity. There had been a distinct and powerful pull upon a heart he had regarded as quite cold, shriveled, under a thick crust of unenthusiastic years. Could this girl be 

Laurel? She had to be. An uncommon name. The student had said she was in her very early twenties. That, too, was affirmative.

Charles' face stiffened as he vividly recalled going to that rink thirty-five miles from Princeton, where a placard in the lobby had told him she was Laurel. And he had sat cowering, to see a young woman in royal blue and gold, cloaked in an incomparable grace of her own, skate out to head a company of professional skaters. His heart had turned over. And he had groaned, "In all this God-damned icy lonely life, the life I have made for myself, its sole asset a reasonable security of food and shelter and clothing, there, out on that smooth floor, is the only human being I might have loved. 

Loved!" Charles stood up, sweating. The word love. The mockery, the accusation of that! A potential love he had run from, staggered from in drunken fog. Laurel. A blanketed mite of six months in the blurred horror of that poverty-painted funeral parlor of an East Side undertaker's premises in New York. Nineteen-seventeen. Twenty-three years ago. The dead mother in the cheap coffin her own mother had paid for. Laurel's mother, she—

Charles looked at the clock on the mantel. He wiped his

brow, pulled on a coat, and went out to stand on the corner where the bus to Elizabeth would stop. His thought was disciplined again. He looked at Nassau Hall, the vague shadows of flanking buildings. The Princeton campus. It was a permanent scene across the street from his home, but half a mile from the small campus of the conservatory where his very humble professional life was conducted. He was, Charles thought for the hundredth time, a sort of musical mole, who ate well, slept well.

The bus loomed in the snow. 

Charles' chin sank into his silk muffler in the opening of his ulster, and the miles passed in white silence outside the crusted windows. Laurel returned. How delicately he must move. Her grandmother would have brought her up in just hate and loathing of him. The grandmother in her own poverty had been rich in character in contrast with him, the drunken husband of the girl who had died after but little over a year of wretched, neglected, loveless marriage. 

Twenty-three years. And now a Neville teacher was riding toward his daughter to offer-what? Well, there was a security of modestly comfortable life. The companionship of a man who had risen from filth. But this risen figure must combat, replace the vague, sinister form the grandmother had accurately given. Unless she had told the growing child nothing. Probably the old woman was dead. Oh, probably, probably, possibly, possibly! Futile to try to guess. And was he sane or insane in this belated action? And was it action or gesture? And gesture of what? Remorse? No. Not that.

After twenty-three years! Was it because he was fifty-five and wandering into lonely age? No. He was too indifferent, too phlegmatic for that. Was it parental love for this grown young woman? N-no. Well, what then? he asked himself

savagely. The only answer was, "That distinct and powerful pull upon your atrophying heart." Through the early years 

Laurel had been a drink-blurred memory, beyond his sight. 

Later, she had slipped away to even less, to nothing. Then, suddenly, a month ago, he had seen a grown woman and the woman was his daughter. No love had sprung full grown to match her full growth. He could not even like her, for he did not know her. He could feel only a tug, a hurt. 

So now he was riding on a bus to a noisy roller-skating rink. And to-night he would by wile or cajolery or some disguised force take her to supper. Step by step. Each precarious moment must give its own answer. No man could see the end.

The snow burst into amazing color outside the window. 

He was looking into the opening in one of those magic Easter eggs he had known in childhood, which displayed, through falling flakes, a house of scarlet and green and blue and gold. But in this modern egg was no farmhouse home.

He saw a great façade of chromium and glass and blazing electricity and fluid neon brilliance.

Charles bought a ticket. Inside he checked his coat and hat, and went at once to his accustomed chair. The number was, he recognized at once, Trios, and nervously he peered into the circling rush to catch a figure in royal blue and gold. He would catch Laurel's eyes, smile at her. Later he would watch for her to come to the refreshment stand for chocolate milk. He would be standing beside her. A few words then. Perhaps a request that she sit out a number and tell him a little about the rink, or about an advertised exhibition of hers he would like to attend. All easy enough if he could keep the damned tension out of his voice. 

The rush of countless trios swept wind into his face as

he peered into the lurid lighting of this fast-skated number of the night's routine. Slimness in blue and gold. Ah. 

There! But it was June skating between two young men in crimson jumpers. He leaned forward. Rounding the turn another figure in uniform skated wide with flanking youngsters. Alice! He wondered a little at the ease with which even a spectator in this little world came to think of every one by first name. Laurel. June. Alice. Eddie. Harry. Pete.

Ben, the ticket-taker. The informality of Neville did not reach this. To students he was Mr. Easton. To colleagues who knew him as well as any one knew him he was Easton. "Come to think of it, no one in the world calls me by first name. No one is interested enough to do it. But an instructor in ROLLING AMERICA is Eddie or Laurel to any ten-year-old.

Humph! It's, it is kind of nice-in an unimportant way." 

A minute passed. There was June again. And a few moments later, Alice. Laughing in their unit lines of three. 

Trios ended. The white flood-lights replaced the color. The floor was emptied. The instructresses and instructors formed before the organ, skated out slowly in matched strokes to the center, where the floor manager came to face them. No 

Laurel. Charles waited a moment to see if, delayed, she would coast out in gull flight to join the formal pattern. 

Then as All-Skate was signaled he walked back to the refreshment stand, where a floor man was drinking Coca-Cola. "Can you tell me if Miss Easton is here tonight?" "Oh, Laurel? No. She was. She'd just got into her costume after giving a private lesson, an' the doors were opening when she was called to Trenton to give a free style exhibition." "On such short notice!" "That's the way it's done sometimes. She's almost halfway

to Trenton now on the bus. In an hour or more she'll be gettin' into her show clothes and sittin' around in a dressing-room waiting to do arabesques an' cartwheels an' spins." "Will she be back to-night?" "Here? No." "Well-thanks. Have a cigarette?"

In the flare of Charles' match cupped against the strong wind from the skaters the instructor caught a wince of sadness in the eyes of this queer middle-aged man who came so often to sit through night sessions in the same chair. 

Jerry thought, "Nice-lookin' guy. What the hell's his game? 

Oh, well, takes all kinds to make a rink. Jeez. Looks like he's goin' to cry!" So Jerry said, “Any message for Laurel? 

I'll see her to-morrow afternoon." "No. No thanks. Very kind of you to offer."

Charles threaded an unsafe way through the crowd outside the rail. This wide space where skaters moved idly, swiftly, crazily back and forth, or slumped into chairs for rest, with banging skates, unpredictable movements, always seemed to him the maddest, noisiest place in this mad noisy world of singing skates. Figures rushed at him, braked nervously, stumbled, fell, swept past. To-night he was not as watchful as usual, for he was dull with disappointment. 

Everything had pointed toward this night when his courage was ready. Weeks of spiritual, emotional preparation, at first, perhaps, unconscious. The risk of actually confronting 

Laurel. To find in her loathing, perhaps active hate, certainly dislike and distrust. After careful consideration the instrument of a letter had been tossed aside-literally one attempt into his waste-basket. Bah! It was no matter for a letter. 

It must be a meeting between two human beings who were blood and flesh and heart of each other's blood and flesh and

heart. Better to say, "Miss Easton" and look into young eyes that would gather accusation, than to write, “Laurel dear,” and go on in smoothly written words. Better, by God, a stumbling tongue than a precise and fluid composition. . . . 

Charles got into his thick coat, stood for a long time waiting for the bus to Princeton. Then, seated in its brassy interior, he yielded to his nervous disappointment. The white miles stole past. He thought dully of arrival in Princeton. He would not eat. No appetite. But a cup of coffee in the Balt. 

Then bed. "Good Lord!" He sat up with a jerk, glanced at his wrist.

Five to nine. This was the bus to Trenton! Princeton was only a way-station on the line. Why, the child must have passed him when he was riding to Elizabeth. Trenton was but ten miles beyond Princeton. Extra attractions at the 

ROLLING AMERICA rinks were always scheduled for ten o'clock. Hah! He would make it with a half-hour to spare.

Ten minutes of hard work, of tingling joy because she knew that seldom, if ever, in exhibition had she skated better. It was over and Laurel, gliding out to make a bow in the drumming of sharp applause, maintained with difficulty the gravity of her expression. She wanted to laugh, to whirl her arms in time with the rapid beating of her heart.

But that was not in the long-projected personality of Laurel Easton, so she gave her brief, cold smile, stood erect fifty feet out on the floor, bent her leg in curtsy, and withdrew in a swift and incomparably lovely backward arabesque, a leg slanted out behind her in upward flight, body arched in sweet curve, arms spread wide apart behind her waist.

All-Skate was signaled and she slipped through the throng

to the dressing-room. In three minutes she was out of her exhibition costume and in an ordinary blue plaid skating dress that reached almost to her knees. In another minute lipstick and powder were applied. In still another she was among the skaters.

At intervals as she moved along she came face to face with the backward-skating floor men, all of whom she knew from transfers and visits around the circuit. Some of them grinned, two or three moved their hands as if in applause, but there was little personal warmth, and Laurel told herself for the hundredth time that she must ignore this regrettably general attitude among the employees of ROLLING AMERICA. 

It was, of course, her own fault. She had realized it almost from the beginning. Coolness drew coolness. She had worked so hard, with such febrile concentration week in and week out, that a coating of indifference had formed over her manner, her face, and in her voice. Her pupils-for the most part, of course, amateurs-seemed to like her thoroughly, but her fellow-instructors held some sort of inactive resentment. She saw this clearly, but she was too young for immediate compromise and, figuratively, shrugged her rather square shoulders, and skated on into her career. "I mean to be friendly, I am friendly, but when I'm working, I just can't remember to be soft. When I'm teaching a class, and want a run of faster music, I know Dorothy resents my yell from the floor up to the stage, and I suppose I'm scowling, but she ought to know it isn't at her. I'm thinking of the dance steps to come the next minute, and I'm impatient and-oh, what difference does it make? I'm a gal on the way up and whatever I do will be done with my skates, not with a smile plastered all over my mug." "Hi, Jimmy."

"Hello, Laurel. How are things in Elizabeth?" 

A chill touched her as he slid away from her to touch a speeder on the shoulder. Why hadn't he smiled, grabbed her arm, perhaps turned to skate with her for a few yards, and told her how well her exhibition had gone? That was surely the way of the rinks. Oh, well. The same old stuff. 

Popularity? Blast popularity! Rink fame, that was the goal. 

And increased pay. And the joy that came on singing skates. This last most of all.

So alike were these rinks in structure, design, atmosphere, that Laurel, rounding the end near the refreshment stand, forgetting for a moment that the ELIZABETH ROLLER ARENA was forty-five miles away, glanced automatically at a certain chair behind the rail. "But this is Trenton," she gasped. “And there the old idiot sits! What is this, the long prelude to rape? 

Of all the-" But she answered his uncertain smile with the artificial shallow movement of her lips that she honestly considered a natural smile. She forgot the man as the floor was cleared. The floor men and Trenton instructresses formed in front of the organ, the floor manager skated out to meet them. The great flood-lights of white were extinguished and Waltz & Tango was begun beneath the dream-inducing streams of red and green and blue. An acquaintance from the New Brunswick rink came to Laurel's side of the rail. "Waltz, Laurel?"

She smiled and moved with him out to the floor. "I ran over to catch your show and try the floor here. Both tops. "Thanks. How is Hilda making out at New Brunswick?" "Q.K. as far as routine is concerned. But she isn't a

fighter." Bending his knee, the young man slipped, and Laurel caught, sustained him, without interrupting the waltz. "Sorry, Laurel. Thanks. Oh, yes. Hilda. She's a swell instructress, in dance and free style, too, but she lacks that little something that means glamour-" "You mean that big something, that everything." "Guess you're right. Anyway, everybody likes her." "She's liked all around the circuit," Laurel said. "But popularity won't advance her beyond a certain point with this outfit. She'll become head instructress at New Brunswick, I think, and I hope, but she'll never, in my opinion, which you needn't quote, be in line for head instructress of all the rinks. She could not in a thousand years teach the floor crews. Professionals would find her soft. You've seen enough to know that ROLLING AMERICA is pretty tough on personal ambition." "What about you? You seem to be climbing." "I study, work-eat, think, and dream skates." "So does Hilda." 

Laurel smiled narrowly. "You just said she lacked that certain something." 

He slipped again. "I'd better stop gassing, and tend to my feet. It wouldn't do to disgrace you with a fall on your exhibition night."

They danced on. A tango followed the waltz. 

The floor was cleared. "Drink?" he suggested. "I'd love a chocolate milk.”

Sipping, she indicated with a movement of her head the middle-aged man whose profile was visible among the skaters waiting for All-Skate. “Ever seen him around, Bill?” "No. Who is he?"

She shrugged. "That's what I wonder. He's in Elizabeth a lot. Just sits like that. Probably some screwball with nowhere to spend his nights." "Here he comes now. He's no screwball. A swell-looking man. Good clothes, too. Finished? Thanks for the dance. 

Well, I'll be seein' you. So long.” He slid away. "Good evening, Miss Easton. I'd like to tell you how much I enjoyed your show.” "Thanks," Laurel said. "Just what differentiates free style from other specialties in skating?" "It's what I did to-night. Highly individualistic work.

Spins, arabesques, spread-eagles. It's the skater's interpretation of ballet dancing. I added a little acrobatic business for the crowd, a cartwheel, that sort of thing. I don't like acrobatic skating. It is discordant—that is, I think it is.” "Thank you. A bit at a time I'm learning a little about this world of the rinks." "Why do you want to know about it? You don't skate. Are you connected in any way?" "No. Let's say interested." "I'll probably see you at Elizabeth- "You shall." 

Laurel caught a peculiar emphasis in his tone. She said quietly, "I'll be glad to answer any questions. That's part of my work there. Good-night. I'm leaving now. I'm very hungry. Two sandwiches for supper in the Elizabeth rink just before the session opened. Then the unexpected summons to 

Trenton. A performance is always exciting. Now I'm ravenous.' "How late do the buses run to Elizabeth?" "Oh, very late.”

"Would you have your delayed dinner with me now? Then we take the same bus. I get off ten miles north of here at 

Princeton. Please don't refuse. I want to talk to you. Believe me, Miss Easton, I'm not trying to get fresh, or to annoy you-" 

Now her quick smile was genuine. “A dinner would not annoy me, I can tell you that bluntly, and even gratefully. 

Your mysterious interest in skating—” "Will be solved after we consume, I suggest, a steak and fried potatoes." "Let's go,” Laurel said. "Wait here. I'll be back in a flash.”

Removing her skates in the dressing-room, pulling on coat and hat, she wondered without much interest of the hour before her. There would be, she knew, good solid food- "And gosh, I need it!"-and there would be the solution of this minor puzzle. It was believable-almost-that the man wasn't trying to pick her up. Unless he was very clever. Yet some men did feel the glamour of performers and pursued it just as this one did, at first keeping at a distance and yet within sight, and then slowly approaching until the moment seemed exactly right for the sudden swoop. "Well, he's hit the right moment," she muttered. “I feel good after my show, I'm starved, I have an hour or so to spare." She shrugged, walked out to the refreshment counter, and moved through the maelstrom at the man's side. 

They were in the deepening snow on the sidewalk. They were in a taxi. They were in a brightly lighted restaurant. 

He spoke to a headwaiter, and then they were in a little alcove facing each other across a table-not the cold, marbletopped table she was accustomed to, with paper napkins and glaring light, but one covered with heavy linen, correctly arranged with bright cutlery, bearing large stiffly folded nap-

kins. A busboy tilted a carafe of water, provided butter, and vanished. A waiter stood at close attention. 

Laurel ignored the confusing menu. "You order, please. 

I really don't care what I have. I'm just hungry. You suggested steak. That sounds grand." 

He gave quiet, detailed selection. Porterhouse steak medium, French fried potatoes, peas. "Will you have a cocktail, a wine, Miss Easton?" "No, thanks. I'd like coffee." 

When they were alone she said, "I warn you I'm going to eat everything on my plate. Every last bit. The show following a couple of cold sandwiches and a long bus ride, the chilly taxi, and now this warm contrast. Oh, will it take very long for the steak to cook?” "That delights me. If you only knew how much. Now munch your hot roll until ambrosia comes.' She saw him look away suddenly and hold his gaze on distance. She was able to scrutinize him through narrowed eyes. He really was, she thought, handsome for his age.

Slender, fairly erect. The silver hair was brushed neatly from its part on the side, his face was soap-clean, his collar and shoulders free of the least speck of dust so often neglected by men almost old. Then he turned to her, began to talk of the rink, and Laurel realized sharply that he was very nervous. The quiet assurance with which he had ordered their dinner was gone. Suddenly he seemed an unhappy man trying to act as though he were a happy one at some sort of triumphal hour. More than ever now, Laurel admitted to herself, she was in the dark. She wanted to say, “Look here, bluntly let me have it, what is it all about? Do you think you've fallen in love with me? Or are you out to buy favors, or what?”

Wonderful steaks were presented on bright silver platters, a superlative coffee, like none Laurel had ever tasted, was poured from a slender spout-and strain was lessened for a while. "You aren't eating," she said coldly. “You make me feel so greedy."

His immediate obedience amused her, but, under consideration, added to her impatient curiosity. Then to her further suspicion. Wouldn't this be exactly the way of old men craving young women? A succulent steak could mean little or nothing when young luscious flesh sat almost within arms' reach. "Stop that," she told herself, and launched into description of her professional environment which, she noticed, came to him with immediate relief. "In ten weeks of exhibitions I haven't had a spill. Oh, when it does come! It will be a staggerer. Sometimes, of course, it is the skater's fault. A bit too much effort, a miscalculation on a fast turn, and bang! Sometimes it's just bad luck. A thin coin, lost on the floor, lies on just the place your wheel strikes-and you are flat on your back or stomach, jarred heavily, with breath knocked out. You must get up, smile, pretend you aren't hurt and enraged at the ruin of your act. Or it is a wedge of chewing-gum-none is sold in the rinks for this reason, but people bring it in from outsidethe one spot in the whole rink and for some horrible reason your skate touches it. You see, rink skates and rink skating are wholly different from the street skating of old. Perfect floors built by specialists, perfectly made skates with wheels of flawless precision, make you skate without thought of obstruction." “I can understand that. It is a fundamental part of the whole display."

An elaborate dessert was ordered. "Another thing," he said, "is this question of costumes.

I mean at general sessions. There is a large proportion of decorative dresses. Pronouncedly on the ballet side." 

Laurel smiled. "That's part of my job. It's called pioneer work by an instructress. When a new rink first opens and for some time after, the public dresses in street and ordinary sports clothes. Little by little, as the kids and young women get to know me, I encourage them to get skating costumes. 

And visitors from other rinks help by example. Here and there on the floor appear the figures in bright colors, with bare legs and perhaps chiffon sleeves and-oh, you've seen it! They take to it quickly for freedom and appearance.” "You're a smart girl, Miss Easton.” 

She saw his long thin fingers toy with a spoon. 

Suddenly he stared straight at her. "You must have wondered, you must be wondering now, why I've been following you. For that, clearly, is what I have been doing. Loneliness-" He stopped. "Oh-oh," she thought. "Here it comes. The lonely old man, the tempting young woman. That stale old story!" 

She saw his throat bulge in a convulsive swallow. "Miss Easton, my life up to a few years ago has been a tough one. I want to tell you parts of it. Brief bits. Will you listen?"

She said gravely, "We've plenty of time. I may, most impolitely, fall asleep. There is a stillness in this room. The dinner was so much more than I-" "And you're tired—” 

She shook her head. "Tired? Remember my boast in Elizabeth."

"It is a night deep in winter. There is stillness outside, too. 

But I'll try to keep you awake until our bus is due." 

Laurel did not fall asleep. .... New York City, 1912. 

At three in the morning the air in Billy's cellar all-night joint was a fog of smoke, a reek of alcohol, a spring of singing. 

A police captain out of uniform ordered cocktails, and a blonde from the first row in the chorus of a current Broadway success widened lovely, humid, lubricous eyes and tightened her arm around the captain's neck. An extremely successful pickpocket gazed from another table at the chorus girl. The drunken police captain stared recognition at the pickpocket and called out, “O.K. on pockets, oldtimer, but not another man's broad," and the Broadway pickpocket coughed and beckoned to a waiter. The waiter came, departed, returned with whisky and, standing erect beside the table, breathed deeply, and sang in sweet tenor, My Wild

Irish Rose. Then bedlam of talk in the cellar. And another waiter, after service to a table in the long narrow cellar, sang in baritone. Ballads and hits of the day. Sentimental songs for the college boys, police, politicians, whores and criminals of The Great White Way of 1912. Every half-hour, in a row reaching from wall to wall across the cellar, all the waiters lined up, with a couple of sopranos, and the iron rafters rang with gay song from surprisingly good voices.

At the piano a young man with hollow eyes played, drank whisky, a limp cigarette glued with spittle to his lips.

1 .. Coney Island, 1913. 

A tender dawn was stealing in rose and lavender and gold over a quiet sea that washed the bathing beach littered with the newspapers and trash of yesterday's hundred thousand. In Rafferty's small pavilion just behind the famous boardwalk a man reached across a sloppy table and slugged a woman flush on the mouth. A half-dozen waiters leaped on him, rushed him to the door and flung him onto the boardwalk. The manager snarled at the weeping, collapsed, half-conscious woman, “Get out, you bitch, an' don't come back! This is a decent place!" He gestured furiously toward the platform at the end of the pavilion where, at the piano, a young man with hollow eyes ceased staring, gulped from a glass and resumed playing, a limp cigarette glued with spittle to his lips. .... New York City, 1914.

In an overdecorated, but cheaply furnished Greenwich Village studio in which no one ever seriously painted, sculptured, wrote, sang, danced, or studied, two dozen or more fairies were dancing and flirting. Powder, rouge, mascara, false breasts, silk stockings, falsetto voices, mannish coats and collars painted the sweet, sweet picture. A tall youth in a black evening gown pouted, flounced away from a scolding dancing partner and minced defiantly over to the piano in a shadowy corner. "Hello, dear."

The pianist scowled, shook off the delicate hand that had come to rest on his thin hunched shoulder. The sulky reddened mouth of the drunk youth in the evening gown grew straight and hard. 

Suddenly he cried shrilly, "Everything at my party has

gone wrong. And now even you! Get out! Everybody get out!" "Keep your God-damned hand off me; I'm hired to play the piano, and that's all!"

The squall moved off in the fetid air.... At last money was collected; the hired pianist was paid. 

A young man with hollow eyes breathed deep of daybreak's air on the steps of the house. He stuck a cigarette in his lips. "Never again! By Christ, I'll starve first!”

Down the street he found a dimly lighted back entrance of a saloon. "Whisky straight," he mumbled. ... New York City, 1914.

The small chartered boat moved insignificantly up the Hudson.

The ward boss's face was apoplectic. But he would not curse in the ears of women and children, these annual guests from his political domain. He said bitterly to his lieutenant in political thievery, "Conway wished this key-thumper on me. He walked aboard all right. An' here we're not off 

Grant's Tomb an' the guy is too drunk to sit on his stool, let alone play. Take him downstairs and squirt coffee down his throat, and ammonia up his nose. We gotta have music...."
