New York City, 1916.

An old young man of thirty-one stood at the window 1

of a rear room on the third floor of a shabby lodging-house on far East Twenty-eighth Street. New York was preparing to breakfast. The man was preparing to shoot himself. He gazed out at the dreary back yards just visible through the fluttering gray flags of the district's wash strung from building to building. Gray battle-flags of a thousand toiling women. Sheets upon which young brides had sweetly lain, sheets upon which the shriveled bodies of hags had tossed and groaned, sheets upon which poverty had laughed, cursed, writhed, slept peacefully, conceived, and died. 

The man turned away, fumbled through his pockets for a cigarette he knew was not there, looked under a washstand for a bottle he knew was empty. He shivered in the cold room and sat down on the edge of his bed where, staring at the rag the landlady called a carpet, he considered the pattern of the armies who had planned to die. A note to be scribbled. They always did that. A grin flickered on his puffy lips. A letter? To whom? Not to dead parents.

Not to the proprietor of all-night café, restaurant, saloon. 

Not to prostitute, pimp, college boy, waiter, fellow-musician (musician!), sporting business man. The grin vanished. The man went to the rickety bureau, took out the pistol he had bought in a pawnshop the day before, stretched himself out on the bed, grinned, said clearly, "Hello, God, here I come; get your piano tuned," and placed the revolver against his forehead. He pulled the trigger.

The noise was the explosion of a universe. Then silence. 

The gushing red flood blinded him, but through it, in the moment when he felt sleep or death, he saw, at long last, materialized beauty, an angel at the threshold of the third floor back. 

Six months later he married the angel.

Much had intervened before the humble nuptials: a farcical arrest for attempted suicide; an amazingly speedy recuperation; a period of comparative, really heroic sobriety; a continuation of his professional career in the same old night haunts; a furious battle with the angel's mother, who was his landlady.

On that bed of attempted suicide, augmented for marriage by two chairs, a plank, and arranged sheets and blankets, a child was born in nineteen-seventeen. The frail angel gathered it to her temporarily rich breast, and tried to ignore the husband who once more was arriving in the room at daybreak, drunk and hollow-eyed and plastering a cigarette between his lips.... "Waiter!" 

The waiter hovered. "Fresh coffee, please. And a pack of Camels.” 

Laurel's cool eyes were narrowed, intent, but unexcited. "Well, Miss Easton, the foolish lovely angel died. Angels can die, you see. Devils are harder to kill. This particular devil was flung into the street by the grandmother of the six-month-old child. And where the bullet had failed in its directed office, drink made a better, though slower, stab at it, for the insides of the man collapsed. D.T.s? Oh, they were only pain and horror. But liver, kidneys, tissues, placed a man in the charity ward of a hospital where a tired and overworked doctor said indifferently, ‘If you drink any more -I mean any more-you will die.' This, Miss Easton, was three years after the flight of the angel. The landlady had long since lost the house, had plunged into New York's maze, taking the child with her. Whether one moves one block in

the city, or to a distant borough, makes no difference. The lost poor are seldom found. In this case the man did not try. After all, he had never faithfully loved the angel, rarely seen his kid with clear eyes." Charles paused, poured coffee into Laurel's cup. "Let's see, where were we, in circumstance and chronology? Oh, yes. A doctor said, 'You will die.' It was nineteen-twenty. A world war had been fought and was being hilariously forgotten, and a man stumbled out of 

Bellevue Hospital and began to fight. Why he didn't want to die via drink I cannot tell you. Remembrance of pain, perhaps. Fear of worse pain. Possibly he remembered the angel's white arms, or moments of her cheated eyes. I doubt this, for the man was without worth beyond a seed. Seeds are peculiar. I know nothing of them. They aren't in my department at Neville Conservatory of Music, just outside the town of Princeton where I live." "The seed flourished, grew into you?" "I'm hardly a flower in the garden of music; I am, possibly, a vegetable in its kitchen-patch. No, Miss Easton, the seed did not flourish. It outgrew strangulation and crept toward the sun. Crept. I never took a drink after I left the hospital.

I used to smell it once in a while. I still do. I hate it so viciously-but later that can be told. Essentials, to-night, Miss Easton. You are kind to listen so long." "For the first time, I think, in four years, I've forgotten skating." "I owed everybody-landladies of gray, bug-infested, airless rooms, bartenders, waiters, violin players, drummers, the whole fugitive company who lived on the gay, soiled fringe of the city's raucous night life. Slowly, as I began to climb just a little, I began to pay them off, two dollars here, one dollar there; even fifty cents at a time. I got fairly steady

work-still in noisy dumps-playing the piano from eight at night until two. Then I dug up and held a salesman's job in a music publishing house. But I kept up the night work too. Besides this, I managed to study. Oh, it didn't come quickly, this incredible reformation. I gave it years. Pretty tough years, Miss Easton. Somewhere in one of these I began to teach theory to a few youngsters, in my hours before and after dinner." "You still played the piano at night?" "Yes. It's a wonder the work didn't kill me where bullet and drink failed by an edge. Five hours of sleep. I saved money. The frugal personal life of a monk, you must understand, and a small income that was increasing, however, a pittance at a time. I was terribly nervous, irritable. When some lazy pupil would fail to prepare the day's assignment and give the excuse, 'I didn't have time to get it done,' I would fly into a rage. No time to study! God! When I was bone-thin and half sick from my grind!" He paused to sip coffee. me;

Laurel gave a slight shrug. "Your tragic early years shock there is much to feel sorry for. But this work business, this climb out of a gutter-I'm no stranger to poverty and work. I work as hard to-day as you did then. But I use my hours with more sense. I sleep, for instance, as much, or almost as much, as I should." "I admire you for that work. Well, Miss Easton, I'll skip all the years from then until to-night. It was a steady struggle. Through a scholarship, two music schools, teaching positions. Then ten years ago I leaped to a berth at Neville. 

I've been in it ever since. "You are a professor?" 

"No. An instructor. The spring went out of me somewhere

on the way up. The spring you show, the spring I admire so deeply in you." "You don't look that way at all. You don't even look oldthat is, very old." "I'm fifty-five." 

His face, she saw, had grown deathly pale, the pasty color of the remnants of cheese on the plate between them. “Laurel—I—can't you see, guess— "I'm afraid I don't. Am I stupid?" "No. No. How could you guess?"

His voice had deepened with an effect she found pitiable, and hateful, too. Why all this emotion? What had it to do with a good dinner and a talk? 

Then, staring at him, she knew. 

They were silent. 

At last she whispered, "Your name is—” 

He nodded. "Charles Easton." 

Her lips straightened; her eyes were wary, on guard. "Laurel dear." "No!" she said sharply. "The Laurel, if you like. The dear, no." "You've been brought up to hate?” "No. I know that my grandmother hated you. Many years before she died, just as soon as I was old enough to know anything, I knew that. Her hatred was like acid. Perhaps for a while there was some of that in me, but it changed to indifference. She gave me no details. That's why, while you've talked, I didn't guess. She just dealt in—in adjectives." "A faithless husband, rotten father, yellow deserter?” 

Laurel nodded. "Well, your grandmother was accurate. Precisely. I didn't

miss a slimy trick in marriage except physical violence-and maybe, in stupors, I used that. But I don't think so.” "You are frank." 

He laughed grimly. "Have I any choice? The situation demands honesty." 

"The situation? Is there one?" Her lime-colored eyes, once more clear, untroubled, were fixed on his strained face. 

He stammered. "Yes. Well. There's-you and I are here.” "We are. Right here at a supper in Trenton, New Jersey. 

In a few minutes a bus carries us off in snow. At Princeton you get off, and I go on. Situation?” “Laurel! God knows I don't expect dramatics, sudden sentiment. Truly these are not in me and, I'm certain now, not in you. But this is a lonesome, lonesome world. A man finds, reaches out to a daughter-" "After separation of almost twenty-three years. That'swhy, that's a laugh, Mr. Easton." Laurel saw him wince. She saw, too, the unhappiness in his face, and the hard mass in her heart softened a little. Poor old man. She struggled fiercely against the softening. This barging into her life, threatening complications, muggy sentimentality. She spoke coldly. "What is your idea? Why have you come to me? Even when you ran across the trail, I should think you'd just have veered away from it again." "I want to know you, to be able to-to have something of you. To give you something of me. You are my flesh and blood, and-" 

Her quiet laugh whipped him. "Mr. Easton, I've fought a hard fight. How long my grandmother lived, when she died, made little difference. I've been on my own since I was a girl of fourteen. That's nine years. Let's see-" Laurel lifted her hands above the table,

held them open, folding finger after finger as she spoke. “In boy's pants I hung onto the back of a newspaper delivery truck, jumped off at houses, tossed the folded paper, and swung myself onto the truck again. A newspaper route. 

Hard, cold work in winter, Mr. Easton. Tough on a boy, horrible for a girl. Just as hard as playing a piano in one of your dives. Then I was a helper in a cleaning and pressing establishment. This one covered three years of hell. 

School seemed heaven. So, like you, I studied while I worked. I loved school. Especially English and drawing. 

To-day I design many of my skating costumes. I graduated.

I went to work in a dime store. The only job open. The five-and-ten-cent grind. That was tough too. Street skating, cheap dancing helped me keep on a level, and on the level, if you know what I mean. A girl must have fun. My fun was on my feet and with a controlled body." She dropped her hands to the tablecloth. "Not like the vivid tale you gave me, but, in many ways, not so different. I've fought-without a mother and a father. I've lived alone. I live alone now. No one cared how I looked, no one cares to-day, except my employer. I try on a new costume in my room. No one is there to say, “That's nice, Laurel, but don't you think the skirt could be just a bit fuller?' I'm not complaining, I'm enjoying my life, but I'm pointing out a few things, Mr. Easton-" "For God's sake, Laurel, can't you drop that 'Mr. Easton' once in a while?" "Father? Dad? Papa? Pop? Would one of these seem natural? I-I couldn't use it naturally. A bit forced, don't you agree?" 

His head went low. Laurel thought, "It's like beating a gentle dog, but it's the only way. I can't have my life upset.

I have a single road ahead of me, and I've come a long way

on it. A father? What could I do with a father, now? Years ago, yes. Some one to love, to run to, but now just a welldressed, good-looking stranger."

She said softly, “I think it is time to stand on the corner. 

Thanks for a generous dinner." Her voice rose. "Don't look so unhappy. All this has been impulsive. You know it has. 

And you'll get over it quickly. Your life is in Princeton and 

Neville. Mine is in the rinks. I wish you good luck." 

Snow whirled outside the whitened, faintly translucent windows of the bus. For a mile they sat in awkward silence. 

Then, "When is your birthday?” he asked. 

The word crashed into her distant, uncomfortable thoughts. She said dully, "Birthday." Then she understood. 

His next move, a gift. He must be forgetting Christmas. Her words came, clipped and sharp, "When a woman is long past middle-age she ignores-if lucky, forgets-birthdays." "I don't get you-middle-age?" "A skater's professional life isn't very long. I'm not working in the permanent shelter of a conservatory. In a few years, perhaps a dozen, some of these kids I'm teaching will say, 'Poor old Laurel. She's getting stiff. About time she realized it.' Yes, that's coming, and so, let's forget my middleage reminder." "What will you do when the kids say that?" "Oh, write my memoirs." "Seriously." 

She glanced around at him. “I think you are out of touch with nowadays young people, the nineteen-forty variety.

We don't plan definitely for a lifetime. We've learned not to. To-day, a few years of to-morrows, are all we can see, and even these aren't sure.' 

The bus slid around a curve, recovered its friction,

lurched on. Laurel guessed impatiently at the miles. Would 

Princeton never come?... Princeton came, announced by a weak golden blur through crystals. The bus stopped. "Good-night, Laurel.”

She drew away as, standing in the aisle, he put his hand gently on her brow. "Good-by, Mr. Easton.” 

The door in front opened, closed.

Laurel looked stonily at the floor. Not until the bus had gone its length would she raise her eyes, for she did not want to see what she knew would be just outside the window, a man standing in deep snow, staring at her, with snowflakes, or worse, blinding his eyes. Resolutely she muttered with still lips, “I held the crowd to-night. They liked me. And my backward arabesque makes a swell finale. I must have my wheels ground pretty soon."

She snuggled her neck down deep into the collar of her coat. Thirty-five miles to go. Her room. Then sleep. Dreamless she hoped, unless the flash of skates should sweep her on-and on-and on.... "Elizabeth, Miss!" 

She woke, grasped her skate box, and wavered a little on her way to the door.

THE STATE'S TREMENDOUS snow-plows were hurled into ponderous, blinded motion, and the highways were kept clear. The temperature dropped following the last of the falling snow; beyond the limits of towns and cities a white land emerged cloaked in frozen grandeur, but within the limits the side streets were frosted in black. Smoke. Soot. 

Dirt. Mrs. Green's house was dark save two oblongs of yellow light that marked Laurel's windows. At six o'clock the room was fairly warm. Laurel, pulling on her coat, shivered a little at thought of the return long after midnight when the room would be arctic. And the coat itself. 

The price, the date of its purchase, she knew, told the story. 

The coat was not to be blamed. Fifteen dollars, three years ago. Indeed, to its credit, was that it looked like twenty-five dollars, one, we-ell, two years ago. So much for appearance. 

The fabric, however, was but masquerade of wool. 

Laurel buttoned it, buckled the belt, and felt in advance the iced air that would finger her on the way to the bus. 

She ignored a series of weak, impatient hoots as she closed the street door and went down the steps, but the insistence finally caught her attention. Eddie's thin face was framed in the closed window of a rickety coupé held by the snow 45

three feet from the sidewalk. The door was swung open. 

Laurel slipped in, slammed it shut. "Eddie! How nice! Is the Rolls yours?" "Don't sneer. It's a borrowed car. And it will save you bus fare and standing on blocks of ice." "You're a dear."

The little car lurched forward. Laurel forgot the cold, the brave but inadequate coat. She thought warmly of 

Eddie, who had come far out of his way to do this for her. 

It really was sweet, and marked, certainly, the end of his recent period of obvious dislike. That was good to know. In this whirlpool of rink life the needless antagonisms, jealousies, resentments, quarrels, destroyed so harshly the pleasant interests and excitements. "Here is Eddie. As fine a skater as I. So many times my dancing partner. A flash on skates. Just as ambitious as I am. When we do our double spin in free style and he, bracing backward, gripping my wrist and ankle, whirls me with that first dizzying swoop when my breast is clearing the floor by a scant inch, I'm a happy girl that it is Eddie who is holding me. Round and round in a razzle-dazzle circle, my face turned up smiling and that scant inch of clearance! That's when confidence in your partner counts. His grip on your wrist and ankle must be steel and yet flexible. One slip? An ugly danger, besides the failure in the eyes of the public. Yes, Eddie is pretty real."

The car left the mean street, turned into an avenue, skidding continually. "Tough driving," Eddie grunted. “Gotta creep. We don't want no accident on the way to work." He laughed. “On the way back it's different." "You see that we do creep, and in both directions."

They talked little on the way to the rink. Eddie turned into the dark parking space for employees' cars, switched off the weak headlights. Laurel reached for the handle of the door, but his arm suddenly rounding her shoulder drew her tight against him and she felt his cold lips pass over her cheek in the darkness, find her mouth. She responded lightly to his kiss, tightened her arms around his neck and permitted herself to drift into the enjoyment of a warming, teasing sensuality. And in the very moments of this pleasant drift her mind worked clearly with question and answer. 

Rarely had Eddie and she got together in this sort of thing. 

But why hadn't she been drawn further? "I like it. So does

Eddie. Then he slips over the line I draw and I recoil, quit cold. And he doesn't get angry. He just cools off and accepts the denial. Obviously, then, he enjoys what we are having this minute, just as I do. There's this difference though, he hopes there'll come the instant when-oh, the deuce with cold thought! His lips are lovely, and his hand on my leg is a shiver of feeling and I'd love an hour of this; I'm almost sorry I'm a virgin." Laurel returned his long hard kiss, and they clung together in silence. Then thoughts came to her again. "Never has he said he loved me. Never. Even in these breathless moments. And certainly I don't love him. It's just that we are young, working together, thrown together all the time. I like him so very much. But that's not love. Evidently we agree on this." She sighed, moved out of his arms, and realized the reluctance with which he permitted her to go. "You're sweet, Eddie. Much more than you think." "Five minutes more, Laurel. Please." 

She kissed him, drifted back into warmth and pleasure, but at last sat up with finality. "Come on, Eddie, in we go.

I've got a class before the session. Seven girls, six men. It's really a rehearsal for my show in February.” “God damn this outfit! We're gypped all along the line. 

These free classes we learn. They had ought to pay." "Oh, go on! We are paid for giving private lessons. This free class arrangement helps the game along. You know that. 

And no instructor in ROLLING AMERICA is more willing to teach and give his time than you are-so stop squawking. 

Besides, I told you this is for the show." "I guess you're right, at that," he grumbled.

The vast arena was dimly lighted and in its emptiness Laurel began to teach her class. A Clarovox on the stage pounded out its music from records Laurel selected. At one moment she was skating with the row of precision skaters in this advanced class, at another she was standing alone in the center of the floor, at another she was on the stage changing music records. And all the time she was instructing, directing. The rink was cold. Not until seven, a half-hour before the night's opening, would the blower system of heating be turned on.

The couples were distributed at the corners of the rink for a special dance. Laurel remained on the stage giving directions through a microphone, before turning on the music. Then the dance began. Laurel smiled. "They really are coming on," she thought. "They'll be ready for the show." 

She spoke into the microphone. "The tango next. You corner couples will advance and retreat in narrow circles as I showed you the other night. Keep your eyes on the corner entrance where Elise and Bert will be waiting for their cue. 

Now-" The blare of the record swept the building. "No. 

No! Stop. Just a minute. Let me think. Start closer to the

rail." She saw Eddie skate onto the floor and on down to the couple farthest from her, where he began to talk with his quick, characteristic gestures. Then he withdrew to the rail.

Tango music. The dance was begun. A cold anger broke over 

Laurel. "Eddie again. Up to his old stuff. Correcting my pupils. Look at that couple!" She swung herself easily from the stage and skated the length of the floor while the music blared on and Elise and Bert danced the tango and the couples danced in their appointed, restricted places. 

She braked from swift approach to ask crisply what she already knew. "What's the idea? You are bending your knees, swinging entirely too much as you make your turns. Why have you suddenly changed your skating style?" "Eddie just told us. He said we've been doing it wrong." "As long as you are studying with me, and practising for shows I direct, I don't want you to follow any one else's instruction. That's definite. Now let's go on."

She watched them for a few moments, then skated back to the stage, sat on its edge and, without removing her skates, swung her legs up. She climbed to the Clarovox, turned off the music and spoke into the microphone. "You people are not to take direction from any one except me. I mean in regard to your practising for my show. If the national champions themselves should come in and tell you I'm having you do something that is wrong, don't follow what they say. Come to me for discussion, but don't change one single detail without my O.K. A show can't go right with conflicting direction. Now let's get on. Ready!”

The rehearsal went to conclusion without interruption. 

The dancers gathered in front of the organ stage. One of them whispered to her partner: "Say, Ken, doesn't Laurel

look like a young evangelist? In this half-light, for a homely girl she really seems lovely." The youth stared at her. "Homely! How do you get that way? Take another gander at her." The organ, winged on each side with tiers of glass and gilt, in the center of the great shell of scarlet and gold, framed Laurel visible from the waist up as she talked a little grimly to the group below. Her slenderness was accentuated by the lofty reaches of planes she centered, and the elaborate and garish color flowed down upon her pale face and dark hair and thin arms until she seemed painted back into girlhood. Ken thought, “Homely! Oh, yeah? Now if I can get her into a poem. Even her name. Laurel. Silver skates. 

Arabesque. She's a dream."

The dream clumped down the steps from the organ, slid to the edge of the stage and swung a lithe human body to the floor, saluted the dispersing group and skated to the door of the narrow passage separating the women's and the men's dressing-rooms. Eddie, changed into uniform, met her in the short passage and with a smile stood against the wall for her to pass.

The smile died as he saw her smoldering eyes. "Well?" she demanded. "Well what?” "You told that Number 3 couple to skate the way I'd told them not to. That's what." 

Eddie's mouth was set. "Then you told them wrong." "That's up to me. I'm asking you to keep off the kids in my show. Two directors are no go. You ought to know that."

He laughed. “It's hard to keep quiet when the rock-bottom rules of skating are forgotten." 

"Eddie, for the last time I tell you hands off my show.

I'll take care of my own headaches. As a matter of fact, that couple was right. But that's not the point. If wrong it was no business of yours. 

"I'm an instructor of this rink." "And I'm the head instructress," she stormed. “And for the show what I say goes. Do you get me?" "I get you, but damned if I'll keep quiet when I see lousy direction. Do you get me?"

Laurel whirled for the door to the rink and in a few seconds reached the manager's office next to the main entrance. The half-light had been turned on in the rink, the first arrivals were crowding past the door man, hurrying to the skate room. 

Laurel stood before the manager's desk. "Mr. Brennan," she said quietly, but her breathing was fast, "Eddie is interfering with my troupe. He's done it before. He refuses to stop. 

"Exactly what has he done?" The tone of the thin young manager was cool, noncommittal. "Corrected the style of a couple I'm teaching." "Sure he isn't right? We all make mistakes." 

Her heart pounded. Was Brennan going to let her down? 

Even if he had never really liked her? Oh, he couldn't in this. It was fundamental. Now cold anger steadied her. "If shades of difference in technique are to confuse willing and loyal amateurs in my show, I—” "Your show, Laurel? I think you harp on that too much. 

It isn't your show. It's the ELIZABETH ROLLER ARENA's and not even that. It's ROLLING AMERICA. We're all partners.” "But not all show directors."

Mr. Brennan's face was hard. “No, we aren't. We're rink managers, skate mechanics, lavatory attendants, publicity

men, everything. I'm manager of this rink, but I take suggestions from other managers in the chain.” "When they go over your head straight to your employees?" "Now, Laurel, cool off a bit. Eddie's a good man in this game. He's no fool. Talk things over with him. You'd better run along now and get into your uniform. Almost time for the first line-up." 

Laurel locked her lips on the flood behind them and skated out of the office. She spoke into the window of the skate mechanic. "Will you please grind my wheels before to-morrow's session? I'll leave them with you when I'm through to-night. They're in poor shape." "I won't have time." "Not before to-morrow afternoon?" "Nope." "But-but they need it.” "Can't help that. Over the week-end mebbe. I'm rushed to death. This damned place is a madhouse. Leave 'em 

Sat'dy night. Mebbe I kin crowd 'em in." "I-" Again Laurel locked her lips. This time against the declaration that she would report this to Mr. Brennan, for was she certain what that jelly-fish would do? It would be just like him to advise her to coax the skate mechanic. Oh, this was the last straw! Was she the hard-working head instructress, or a minor figure? Now even the mechanic was pushing her around. Oh, they all disliked her.

She hurried to her dressing-room and changed quickly into the royal blue and gold. Her reflected eyes appeared ovals of dark green glass. Her lip was trembling. “Thank 

God I've grown hard! Thank God my father deserted me so that I've had to be strong and hard! I'm not a girl crying in

a corner. And if I were? Better crying than whining. But 

I'm not doing either. Nor sulking. I'm raging. Damn this outfit! I'll beat it with my skates, with my ambition and because there isn't a lazy bone in my body." 

Her face calm, her eyes quiet, she skated out to stand in the formation introductory to the opening number. Bob, the floor manager, skated out to face the floor men and instructresses while Dorothy played the overture to the evening. Laurel stood, immobile, suggestive of frozen action, legs tight together, hands at thighs. The music concluded on a long-emphasized bar. The flood-lights were turned on in hard glare. The public streamed onto the floor. Laurel moved slowly around the inner oblong. "Laurel, please look at my spread-eagle. I'm doing it wrong." 

She watched Beatrice closely. Then, “But it's good, Bea. 

Don't worry about it." "Hi, Laurel." "Hello, Jeanie. Oh. Stand off, I want to look at your new dress. Why, it's beautiful! Your mother is a marvel! Now, come on, let's go to work. You and Beatrice go through your routine." 

An hour later, passing Eddie in All-Skate she received and returned a cold glance, and she was amused: only two hours since she had remained in the dark car with him, playing on warm verges, and now they were opponents, resentful, jealous of prerogative. Presently she saw him wing swerving passage through the circling crowd, and fling himself into a series of spectacular jumps in the inner oblong. 

He was, she admitted grudgingly, a falcon in flight, but when he stopped, stood still, and then went into a spin of terrific speed, his skates the diameter of a circle of mere

inches, she expected him to fall. That was her spin, not his.

He fell crashing to the floor just as she passed and a smile she thought hidden touched her lips. "What are you grinning at?" Beatrice demanded. "Am I? Oh, nothing. It's about time for you to learn to spin. Come on."

She went to the comparatively open oblong. Eddie was again a blurred form on a pivot. Then he was again a crashed length on the floor, picking himself up slowly, forcing the invariable, patterned smile of the rink fallen that said, “I'm not hurt," even when one was jarred from head to foot, bruised, scraped. While he stood for a moment, recovering coördination, Laurel told Beatrice to watch her. She stretched her arms wide, went up on one toe and flung her body into whirling blur. A spin executed perfectly on fundament of technique. Coming out of it abruptly to stand erect, she permitted a faint derision to touch her lips, to express the laugh behind her remote eyes that stole a glance at the darkened angry face of her professional dancing partner. "No use in rubbing it in," she thought and moved to the other end of the oblong. "Now, Bea. Stand straight. And make only three or four turns at first. Not too fast." 

Watching the young girl closely she nevertheless heard under the heavy tone of the organ the sharp failure of 

Eddie's next attempt at her own specialized spin.

Pencil and paper, Charles recognized wryly, were not needed for his private statement of assets and liabilities. A thousand-dollar-bond, a few hundred dollars in the bank, a salary of twenty-two hundred dollars a year, were his at

the end of fifty-five years of life. Debts were lacking, or trifling. And that was that. All heart-warming and assuring for the solitary man of no ambition and simple tastes, but disturbing for one who had found a daughter living her young years in a screaming merry-go-round. However deeply she cared for her work, however smartly she was getting on with it, the fact remained that it was precarious in high degree, physically of course, but of even greater import mentally. "She's surrounded with the 'them things,' the 'I done it,' the 'he don't count,' crowd. The Lord knows I'm not an educated snob, but decent speech is necessary for a young woman. Laurel uses it, she studied it in her school. But how long before the daily barrage of murdered English will affect her? Diction is important. She won't be on skates forever.

She admitted that when she spoke of middle-age already reached in her work. Work, profession, art. Well, I must grudgingly grant that her skating is all three. Plainly, even if 

I were rich I couldn't take her out of a rink. Its dust, its noise, its glare, are the breath of her life. And I'm poor.

Beyond this consideration is the straight truth that she'll have nothing to do with me. Her words to this effect might not be a complete barrier; they might hide latent yielding she does not suspect; but her shrinking away when I touched her forehead expressed more than she had said. 

Then it was her spirit that said, 'Keep off!' 

The landscape rushing past the train window was bare and forlorn now that last week's snow had melted to patchwork. The rhythm of the wheels was in the turning of his thoughts, on the occasional movement of his lips. Human emotions, he cried to himself, were utterly unpredictable. He had stood in snow watching the girl ride away in the bus less than a week ago, and in that bleak instant love was

born. It had not come to normal growth through the weeks of observing her, and the culminating dinner. Sympathy then, and anxiety for her, and a stirring of loneliness in himself. 

Weak stuff on the whole. But standing in the snow, peering in at her rigidly averted profile, he had felt the full tides of love in the shallow basin he had come so many years ago to call his heart.

Daughter. He had a daughter on a far horizon. And he wanted her. To hell with all this fear for her health, wealth and happiness! That was fear, true enough. He did want to do something, anything for her. But above all thought of her well-being, was this ache to have her emotionally open to him. His daughter. She must blend her love with his. Jesus!

His life was like that wet mud beyond the train window. 

Soggy, porous, cold. Thump-thump on a piano, talk-talk from a lecture platform, scratch-scratch with chalk on a blackboard. No human attachment. Not even in sexual relations. In earlier years prostitutes, in later years a few affairs with women who found him attractive, in years just past a subsidence of desire. There were just the soft comfort of his apartment, the dull necessity of his work. He was, in every real sense, alone.

The train rushed into the gloom of the tube. 

Charles' thought grew wild and, he felt, maudlin, but his thought could not be suppressed in the moment's vagary: he was in the tube beneath the Hudson River, and some few feet above his head was the keel of some great cruise ship bound for southern seas and the fragrant islands. Her white decks were lined with pleasure-seekers saying good-by to 

New York. Brisk walks round the decks in the cold air, and then the interior of warm saloons, cabins, lounges, all the appurtenances of luxurious liners. One day, one day not

! 

too far off, perhaps in the Easter holiday, he might be tramping round such a deck and a daughter's arm on his would be forgiveness....

The train emerged from the tube into winter dusk and the hard reality of the naked backs of gaunt houses where it slowed into the Pennsylvania Station. In the cold concourse above train-level, in the vast rotunda, the swirling crowds of Christmas shoppers, of regular commuters, emphasized with sudden sharpness Charles' sense that he had come to call, without regret, his "outside feeling." There simply had been nothing about it to regret. He was outside persons and things, marriage, parenthood, human attachment, music, Princeton and Neville social life, everything. The outsider who wasn't sorry to be outside. A simple enough man who did not need support beyond the passing material security of career. But now, in this entrance to a city, bound on an errand he was beginning to suspect might not be easily accomplished, there was in him a dark uneasiness. At this hour of winter dusk, in the rush of thousands, he was really alone. 

The great Christmas tree blazing with color, the bright stands of magazines and candy, the cheerful porters, were symbols and actuality of insideness. The placarded names: 

Washington, Florida, Pittsburgh, Montreal, Princeton, Philadelphia. Homes. Fathers were going to those homes where daughters waited with the little corners of their hearts open to fathers the world over. Insideness. Outsideness. In this city, he realized grimly, he did not know one person except in the relation of exchanged business. Music seller, booking agent, band player, and these were of more than a decade ago. All probably were gone now. The names that were really written on the city's sheet of music had always been

but names to him. "I was small fry." A few, four or five of his old crowd of twenty-thirty years before, had emerged, climbed to various successes. 

Riding up the escalator, Charles shrugged. What the hell was he grousing about? He knew no one in the city. So what? Why should he know any one and if he did, what would he be doing about it? Nothing. Bah! He wasn't here this murky day to moon over emptiness. He was here because of a card in his pocket. The leaflet that had been accompanied by the card, he had thrown away. Now Charles fished out the lavender paper with its purple print, and faint scent of lavender. 

Mlle. Camille La Tourette 

112% West 34th Street 

New York 

Humph! Perhaps. Perhaps. Well, at least he would find out quickly. Only five minutes' walk. Once he was at La Tourette's he could trust his eyes and fingers. Taxis, private cars, trucks, buses, blinded, but were prevented from killing him, by blue police and lines of rubies and emeralds. Hotels rose in sheets of yellow flame to the unseen sky. “And I would be unimpressed if the God-damned city were ten times this size, ten times as noisy, and ten times as rich or as poor. I'm not here to gape, to admire, to attend a concert, to study. I'm here because a youngster is"-he glanced back across Seventh Avenue to the gloomy station clock-“in all probability at this very instant teaching a class following the afternoon session. During the holidays from 

December 17th to January 4th, the damned rink holds afternoon sessions every day. Because that girl is there, and because La Tourette's office boy or girl must have got a

Princeton directory and presented me with a wife I'm now in this Christmas crowd."

He turned east on Thirty-fourth Street, found a narrow squalid entrance to a shabby building, and climbed one flight. Lavender letters on a glazed door invited unceremonious entry. Charles accepted. 

Margaret Peters approached unhurriedly. "I'm Mademoiselle Camille. What are you interested in?"

Grinning inwardly he wanted to say, "In your Mid-West accent, my sweet," but he resisted. "In a fur-trimmed winter coat, soft and warm and"-his voice underlined the ending-"as good as new." "All my stock is good as new," she said briefly. "I assumed that from the advertisement you sent me." "Sent to you?" "It was addressed to Mrs. Charles Easton, Princeton. There is no Mrs. Easton. I am Charles Easton." 

Margaret Peters laughed. “Don't be alarmed. My merchandise values are more dependable than my mailing lists." Her manner turned cool and brisk. “Now, then, let's see. You are interested in a fur-trimmed winter coat, soft and warm andas good as new." "That's right." "Preference in color, material, design, range of price?" "Good Lord!" "I see.” She chuckled. “I was trying to save you time and groping. One of many reasons my prices are so absurdly low is that I pay low rent, and hire very few people. My employees have gone home for the day. So my system is to ask what a customer approximately wishes and then if the wish can be met, to offer him the sometimes narrow, but almost invariably satisfactory choice. However, if you want

to go into the stockroom with me"-she shrugged smooth 

American shoulders-"but it will take time, you know. I won't know at all what to show. If you could give me an idea of the wearer I might at once find the right coat. The clearer the idea, the quicker it can be met." "It is a Christmas present for my daughter. She does not live with me. She's a young woman, slender, but strongly built. A professional skater. I—I—” Charles swallowed, then plunged on. "I noticed that her winter coat is poor. I suspect that it was that way when it was new. She must have something good." "Height?" "I must guess. Five feet five. A shade over, perhaps." "Did you read my advertisement?" "Hurriedly. For main facts. Second-hand clothes-" "There's the door!" Margaret Peters shrieked. "Never come back! Second-hand clothes!" 

Charles' voice was steady. "Second-hand clothes was what your leaflet described. Clothes that had been worn. I ask you patiently enough, what is the difference?" "If you had read with the slightest attention you wouldn't be asking such a silly question. But that's a professor for you, literal to the core. Yes. Literally my stock is second hand, but-" Her arm gestured wildly. "Now, look here, my mere man. This is toward the close of a busy day. I'm very tired. 

So, briefly, I'll tell you what I sell. First what I don't sellnew clothes or old clothes. Neither. I sell grand things that have been worn for a short time. They are sold to me by actresses, night-club entertainers and, you may be surprised to know, socialites of this fair city. In consideration of values 

I buy them for slightly less than nothing, and sell them for

slightly more than nothing. They have been cleaned, made perfect. If you go out of here with a coat it will be a coat that no one lacking a microscope could tell from new. So. Now 

I talk no more in generalities. I show you a few coats. I'm a smart college gal from the State of Nebraska earning a swell living in New York. That makes me a native New 

Yorker. Price range?" "I don't quite know.” "Lord in heaven, you don't seem to know anything. Furtrimmed coat. Why not fur coat?” "Now I do know something. The cost.” "How much do you expect to pay for a fine fur-trimmed coat?" "Look here, Miss La Tourette-" "Miss Peters to an old customer in the know." "-you've got me standing on my head. It's for you to name the price. I—” "God give me patience with the great brute. Listen, you big bully. The motto of this successful salon is, "The customer is usually wrong.' Go to Lord & Taylor's if you want to read, "The customer is always right.' Mere euphemismpretty good, that, for a high school alumna. Both mottoes boil down to the same thing. Now to business. The reason 

I'm trying to sell you a fur coat instead of a fur-trimmed coat is that I'm interested in a father's having to guess the size of his daughter, and I'd like to have this daughter swathe her litheness in rich fur. Lithe? All professional skaters are that." "I expect to pay, well, perhaps seventy-five dollars for the coat." "Is it to be a conventional seasonal gift? Just an easycome, easy-go affair. If so, you are right. I can sell you a fur-

trimmed coat, a very good one, for less than the price you mention."

She looked at him sharply and, at the change in his face, the hunger, the doubt exposed, her eyes were grave. "No, Miss Peters. It isn't as you say, easy-come, easy-go. 

Neither for my daughter, nor for myself. I—I'm not even sure she'll accept my gift."

She thought, "Ah. Reparation. Humph. It will be fur, you old softie. You nice old softie, I mean.” She said briskly, "You said five feet five. I'm five feet six. You said slender, but strong. I'm both. Take fifteen years off my age and I'm a young woman. Really young. Twenty-five. Now stand still, close your eyes and don't open them until I tell you to." "What? Do you do this to all your customers? I mean- 

I haven't 'stood still and closed my eyes' since I was a child.

Now when I'm fifty-five, in a store on Thirty-fourth Street-" "It's good for your spiritual arteries. To all my customers?

No. I don't waste humanity on most of them. But when some one real comes along-" Margaret Peters barked, "Close your eyes!" pro- 

And to his amazement Charles found himself doing just this, without revolt or self-ridicule or embarrassment. Was this New York, the Gargantuan city of indifference and sophistication? This friendly, overpowering woman the prietor of one of those gelid emporia of furs and silks and satins? Was he a cool aloof instructor on the faculty of a classical school of music? Standing here, eyes closed. God damn it, this was buffoonery! The hour was a grave one. For 

Laurel, for him. On this Christmas gift depended- "Open your eyes." 

Gray Persian lamb. Soft fur. Soft gray. High collar.

Warmth, luxury, style from throat to knees. And on Margaret

Peters' head a large crushed halo of gray fur matched the coat. 

The mannequin extraordinary pivoted slowly. "This, Mr.

Easton, needs no sales-talk. The hat is the accent mark of the coat. The whole is superb.' Charles, the urbane, the habitually cool and phlegmatic, to his discomfort faltered, "The-the price. I cannot afford—” "This Persian lamb creation, including the hat, was worn in the first scene only of a Broadway flop that broke the producer. First night! I was there. A detail of my business.

The leading lady had to enter the outer precincts of a night club, nod to the hat-check girl, and slap the face of a headwaiter beyond whose obese figure whirled a scene of dance and drivel. The inane plot then compelled the lady to sink into a divan, still in her lovely coat, and weep for three minutes that seemed to the audience an hour. A middle-aged man named Nathan left his seat on the aisle and in a few strides the theater. I saw him go, and remembered the coat.

Later that night, after the obsequies, it is possible that the leading lady slapped the producer's face, or maybe he slapped hers. What matter? The play lingered only a week in death throes, and presently the coat was in my hands." 

Margaret Peters slipped out of the fur. "My professional experience tells me infallibly that it is worth two hundred and twenty-five dollars. Probably the boy-friend producer paid three hundred for it or at least told the lady that he did—it came from a first-class shop. I paid her sixty-five dollars for it, and it will give many years of pleasure and pride to your young skating daughter for the exact sum of one hundred smackers. If she refuses the gift, she is too dangerous to be at large and I advise you to have her committed to the State Hospital for the Insane. Also if she refuses it, it will be

(

money in my pocket, for you will return the gray dream to me and I'll sell it for more money to the next customer, who won't have the luck to be the father of a kid skater, or to seem to me-in spite of your good clothes and generally well-groomed appearance-to be a wistful and extremely male animal. You want the coat?" “Terribly. And I mean that word with all its implications.” 

Margaret Peters did not smile.

Neither spoke and the madness of Thirty-fourth Street rattled window-panes that were light-painted with blue and rose and gold from a hundred street signs. Stained glass of cathedral. Chartres. The Princeton chapel, after Cambridge, England. The Rose window. The Blue window. The Gold window. Of great cathedral. Of salesroom in a shabby little building. Stained glass by the supreme artists of all time.

Foot Shoe Shoppe, by People's Cut-Rate Drug Store and by the Puppy-Suppy dog food store.

Margaret Peters sighed. "This whole transaction has been a little unusual. I'm hoping now that there is nothing left to be said or done beyond your paying the money and taking the coat and saying good-by, all a matter of three or four minutes. I'll accept a check, of course, if you haven't the cash. Your Princeton address establishes the check." "It isn't quite so simple. Of course it must be if you require it. But I—I wonder if you would help me-er-er-in the matter of-" "Oh, God! Here it comes! I feared it. I knew it. The minute I began with you. Nothing can possibly be simple about you. You to pay the money and take the coat and walk out of my exhausted life before that door opens for the next customer! You to leave so that I can snatch five minutes' rest

before the after-dinner shoppers straggle in! Oh, no, that would be too, too divine. So, now it comes. The purchase is just a detail in your planned campaign. The mere first step. Now I am to become your subordinate officer. God!" “I—” "Let's have it, and as bluntly as your academic world can shape its talk. By the way, what do you teach? I've forgotten your place on my list." "Music."

Her hands were wild in the air. "It would have to be that! 

Oh, yes, it would! A professor of chemistry, of physics, of mathematics, of civil engineering, of history, couldn't be my customer. It had to be music! Department of the Helpless. 'Kitten-on-the-Keys' stuff. Very well, if I'm doomed, so be it. 

Now, hurry, give it to me straight from the shoulder." "If you'll give me an opportunity to speak."

She laughed gently. "We've been standing for a half-hour. 

Sink onto that uncomfortable straight chair." She perched on a table, locked her hands around silken knees. "Where does your daughter live?” "I don't exactly know. That's the trouble." "That's a help. A just ducky beginning." "The town is Elizabeth, New Jersey. Laurel works-" "A lovely name!" 

His gray eyes brightened. “Yes, isn't it? Well, she is skating instructress, head instructress, of the ELIZABETH ROLLER 

ARENA. Of course the coat could be sent there, but somehow a-a Christmas present ought to go to whatever home she has it's one room, I believe-" 1 "But why do you bother me with all these details?" "You said-" "You are a nice man, obviously kind. Now run along and—

oh, yes, give me a check for the hundred dollars. The gift will be in her lonesome room on Christmas Eve when she comes in from the rink-the landlady will put it in Laurel's room-all wrapped up in silly crimson paper, silver ribbon, with a sprig of holly." "But I don't understand. My daughter's address is—” "Damn the man! Will you go away, far away or near away, just so it is beyond my door. I want to catch a nap." "How will you-” 

Margaret Peters hissed, “Imbecile! I skate at the rink once in a while, when I visit New Jersey in the autumn. Your 

Laurel has taught me elementary steps." "This is amazing! Almost incredible!" "Nonsense. It's not even marked coincidence. A million persons are roller-skating to-day in these United States. I'm one of the million. The ROLLING AMERICA chain covers quite a great deal of New Jersey. Haven't you learned yet that individuals, parties, skating clubs from a home rink visit the others? Well, then, I'm an autumn visitor in Laurel's rink. 

By the way, I like her. Her manner is too aloof for her own good, as far as popularity goes, but under that carapace she is only an extremely shy girl.” "Carapace?" 

She laughed. "And that's one for a business girl on Thirtyfourth Street versus a savant of music. Look it up in the dictionary, I've done quite enough for you already without teaching you English. Avaunt thee, churl, and quickly, the dawn of evening's business is about to gladden the souls of shopkeepers."

Dazedly he took a check-book from his pocket, wrote. 

Margaret Peters dropped the check on the table without looking at it.

"Good-by," she said, “and I mean it.” "I can never thank you enough." 

She glared. "Go!"

He paused at the door. "Carapace. The context supplies to my ignorance the meaning of your trick word. And I'd like to say to you that under your carapace of business shrewdness and brittle fencing, is a woman of extreme generosity and deep sympathy. Your kindness shortens the long, long odds against me in a difficult attempt to make up for-for shameful, rotten neglect. Good-by."

When his shadow had faded from the glazed door Margaret Peters, still seated on the edge of the table, swung her legs, tapped her teeth with a pencil. Laurel Easton's home address? Ah! She walked to her desk, looked up a telephone number, lifted the instrument. And presently, “ELIZABETH 

ROLLER ARENA? I'd like to speak to the manager. Camille La 

Tourette, New York, talking.” A long wait. Then, “How do you do? I'm Mademoiselle Camille La Tourette. I must apologize for what seems, perhaps, a triviality. I want to send flowers to Miss Laurel Easton, directly to her home, not to the rink. Would you please give me her address? ... Oh, thank you very much. 

Margaret turned slowly from the desk and stood quite still. 

Then she muttered, “And the hell of it is that I really shall send flowers. There was that in Easton's eyes that impels me to put a patch of fragrant color into a room-oh, damn it, I'll put on the card, 'From a woman you patiently helped around a slippery floor.' 

Card! She swore sulphurously. Card! The idiot had gone without leaving any word to go with the coat. But perhaps he intended writing to his daughter in care of the rink. "Whatever he does, it will be the longest way around. Any-

way, it's not my further anxiety. But, God damn it, I know 

I'll telephone him late to-night for instructions. And”—her voice was vicious-"I'll reverse the telephone charges. I simply won't be a sap for this Christmas spirit. Christmas to me means dollars and cents, profits.” Her ripe, heavily reddened lips twisted in a peculiar grin. “What a son-of-a-bitch that Charles Easton figures that he once was! Well, probably he was. Was."

TO THE seven rinks of ROLLING AMERICA the week before 

Christmas brought throngs of children released from school, all in holiday spirits. Matinées and nights. The number of floor men was increased. Rink parties were gay with prizes, costumes, special skating games. All was flourish, music, noisy happiness. 

Afternoon session of the day before Christmas.

Mr. Brennan, the manager, looked up from his desk as the office door was pushed open and the noise flooded in accompanying the floor manager, who skated to a chair, swinging the door to behind him. "Jim," he snapped abruptly, "ain't there a rule against visitors in the instructors' dressing-rooms?" "You know there is." "Yes. And I follow it. And I see that the others do, too.

Yesterday I made Laurel put a couple of girls out of hers. She squawked, but out they went. There's a mother and daughter in there right now, leaving their coats an' hats instead of usin' the checkrooms. I can't keep the other dressin'-rooms clear if Laurel breaks the rule." "Tell her I want to see her." Jim Brennan, young like all the rink managers of ROLLING AMERICA, thin, and unhealthy, drummed with his fingers on the desk, muttered, "This girl's 39

getting in my hair." He looked up as the door opened and closed on the wall of noise. “Laurel, there's a rink rule about the dressing-rooms. 

You're breaking it." "Not often, Mr. Brennan. There are times when mere courtesy-" "You'll have to let me run that." "I keep the kids out. As a matter of fact, the door is kept locked. There are three keys, June's, Alice's, mine." "How did those two, whoever they are, get in now?” "I let Mrs. Gale and her daughter in. They've come over from Trenton for the session. The daughter is a twirler. She was in the Trenton show; she's going to twirl in our February show here. It seemed friendly and polite to talk with them for a few minutes in my dressing-room while the daughter changes into skating costume. And, after all, they feel they are friends with me. Isn't that good for the rink?” "Possibly it is, but still the rule must be obeyed. Please get them out. The daughter can change in the ladies' room. 

I can't have exceptions, or we'll have loungers all over the dressing-rooms and in this office, too. The public owns the floor out there, the refreshment stand, and the rest-rooms, but there is a wall around the machinery. No gaps." 

Laurel asked evenly if that was all he wanted to say. "Not quite. I noticed yesterday that you were skating around the floor with an old lady during All-Skate. You weren't wearing your white gloves. That's part of the instructress' uniform, you know. And you know why. A firm grip when hands perspire." 

Laurel was angered. "Mr. Brennan, I'm head instructress in dancing and figure skating here and you know that I'm not supposed to tow beginners around any more. So I don't

wear the gloves. No head instructress in any rink does. Once in a while I'm asked to skate with a beginner. Yesterday it was an old lady, and of course I was glad to help her." Laurel stopped, shrugged, finding nothing more to say about this raked-up trivial incident. Just another weight added to the balance of resentment, intangible but unmistakable, that had been quietly forming against her for a long time. "Oh. Well, never mind the gloves. Unless you are called on to work the floor a lot. O. K. That's all."

She skated out unsmiling and, the manager saw, depressed. 

He frowned at the closed door. Laurel was a valuable skater, hard-working, tireless and willing. “But I don't like her. She's one of those quiet climbers that try to climb too fast. She needs to be slapped down a bit.”

He looked up as the door swung open. Then he rose quickly.

The general manager of ROLLING AMERICA, Henry Stevens, nodded, sat down beside the desk. "'Morning, Jim." "Good morning, Mr. Stevens.” Now what had brought the chief to Elizabeth? "This your publicity?" Mr. Stevens flipped the the last summer's show across the desk. "Why, yes. Most of it.” program of "Will the program for the February show follow this pattern, I mean in general treatment of the announcements?" "Yes, I think so." "The hell it will!" "What's wrong with it?" 

Mr. Stevens snorted, crossed short legs. "Before we go into that I want to talk to you about your head instructress here, Laurel. How do you rate her?"

"Oh, she's good enough." "Humph. I'm thinking of sending Hilda to you from New 

Brunswick, sending Laurel there. Any objections?" "None." Jim smiled inwardly. So Laurel would take her aggressiveness to New Brunswick! Good! She would be off his hands. "But there's a show coming up in February. She's directing it, as you know, and it would be better if she was kept here until it's over." "Why? She ain't the only one does that sort of thing, is she?" "No. Of course not. There's Eddie. But the show is in rehearsal and I don't know that Eddie could carry out exactly the plan." “‘ELIZABETH ARENA REVIEW, August 10, 1940. Presenting a spectacular skating... > "" The fat man paused. "I'll skip now seventeen numbers, an hour of exhibition, down to the final two lines: . . . No. 18Poem on Wheels. Laurel Easton. The review is directed by

There was a long uneasy silence. 

Then Mr. Stevens exploded. “Jesus Christ!" "I don't get what you are driving at, Mr. Stevens." "And you been in the rinks five years! Worked up from skate boy to rink manager, same as we all do." "Yes. Isn't my work satisfactory?"

The heavy hand slapped the program on the edge of the desk. "Not this part of it. This stinks. And your part of this interview stinks too. You gotta kid here whose general work in the rink is Class Double A. An' she's only been with ROLL- 

ING AMERICA Since last spring. Whose show was Class Double 

A with an amateur troupe? Who works her head off all her hired time and plenty besides?" His voice was a lash of bitter sarcasm. "And when I ask you if you have any objection to her being transferred to New Brunswick, you answer, 'None." 

What's the matter, Brennan? Won't she sleep with you?” "You can't talk to me like that. I'll quit!"

The heavy shoulders shrugged. “That's up to you. The floor manager will be glad to move up. Resigning?" 

Pale, furious, frightened, thinking of his wife, young Peter, even younger Joseph, Jim muttered, "No."

The general manager's tone changed to impersonal briskness. "Let's get busy. Take this down for the February pub-

licity and program announcement. You can put it into smoother lingo, but the guts of it is this. It goes at the top of the program: "The entire production is under the direction of Laurel Easton, Silver Medalist of ROLLING AMERICA, and head instructress of ELIZABETH ROLLER ARENA." 

Mr. Stevens paused while he reached into his pocket for a cigar. When it was well lighted he said, "And on the microphone when it comes time for Laurel's act, "The ELIZABETH ROLLER ARENA takes pride in presenting our own—' hit that ‘our own,' Jim, ‘—our own Laurel Easton.' Put her pitcher on a page by itself." He laughed softly. "Jim, Jim, what the hell's wrong with you? Don't you want the public to know this kid Laurel's got 'em all beat? She's a winner on skates, an' what's of more importance to us than anything, she's a fanatic. Get that! A fanatic. And fanatics are what we need. 

She ain't going to New Brunswick." 

Jim grinned wryly. “She wasn't, in the first place.”

Mr. Stevens said placidly, "Of course not. I just wanted to know how she stood here." 

The door opened. 

Laurel said, "Oh. How do you do, Mr. Stevens? I didn't know you were here. It's nothing important. I'll come back." 

The general manager grunted, asked harshly, "How's the show shapin' up?" "It always looks bad in the beginning. But I have hopes." 

Another grunt from Mr. Stevens. Laurel skated out. "Sterling, Jim. See what I mean? Even in little ways, an' the little ways means big ways. Trouble is with her she's no pusher. She can't sell herself. Keeps in the background. Look at that summer program-Christ, I'm gettin' mad all over again. Well, if she can't push herself, the management will have to do it. First thing we know, some bastard from an-

other chain'll take her away from us. Well, I guess I'll be toddlin' along. Slong." 

He went out. 

Jim lighted a distinctly bitter cigarette.

It was midnight when Laurel left the rink in mist and rain, protecting as well as she was able a pile of small packages she had already opened after the session. A Juliet cap of gold sequins and net from Beatrice, a silver compact with skaters in enamel relief from Jean, handkerchiefs, stockings from other pupils, and the gift of gifts from Eddie, a finely bound collection of articles on dance skating, by Perry Rawson, the great international authority on roller-skating. How nice that this flare-up with Eddie had subsided. He had not interfered again with her direction. She hoped he really liked the sweater. It would keep him warm in those hours between sessions when he waited in the rinks that so quickly chilled when the heat of the giant blowers was turned off. She hugged her gifts in bulges of her coat and sank onto a seat in the bus for the short ride to her room. 

Then depression struck again. Jim Brennan had been beastly, and that sneak Bob too. Manager and floor manager against her. That was serious. And why, why? She was working hard and well. That was definite. What else could count? 

Jealousy? No. Their territories were never really in conflict.

Brennan ran the rink; Bob ran the floor men and the floor in general; she taught. Naturally, they helped one another, depended on one another for support. And she was giving it, but not receiving it in return. To-night that fuss about her dressing-room. Of all the ridiculous affairs! And the gloves! 

Then the general manager, his greeting grunt, single ques-

tion, and dismissive grunt. Of course, he was always ungracious, even harsh, but to-night he had seemed to aim straight at her, to cap the hostility that had risen around her generally. Skate mechanic, Dorothy at the organ, floor manager, rink manager, general manager. No one liked her. Except the pupils, and, in his erratic way, Eddie. "It must be something in me. I don't reach out to people, I only pay real attention to skating. Eddie once told me that. Probably he's right. Anyway, I'm making too much of it. The rinks are all the same. All around the circuit. A bright surface. Polish, glittering for the public, and, underneath, a mess of bad feeling. The only ones to escape it are the meek and easygoing. No one is jealous of them. They do their work well enough, draw their pay and let it go at that. Maybe they are the wise ones. But how do they do it? I can't. Skating simply isn't their life. It's just a job. It's when one works up in the rinks that the air gets lurid. Oh, the devil with it! It's my skates that count!"

She hurried through the dark cold hall, unlocked the door of her room, and dropped the packages on a chair. B-r-r-r! 

There was never heat at this hour. Bed. Bed. Teeth in a hurry, face in a hurry. And bed. Sleep. Laurel yawned, shivered into her pale blue nightgown. Then, on a chair, she saw two wrapped boxes. Wondering, she opened the smaller one. 

A wave of fragrance. Nested in green paper and foil a sheaf of red roses. And scrawled on a florist's card a line of appreciation from some skater she had helped. No signature. What lovely flowers! Laurel turned to the other parcel, read the label, Mlle. Camille La Tourette.

The name meant nothing to her. She fumbled with the string, but her fingers were cold and a minute passed before, under the outer wrapping, she found a letter.

My daughter, Nassau Street, Princeton, New Jersey, Christmas 1940 

You drew away when my hand touched your forehead that night in the bus, and it was then, Laurel, I was paid in full for my desertion of my baby. I'll never forget the pain of that instant and the desolation that followed. Unbroken desolation. 

I'm lonely, Laurel, a failure in life, without zest. When I sat down to write this letter, it was to be, affectedly, you may know, in a casual, friendly vein, studiedly a little offhand, a further step in a plan to draw nearer to you a little at a time. The gift was not to be symbolic. It was to be just a useful thing to keep you warm. Yes, that was the sort of letter I sat down to write. But, Laurel dear, I can not write it. You are my own. For years-my fault, of course. Once and for all I admit that everything has been that—I have had no feeling in life; teaching, eating, sleeping, wearing rather decent clothes, reading cheap detective fiction, have been all. Then I stumbled against you, rubbed disbelief from my eyes, accepted you as true. If you wonder when this acceptance turned to love, I can not tell you. Perhaps it was when I saw you in your beauty of skating, perhaps it was while you told me of your girlhood in poverty and mean work, perhaps it was during the hours I've sat in my rooms thinking, but most probable of all it came to me when I laid my hand on your forehead and you shrank from it. Yes. Yes. That was the fulness of it. Then I wanted you, Laurel. Wanted? A word that covers a maze of conditions, most of which, almost all of which, you will, I know, refuse. To live with me? Impossible. I'm not referring to material, factual barriers. I mean emotional. So that is out of the want. To give much of your time to me? Impossible. 

Of your thought, of your affection? Impossible. I know you have none for me. How could you have? To give regularly spaced welcome to me, such as a dinner hour once a week? Impossible.

Because it could so easily become an intrusive burden to you. So then, what is there in my want that you might give? A simple thing, Laurel, so very, very simple. Just this: give me a smile, a

word once in a while if you feel inclined, when I sit behind the rail, or meet you at the refreshment stand. Perhaps sometimes, entirely unscheduled, we'll steal a dinner hour between your sessions. That's the bulk of what I want, what I hope for. And in return for this? I'll promise not to come closer to you than you wish. In any way, whatsoever. Neither by word, nor act. My thought I can not control. I'll be an anonymous occasional presence in your rink. 

Oh, there is one thing more I want. That, at Christmas, on a birthday, on special occasion of some sort, you'll let your father give you a present. So, Laurel, that's the part of you I want. Darling girl, I want it so, so terribly and fiercely and unhappily. 

What words for my colorless self: terribly, fiercely, unhappily. 

Why, such conceptions haven't touched my barren life for years.

Yet here they are now, brought by you. And-and queerly there is pride too; for that slender stray girl on her skates, leaning into flight on breathless turn, is my daughter. She is the color, the strength, the fineness, that were never in me. She is my daughter. 

Good night, Laurel, and a happy Christmas. 

The letter dropped from her cold fingers. DAD.

She sat down on the bed, picked up the heavy linen paper and read again, this time more carefully. And a third time.

Then she tried to forget herself, to examine Charles Easton's position. She strove for, achieved a cold analysis. He was alone in life. He had found her by accident, and, a comparatively short while afterward, felt love for her. This love she would admit, incomprehensible to her as it was, for his actions, and now definitely his letter, proved it. He wanted to come into her life. “That dinner once in a while, gifts, chats at the rink, will not satisfy him for long. It's not natural." 

He would come closer and closer. And for what good to either? He would not gain a daughter, a response in love.

He would be all the lonelier in realizing that she was a stranger, at most a cool companion quite indifferent toward him. This he would face in growing bitterness. "Generally, I feel sorry for old lonely people. But not a bit for him more than the others. Why should I? I don't sit moping in this drab room over my own loneliness. I'm too much in love with my work for that. Skating is my profession, my life. Everything else can go hang. And if he has no happiness in late middle-age I can't give it to him. He's not an outcast shivering in the streets. He"-she smiled crookedly-"dwells in marble halls. He is in comfort and security." 

The smile died as she turned to thought of herself. Troubles were breaking over her. Hard work and more hard work, unending hard work, ahead. There was no one to help her. 

Depending wholly on her skates and stamina she must go on and find whatever answers she could. Alone. This room. It wasn't a home. It was a shelter. On Christmas Eve it was the same as on any other night. Why hang holly on a lamp, ormost absurd-why hang up her stocking? Such things were out of her life. Charles Easton would want to bring them to her, would get, probably, happiness in doing so, but why should she disrupt her difficult and demanding career, clutter it with another's sentiment?

Laurel's young eyes were hard when she accepted, finally, her decision. She looked at the large oblong box covered now with thin crimson paper only. Then she stood up and folded carefully into the original creases the heavy outside paper that had lain like white wings on the quilt, tied the string, drew lines through her name, and wrote in heavy ink strokes: 

Charles Easton

Nassau Street 

Princeton, N. J.

Answer to the letter? Why write one? Rejection of the gift was final answer. She could say nothing in a letter, except wish of good luck, and that in itself was nothing. 

His letter, torn across and up and down, fluttered into the waste-basket. 

She thought, "Oh, I'm right. Now this proves it: I came from the rink dead for sleep. Needing the rest. For to-morrow. Then what happened? Charles Easton touched my life, and sleepiness fled. I'm wide awake, bothered, restless, at close to one. And this just from a letter and a gift I did not look at. The letter"-she paused, sighed-"was pathetic and decent, but it was written to a girl who does not exist.” 

She went to her bureau and took out a large scrapbook and a folio. Then she pulled on a quilted robe and, after putting the roses in a jug of water, got into bed, with pillows piled at her back, and against her upthrust knees a loose page from a children's magazine. Dolls, figures to be cut out, and pasted into the scrapbook, by tens and scores, with notes below each. Now with her hair falling almost to her shoulder, in the wine-colored robe, she seemed a girl in the early teens, playing with paper dolls, unashamed of this clinging to childhood, yet careful to be alone in a bedroom behind a locked door when she stole this fun from the years that had gone. Snip-snip of scissors, a dab of mucilage, a printing of letters below the figure on the page. And months later her cool eyes would examine these pages and when she found what she wanted she would sketch a child's frock, change it a little, and take the drawing to an unpretentious little shop where, in the rear room, a woman would show samples of silk, satin, corduroy, velvet, and presently go to work; in a few days there would be a return for a fitting, and then on some exhibition night under the circus blaze of light,

Laurel Easton would appear in a new costume of lovely line and color and frail enchantment.... Paper dolls rich in suggestion. Paper dolls and a young woman's work. 

The mean street outside the yellow windows was empty under sheets of rain that mocked and hissed this Christmas Eve.

TWO MILES WEST of Princeton, in the side yard of an unprosperous farm, a large battered trailer had been resting for three months. Its towing coupé with tarpaulin-covered motor was sheltered in a tumble-down shed across the yard. Following the damp, misty Christmas, oncoming nineteen-fortyone had glazed the countryside in ice, but on this late afternoon in early January a steady windless snowfall was changing hard grandeur to soft loveliness. The povertystricken farm was, but not at the snow-filled moment, within sight of the lofty spears of Princeton's ambitious and successful architecture. From the trailer's east windows one saw beyond low hills the gray towers and bastions of the university.

The afternoon was cold. The yard was a bleak clearing in surrounding miles of scrub woods and scattered farms. 

The sound of a pump handle and the thick cough of the sluggish water were muffled in the silent flakes. The woman at the pump smiled into the kitchen at the farmer's wife, and turned toward the trailer, balancing her slow movement against the brimful wooden bucket. Fifty yards through snow and cold. And Helen Slade thought, "In a moment I'll be in the warmth of our trailer and I won't leave it until to-morrow morning. A fine big supper if Harold brings meat from 82

Princeton, a long evening with a magazine and a dive into bed. Grand, eh, young lady? Wel-l, almost young lady, forty isn't clearly young or old. But I like it."

The door opened and yellow lamplight displayed Helen without prejudice, after she had dropped hood and coat on a chair. The body Harold called voluptuous enticement, the arms and legs he called ardent assents, the face he called a flower, the hair he called honey in the sun, lived up to some extent, strangely, to his euphuistic flow. Strangely because, after one look at Helen, the starer-and every one staredalways labeled her a slattern: her white neck at the rim of her sweater wasn't shadowed, it was a little dirty; her pretty face, except heavily painted sensual lips, was pale; her dull blond hair was a tangle; under the soiled sweater large breasts were bold without brassière; her stomach, free of restraint in tight fabric and in diet, was comfortably noticeable; the clear line of thigh under the nondescript skirt was firm and-Harold again-irresistible.

Her movement now as she dropped into an easy-chair beside the lamp, reaching for cigarette and magazine, was lazy, pleasurable. Helen was home. A few moments later she was standing, unseen, at the side of the heroine in a cheap serial while two men shot out their rivalry with bullet-spitting blue-barreled revolvers. Helen did not identify herself with the heroine; that would have required imaginative agility, effort of some sort. So she merely watched, enjoyed. 

She reached out to a table drawer for a piece of candy. The paper bag was empty. "That pig Harold. Not to leave one caramel for his wife." She smiled, returned to a dead man, a lover, and a really young lady. 

The silent snow curtained the darkening panes. 

Helen's face lost much of its faded blond attraction when

she lolled asleep in the chair, her mouth opening and closing with gentle snores, but her body, even in uncomfortable slumber, retained its opulent, fleshy gracefulness. She opened wide blue eyes when a bellow of laughter filled the trailer, and then, seeing a grave man standing beside her husband, she gazed at the stranger calmly without edge of interest.

She sat up lazily, but did not give a single smoothing touch to her rumpled sweater and skirt slightly pulled up in the sleep that had been shattered. "Helen, this is Mr. Charles Easton. Easton, my wife." 

She smiled amiably. "Take off your things," Harold Slade said loudly to 

Charles. "We'll have coffee and things to eat. All in God's good time." 

Helen gave a lazy laugh. "I can't help it. You two. You're both covered with snow, but otherwise you look like a lost keeper and his trained bear. Harold, you are a monster in that mackinaw and hood." "And I still am,” he grunted as the snow-covered wool was dropped, unshaken, at his feet. “Two hundred and forty-nine, darling, on the butcher's scales; and my height, alas, remains five feet five. I am a monster." "You are my pet," Helen said, moving to a low wide bed with bright red day cover where she sat with legs half under her, bent at the knees. The men took the chairs. "Easton and I ran into each other in the snow." "Literally," Charles said. "Heads down. I bounced." "You didn't. You were absorbed. I absorb everything that touches me." He turned to the bed. "Darling, there's a great steak in that bundle. Perhaps you'll prepare it. Easton will honor us at dinner bountiful."

Stretching a little, the woman walked to the stove. Harold

became methodical with briar pipe and tobacco. Charles observed. Unwashed dishes were piled in the sink, pots and pans obviously dirty were on the table beside the stove, a torn lamp-shade of yellow silk tilted slightly on the table lamp in the center of the trailer's one room. The bed was mussed, the rugs ragged and creased. Sly notice was unnecessary. It was all there before one's eyes for a glance or two to list. That closet door, Charles guessed, belonged to a toilet.

At the end of the room, opposite that of the stove, was a small cleared space with large easel, brushes in tin cylinders on the floor. There were no curtains at the windows, which, except the wide one behind the easel, were small. 

Charles gave a start at the hoarse voice of his host. "Ah, Easton, you behold a sloven's home. But presently a noble steak will transform this chamber to a banquet hall out of 

Ivanhoe. And French fried potatoes the thanes never knew, and a National Biscuit Company plum pudding. If you eat as heartily as I hope you will, as heartily as Helen and I shall, then the two miles to the seat of learning will be out of the question for your drowsiness, and you'll sleep here on a contrived floor-bed of pillows, coats, blankets and what have you. You shall slumber with your conscience for bedfellow, and I with yon languorous slattern who calls me monsterand husband." “Oh, be quiet,” Helen giggled. “How do you like your steak, Mr. Easton?" "Any way, thanks. I never care. Medium, perhaps. Whatever you and Mr. Slade have when you are alone. The tramp in the snow, the bounce, the hour, have sharpened an appetite generally not large." He turned to the mass of flesh that the chair was propping. "Thanks again for having me. This seems a pleasant setting for your days.”

"Days, months, years, my friend. Florida, Maine and the

States between. A simple setting for a simple life. I eat like a hog, make love to my wife like a bull, and paint like an angel. Beyond these I do nothing, know nothing. Presently you'll see me eat, after dinner you'll see a bit of my painting, and, if you stop the night on the floor, and stay awake for a little while, you'll hear the love of a monster. Won't he, Helen darling?" "Might be," the woman said vaguely, giving closer attention to a pan of potatoes crisping in hot grease. "You teach music at the university?" Harold asked. 

Charles shook his head. "No. Out at Neville Conservatory.

Half a mile from Princeton. But I live in the town. I'm an instructor in music." "Humph. I shouldn't like that. One or two such offers in painting have come my way. I turned 'em down. Of course 

I'm not saying that many good men don't leap for teaching berths. I'm only speaking for myself. I hate ropes. All kinds, the ones you have to know and the ones that tie you to some place.'
