Charles grinned. "You're middle-aged and this is freshmanic talk. Ropes! Good Lord, man, no man is free of them. 

This pleasant spot of warmth and light. You are roped to it in a hundred ways. Many of them inconvenient too. Economic ropes, social, artistic-" “Oh, damn the ropes! I know you are right. Still, I don't want to teach." "That's understandable," Charles said quietly. "Where do you do your painting?" "At the end of this room, in sitters' homes, in fields, on mountains, in valleys, city streets, sea beaches. Everywhere. 

I sell almost everything I paint because, one, I'm a good

painter, two, I sell cheap as hell. The combination works out fine." "Exhibitions?” "In a small gallery here and there. Murals, too, in a Texas post-office. But I don't look for much of that.” The four chins dropped an inch in an open-mouthed leer. "I go stale on any job that runs into a year. I'm always hankering to move on with Helen in the caravan. Oh, there's a gallery in New

York where I drop in once in a while to talk business. I can sit there like Buddha and hear the boss whisper to patron or idler, "That's Slade!' And in New Mexico. In two or three little restaurants too, hangouts for painters, writers. It's my shape that slays 'em. Even a low mountain of flabbiest fat is impressive. After all, it is a mountain." "Dinner's ready,” Helen said.

Never, Charles marveled inwardly, had such good plain food, been so poorly served. The meat was offered directly from the stove, the plates were almost tossed helter-skelter on the table, the perfectly cooked potatoes were placed on wrapping paper for self-service. The painter tucked a large soiled napkin into the neckband of his shirt, Helen used none, and to Charles was given a flimsy square of paper. 

Charles ate heartily-Helen chewed slowly with strong appetite-Harold wolfed everything within reach, washing it down with mugs of coffee. He ate, talked, laughed, belched.

Charles recognized in himself the first lightened spirits since Laurel had returned the Christmas gift such a long fortnight ago.... He would never forget his sorrow when he found no letter accompanying the box. Not a word. Only the harsh rejection. And fear following closely the sorrow, for his emotions so long quiescent in academic seclusion had

come to dark life as nightly he had fought down the impulsion to go to the rink. How unwise his presence there, at this time! And wisdom had won, but at what cost in unhappiness and longing!... Charles shook his head, brought again amused attention to this dinner in the trailer. A one-word description struck him. Flesh! Cooked flesh of beast, flesh of contented, lethargic and sensual woman, flesh of Slade, and, unmistakable in this snug room on wheels, the flesh of greedy carnal delight. Charles glanced at the chewing woman. For all the neglect of face and hair she was languidly pretty, and her body on display in the dirty, tight sweater-Charles grinned over his plum pudding-was unquestionably a damned alluring vessel for the love of her stuffed and quivering idol.

The idol was now consuming all that was left, to the last crumb, of the plum pudding which had been carved straight from the tin. He gulped the dregs of his coffee. He picked his teeth. He lighted the black tobacco in his pipe. Then, without a single preliminary yawn, his chins sagged, and he was sound asleep.

Helen smiled vaguely at Charles, then removed her gaze to space.

And that tableau, Charles recalled with sharp amusement as, very late, he plodded back to Princeton, had lasted exactly a half-hour by the cheap alarm-clock ticking away on the shelf over the stove. Harold lost in sleep, Helen lost in her secret vacuum, a pipe burning out on the floor. "I could have washed the dishes. I should have. They'd never have noticed. I know exactly what those two are doing now. The table, uncleared, is pushed aside, the bed is groaning under its burden of earthly sleepiness and far from ethe-

real love. Hah, what a pair to be nesting so close to the gray monastic cloisters of Princeton! The fellow's painting seemed good to me. Not that I really know. There's a grim baldness under all of it that surprises me. It doesn't fit the man. Even that unfinished portrait of the banker's niece held something that tempered her youth and sweetness. Well, they asked me to come again. But I'm not sure. Probably I shall. When the snow has gone. The trailer is, in a mild way, an objective in a good walk. And the Slades are persons one can say hail and farewell to without formality." He grinned up at the unseen sky. "No ropes between them and me, at least."

Princeton, a blur of gold, emerged from obscurity, the melancholy tolling of the bell on Nassau Hall broke into the silence of the drifting flakes.

Finale. The entire skating company of the revue. Gorgeous uniforms. Dragoons. Ballet dancers. Clowns. Twirlers. Skat ing slowly down the center of the rink in long column, and at the head Laurel Easton, a slim figure in white satin bodice and white ballet skirt. Giant color wheels spilled low pools of color through which Laurel led her troupe. Applause. The final deep chords from the organ. Laurel's February show, a success, was over, the six weeks of intensified rehearsals since 

Christmas rewarded. The white flood-lights blazed down. All- 

Skate was flashed, routine resumed.

Laurel, in her dressing-room open to-night for the girls, was supervising the rented costumes, counting caps, gowns, paraphernalia of all sorts, urging the chattering girls to hurry in their dressing, accepting what excited kisses she couldn't escape, telling them all how well they had done. When the

last of the girls had gone she went to work on the packing. 

She felt suddenly tired, recognizing clearly the let-down following great effort and success. "Laurel. Can I come in?" "Yes, Mr. Brennan." "Well, you pulled it off again. A good show." "Thanks very much." "Only one thing wrong with it." 

Laurel winced. "You don't have to tell what it was. My own free style act." "Right, Laurel, and you don't have to tell me why it was off." 

Laurel nodded, straightening from the cavernous mustiness of a packing-case. "I put too much into the kids, neglecting my own practice. But you know, Mr. Brennan, you do that when you are directing amateurs. They need so much." “I don't think it was lack of practice as much as your just being tired. You skated technically O.K. But you were dead on your skates. No glamour, no fire. That's just being tired.

Well, anyway, the show was a knock-out." He skated to the door, stopped. "Take to-morrow off. And don't go skating anywhere else. 

You need rest." The door banged behind him. 

Laurel smiled, continued to count, sort, pack. What a night it had been! Recognition of her, on printed program, on the microphone. Credit for direction, production, everything. "Our own Laurel Easton!" Gosh! Tears had come into her eyes twice to-night. A brass band hired by a delegation from another rink in the ROLLING AMERICA chain had lined up to march down the rink at the opening of the show. And before the first roll of drums, Brennan hissing into her ear as she was

adjusting Beatrice's costume, “Skate out there, Laurel, and lead 'em. This is your show. We want the people to see our director." Dazed at this from the unfriendly manager, she had stared at him and then moved out to stand motionless in front of the giant drum-major. A moment of silence. Then behind her the thundering roll of drums, and, seconds later, the crash of brass! She had stroked slowly forward to lead the resounding band down the center of the rink. It was then, smiling, that the first tears had blurred. Happy tears. Later when she executed her own introduction, a cartwheel and slow split, and then whirled out, gaining speed for jump and long arabesque, she knew she was failing badly, and at the conclusion of her number the faint applause established the public recognition of this failure. The blur, this time, of unhappiness, to be winked back furiously.

Now, however, closing a lid on the last of the costumes, excitement and delight prevailed. Another Laurel Easton success was recorded. Round the chain it would go. There was no one else to take the responsibility of these shows, and to direct, to produce them down to the last detail. Next year, head instructress of all the rinks in ROLLING AMERICA! 

Possible. At least she was nearer the far goal to-night. Selfdiscipline, unending work-but to-morrow was a gift from 

Jim Brennan. 

She changed quickly into her blue plaid skating costume, glad that on the night of shows she was off routine duty and did not have to wear her uniform, and skated out.

Couples was on the electric signal on the organ stage. 

Henry Stevens touched her shoulder, his face wreathed in the smoke of a heavy, oily cigar. "Good show, kid. Very good. You're tired. I told Brennan to give you the day off to-morrow."

She looked at him gravely. "You noticed my own skating, Mr. Stevens?" "You're on my payroll, ain't you? You were dead on your skates. Not your fault. Rest. G'night." 

A young man said, “Will you skate?" Laurel moved with him into rich pools of color.

At one of the checkroom windows near the entrance of the rink Margery Brennan was saying quietly, “But you don't have to pay me for this. Heavens! It's no trouble. You're in a rush to leave, a friend has asked you to give this parcel to Laurel, she's busy somewhere at the moment, I'll see that she gets it. There you are! And you're quite welcome."

But Charles Easton thanked her hurriedly, left the window, and Margery found a dollar bill on the counter. She remembered for a long time the man's sad eyes.

Following the custom on nights when public skating was interrupted by shows and long exhibitions the closing of the session was delayed until twelve-thirty. Before Margery caught glimpse of Laurel she was busy with coats and hats.

The last All-Skate was ending. The floor cleared quickly. The 

National Anthem was played, with Laurel at the head of the floor crew standing at salute. Now there were hundreds before the checkroom windows. . . . The rink was empty, and half dark, Eddie whirled out in a series of jumps and rushes. 

Pete tried a few spins. Laurel practised a dance step. "Oh, Laurel!"

She came, stroking slowly, and even in that slow stroking a marvel of grace, Margery thought. There was something about the girl's slightest movements-"natural rhythm, I guess," Margery decided; "skaters are born." "Want me, Marge?" "A mysterious stranger, very nice-looking too, left this

big heavy parcel for you. Said he was in a hurry, an' that some friend had asked him to see that you got it." “Thanks a lot. I recognize the shape of it. A Christmas present mix-up. Good-night."

She skated to her dressing-room. It was empty. June and 

Alice had hurried away. Once again she untied the string, removed the outer layer of paper. This time there was but a thin sheet of notepaper, folded once, and on it a few lines of handwriting she remembered from Christmas Eve. 

Please, Laurel. You know that there are no strings attached to this. Until tonight I have not been in the rink since Christmas 

Eve. I will not come unless ask me. you 

I don't expect you to ask me. But I want you to wear this coat. There are still weeks of vicious weather ahead of you.

CHARLES EASTON 

Laurel looked up at her old coat, so carefully hung on the wire hanger. It was presentable enough, but without a sweater under it the cold slipped through to her skin. Living expenses, costumes, new skates-twenty-five dollars for these —a dental bill; well, a new coat this winter had been out of the question. And here was one in this wrapped box! She stared at the coat on the wall, frowning, arguing with herself against even opening the parcel. Then she read the letter again. There was sadness, she knew, in that signature. The formal name. No "Dad" this time. His statement of no strings; the promised absence from the rink. Rather decent. 

With sudden heat Laurel canceled the "rather." It was wholly decent. Generous. And yet, yet-if an uncle, a cousin, even some friend of her mother's, had suddenly touched her life, it would have seemed merely surprising, acceptable; but this man was her own father. Father! That-that was a

deep, a terrific fact. To look into the face of a stranger and find a father! That wasn't just sitting down to a light breakfast on any morning. Laurel took off her skates. But the short note. Its sorrow. And depth. He had admitted every rotten fact of his desertion. "He- I don't know what to do. I don't want to hurt him, but I don't want him in life. He promises to stay away. 

The room was cooling quickly. my

Laurel sat on the bench, one shoe on, the other ignored on the floor. To be hard. One had to be. This rink life was no dream on a college girl's campus. It was struggle. "In my case it has torn away any thought of conventional pleasure, settled home, marriage. I've kept on my armor and-" A 

Christmas gift clearly given from a man's heart was a good thing, a good, good thing, but life was not full of simple goodness. Life was complicated. "And I have no room or time for complication. I'm Laurel Easton on her way up. It's the way I've selected, the only way I want."

Now the dressing-room was cold. 

She thrust her foot into the shoe, stood up, put her skates into her locker. The midnight wind was blasting the windows. Laurel saw herself standing presently on the street corner waiting for the bus. She could feel the cold creeping over her with robust strength that not even the professional satisfaction of this night could disarm. Stars in the sky perhaps, in cold glitter, or a murky sky of smoke and cloud.

But cold, cold. Her eyes touched again on the coat that would give her its very poor best, the lining of its armpits torn, its collar a travesty as far as snuggling into it went. 

She ripped the paper from the box, opened it, pushed aside the tissue filling, and stared without real comprehension at the folded coat. Persian lamb! A soft gray coat, the kind

labeled in the rotogravure sections a “creation for Milady.” 

Lifted from the box, the coat was slipped on. Instantly it shaped itself to her figure. She felt the collar high around her neck, beneath her chin. The fur reached to an inch or two below her knees. In the mirror were reflected the square shoulders and the perfect fall of the sleeves. And young eyes, their natural lime pigment darkened with excitement. Laurel made one last struggle. "A rich coat. A-a possession. And you're acting like a lunatic." But the struggle was brief, empty of conviction.

Then she saw that something had dropped from the lifted coat onto the floor at the other side of the bench. An unbroken bending and straightening movement, and the large fur hat resembling crown or halo was placed on her head, high, backward a little from the first wave of her dusky hair. 

For the first time in her life she was literally draped in fur. “He wrote of vicious cold, and the need of a coat. He said nothing of luxury." Stroking the fur, coming down to earth again, Laurel wondered at the exact size and form. A music teacher! Oh, it just didn't add up at all. Unless he was the world's master guesser at measurements of the female figure. 

Not an alteration required. Magic perfection!

Laurel laughed, took her old coat from the wall, gave it a grateful pat and hung it in her locker. Then she left the dressing-room. The rink was dark save for the pilot light and a feeble bulb here and there, but the main entrance lobby was bright and into this brightness she walked. "Laurel!" "It's nice, isn't it, Marge?" "Nice? It's heavenly. But how, who, why?" "A Christmas present from a relative, delayed on a roundabout delivery."

"Jim!" Margery called. 

Mr. Brennan came from the ticket booth. "Look at Laurel! Have you ever seen such a coat?" "Not around the rinks," the manager said a little grimly. "Come into a fortune, Laurel?”

She answered against a growing confusion. "A relation who writes that he has enjoyed my skating. Don't ask me anything else. I'm still dazed. Good-night."

Laurel stood on the corner waiting for the bus. The wind was iced, blasting. She spread her arms wide, stretched them over her head, challenging the cold to touch her. A laugh was torn from her lips into the black night. "I feel," she said to Winter, “like a polar bear on an Arctic floe, perfectly at home, warm and snug, and wondering what cold means, beyond exhilaration. I wish I had my skates on. 

Three students from Neville went thumping down the stairs and the noise to Charles was a release, if but a cold one, to solitude. Outside his sitting-room window the February afternoon was fading. Soon the streetlights would be turned on; across Nassau Street the dormitories and administration buildings would glow with hundreds of yellow windows. Snug college pocket in life. Like the Neville pocket on the outskirts of the town. But, Charles admitted, it was a deep pocket, inexhaustible in the richness it could bestow on every man in exact proportion to the individual's wish, search, ability. On that historic Princeton campus were the fine buildings, old and new, in which students and loafers spent four formation years profitable or negligible or wasteful. Happy years, Charles knew. There Nassau Hall with its tolling bell stood rocklike, as it stood when the Continental

Congress had met within its walls, when English and Americans had fought it out for its possession, when it had sheltered the wounded of Washington's tattered army. What an excellent chapter in an excellent book had to say about that! 

Azoy's They Were Not Afraid to Die. Charles smiled wryly.

It was by accident only that that kind of book ever interrupted his steady fare of mystery stories. 

He stood at the window. Not more than a couple of hundred yards distant was the university library flanking Nassau Hall, crammed with the best of the world's writing, ancient, modern, and ultramodern. Novels, poetry, biography, science, the whole in ink and paper, and seldom did he enter the old building that the books had long outgrown. Hell! 

What of it? He did his work satisfactorily at the conservatory, drew his pay, read his murder mysteries and let the world sway past. He liked the English, held them in warm respect, loathed the Germans, coolly trusted Franklin Roosevelt, and thought, "I'm simply negative in public affairs. 

Reprehensible, no doubt, but there I am." 

As the lights of university and town came on in irregular streams, he drew down the shades of his windows, turned on his lamps, and stretched out on the high wide window-seat to which one had to vault from the floor or climb from a low stool. Cushions and mattress made it, Charles thought, the nicest place in the otherwise commonplace suite. On spring and summer evenings one could lie there four feet above the floor with face at tall, open window, watching the passersby, listening idly to the talk of the loungers on the sidewalk just one floor below; but this was midwinter, and the window-seat was warm and comfortable behind closed shades. Thank

God the students had clattered away, seven o'clock in a restaurant was still hours distant, a nap seemed indicated by

place and clock. Drifting off, he thought with dry amusement of the Slades in their trailer. Two miles. Probably one day he would walk out there again to say hello to the robust monstrosity and his blond and contented slave.

A rush of feet swept past the door and on up the old wooden stairs. Hah! An astronomy instructor dwelt among paper stars just below the roof, and now for an hour he would engage the minds of anxious youths unsteady in their college courses, upon planets and suns steady in their celestial courses.

Drifting again, almost at the very portal of sleep, he stirred restlessly awake, fighting the question that had tormented since last night. Would she keep the coat? Was it around her now as she was-he glanced at the clock on the mantel-perhaps leaving the rink? A slim chance. He groaned. What a hopeless mess in which to be involved! 

Young Lester was better off upstairs with his confused minds and confusable galaxies, because these could be forgotten at the dinner hour, and in night of relaxation at club or movie. "I can't forget my daughter. She's got me. At cruel distance."

His peace of mind three months ago. That uneventful cycle of the faculty year. Simplicity. Eat, teach, save a little money, sleep. Let the years roll by, watched in calm contentment. No avid desires, no severe anxieties, no delights, no sorrows. Nirvana on the outer edge of local life. Then- 

Laurel. And the demolition of Nirvana. Charles glanced restlessly at the clock on the mantel. More than an hour's wait until seven and the restaurant dinner. He felt so sleepy and could not sleep. He wanted to forget Laurel, but knew that love could not be forgotten. With no progress toward her the days were a leering waste. The window-shade, at his impatient jerk, flew up and Nassau Street became for half an

hour a weak anodyne. Buses were rumbling past like huge unnatural kegs of light, cars were backing and edging into parking spaces, students were on their endless parade of this careless hour before dinner, the innumerable dogs of Princeton were fulfilling arrogantly their various appointments in this amiable town. And Laurel? She might still be in the rink, possibly lying on a bench wrapped in a blanket, or munching sandwiches, or ordering an atrocious supper in that cheap restaurant frequented, for its close convenience, by the rink personnel. "What a fool I am to pity her at this hour. Or at any hour. 

She is lucky; doing what she wants to do and knowing that she does it brilliantly. And yet, well, she sees it with young, unafraid eyes. I see it with aging eyes. Poor food, inadequate winter clothes, cheap lodging. I know well the taste, the shiver, the drabness of these. I remember with horror and fear. I don't want my daughter to come close to the cold darkness I knew. Of course, with me, degradation, slavery, ruled. With her, ambition and decency rule, but still she eats poor food, pulls a shoddy coat tight around her, goes to bed in a dull room of a lodging-house. How long can she hold out and up? Youth is strong, but abuse makes inroads. I could help in little ways that would in effect be big ways, a bit of pocket money, a wedge of companionship-" He broke off to laugh. "Wedge is just the word. Wedges are hard and the young living tree must hate them." A sigh changed to a snarl. "Arrh. Nuts to the world! I'll read that new Who-done-it novel." 

For an hour the pages lisped over.

Charles took the book with him, and between courses in the little French restaurant on Witherspoon Street propped it on salt and pepper shakers. He returned vacantly a few

nods from men he recognized as university instructors. He looked at his food and at the printed pages of the mystery novel. His dinner finished, he lingered over coffee and amused remembrance of Harold Slade, repeating the vague resolve to return one day to the trailer. He would take a raw steak with him, or a haunch of beef, or a whole ox. What an eater that fellow was! A glutton in middle age and probably he would live longer than the calory counter. At least he would enjoy more of life.

A cat rubbed arched back against Charles' leg. He bent down to pat it and, straightening, found directly in his gaze a coat draped over the back of an empty chair that faced a table screened from his vision by a jutting divisional wall in the dining-room. He stared, shook his head, and then gave a mirthless laugh. Persian lamb. And why, he raged, should his heart miss beats for that? Women all over the world hung Persian coats on the backs of chairs. Hundreds of thousands. But his body was a traitor to common sense and he was rising, on his way to slay this imbecilic hope. 

Damned nonsense. That was what reading the Who-done-it school did to one's mind. Yet, in the seconds taken for the few steps across the room he realized with renewed despair the abyss of his longing. Common sense, judgment, had nothing to do with this. There was one chance in a thousand that 

Laurel might be behind that partition.

Then he stopped short, for he saw his daughter mirrored in clear glass on the opposite wall. She was sipping coffee, her lips bright red on the white rim of the tiny cup. The faintly applied color in her cheeks lent, in the lamplight on the table, a sensitive charm to her pallor. Charles could not see her eyes, long-lashed, lowered to the cup, but as she suddenly looked around, apparently for the waitress, he

drank deep of their refreshing composure. "That lovely child," he breathed, “is mine.” For an instant his eyes closed. 

Then, face whitened, he stepped backward in fear that she might see him in the mirror. He must, it was certain, regain calm. Tears and joy were not on the calendar of this reunion. 

Not actual tears and joy, of course, but not even the telltale face that would express them. Laurel would, against whatever resolution had brought her to him, retreat, and this time for ever. 

He must not even whisper to the waitress and pay Laurel's check, much as he desired. It might confuse. He must leave the restaurant and go to his rooms and wait. There was no gamble in this. Laurel was in Princeton, wearing his Christmas gift! She had come to see him! Of this there could be no question. None. And the fact of her dining alone indicated clearly that she preferred the meeting to take place in privacy, for otherwise she would have come to him before dinner.

Hurriedly he paid his check, left the restaurant. The proprietor at his desk stared, shrugged. This steady customer 

Easton. Invariably well-dressed, quiet, courteous. And there he went stumbling out with not even the customary pause and polite good night. Ah, well, something had disturbed his cloistered serenity. What matter? To-morrow he would be back for breakfast, or if not, for luncheon or dinner.

Charles pushed the low stool to the panel above which the large high window-seat must welcome Laurel, and ran the shade up. There, with the night street scene for occasional view, she would feel freer, more relaxed from the demands of this meeting that might, even if things went nicely, take place between lines of stiffness. The momentous dinner at Trenton had been carried along on swift tides of nervousness, ex-

citement, revelation, suspicion, hostility; time had flown, but now there would be quiet without distraction of food, of revelatory personal history. A father would be reaching out to a daughter across a dark abyss of ignorance and neglect; the daughter would be, if with embarrassment and regret, on guard against close approach. “Laurel and her pathetic little citadel of independence which even if I believed I could storm, I would not storm. She must open the gate to me when she realizes that I demand nothing, ask for practically nothing. This nothing? It is everything to me. It is my girl whose eyes will see me clearly, who, if sometime I put a hand on her head, will not shrink, who will enjoy little gifts from the edge of my surplus in material possession." 

Charles sat on the arm of a chair, lighted a cigarette, crushed it out barely tasted. His nervous, yet solemn, happiness went under a cloud as it suddenly occurred to him that Laurel might be going to Trenton to skate in exhibition, and that she could spare him but a fragment of the night. The cloud passed. What of it? The gold base was solid-she had come to him, for minutes or hours. 

The bell sounded. 

Charles rose and, with wild heart, swung open the door. "Hello, Easton." "Oh! Hello, Slade."

The monument moved in, dropping hooded mackinaw to the floor. "We've been looking for you out in the arctic spaces. No dice. So, I've looked you up. Free to-night?" "No. As a matter of fact, I'm expecting some one. I thought-" "Sorry I barged in," Harold boomed. "Just let me catch my breath in this chair. One moment, and out I go."

"I'm so sorry. It is some one special. Do come again, tomorrow night if you can.' "Sure, sure." 

The bell rang. 

Charles jerked the knob of the door. "Laurel!"

With effort Charles found a surface of habitual urbanity and poise, taking Laurel's coat when she slipped it off, her hat, placing them on the black bench beside the door. Then he said, "Laurel, this is Mr. Slade-Slade, this is Miss-"

The surface cracked, then smashed completely. He was dismayed. Was Laurel prepared yet for introduction as his daughter? What was her plan? Complete secrecy? 

But Harold without volition saved him, for he was puffing, grunting, getting to his feet, bowing, hoarsely expressing his pleasure. And Laurel was grave and seemed quite undisturbed.

So they were standing near the door, and Harold was making brief, loud excuse, pulling on his mackinaw. Charles, suddenly irritated, saw him grin with godlike comprehension of this arrival of “some one." And he knew that Laurel, too, had noticed, for she was saying quietly, "This is no casual hour for Mr. Easton and me, Mr. Slade. I've been away from my father for a long time. I'm really sorry to have interrupted-" "Laurel," Harold grunted, hand on doorknob, "you fit your name in a painter's eye and imagination. I hope on an ordinary night to see you and your father again. Make him take you out to my wheeled palace. Good night." The door banged. The stairs outside shook. "That was nice of you," Charles said, "and wise beyond your years. Climb on the window-seat. Perhaps it is the only

thing my rooms can boast." With gold delight he saw her step on the stool, put her knee on the high ledge, swing up, and sit back against the cushioned wall. "What was that tornado?" "My second meeting with it. Its material source is a trailer two miles from here. The monstrous upheaval of nature paints."

The fencing ceased abruptly. 

Their eyes met, held for a moment, and separated. "I-I saw you in the restaurant," Charles stammered. "And I saw you. It is better here, Mr. Easton. We both knew that. I've come to thank you for my coat and to say I'm sorry I was so-so hard about it on Christmas Eve. It wasn't impulse that night; I thought soberly, and then refused the gift. And it isn't impulse that brings me to-night. I thought this over very thoroughly too. I need the rich warm coat and 

I've come to thank you for luxury I've never dreamed." "Need I say how welcome you are, how glad I am?"

She turned her face to the window, and Charles examined with fresh delight the delicate profile. Picture in his bachelor window. Laurel's gracefulness, vigor, the stroking power of her legs, all packed into the figure of this doubting girl on the high wide ledge. He was conscious of tenderness, deep, endless, as he watched her, a clear sensation with no blurring by cheap sentimentality. Two beings were meeting under peculiar circumstances, the young woman swayed by simple appreciation of a gift and by courtesy, the middle-aged man by the oldest emotion in all the world. Staring now at the averted face of his daughter, Charles realized that all selfish consideration had left him: if Laurel were to walk out the door stating that she would never see him again, were she to live up to that statement, but permit him to give her once

in a while something for her comfort, health, pleasure, he would feel, stronger than the sadness of complete separation, a vast measure of joy. For the first time in his adult life, he knew, he could give happily without receiving.

The bell on Nassau Hall tolled. To Laurel's question he replied with brief narration of the bell of long tradition. 

An hour passed with quiet questions and answers. She told him of the strain the floor men endured if the sessions were too many at certain seasons, and of their absolute need of brief vacations; of the family atmosphere in ROLLING 

AMERICA rinks, where the wives of managers, of instructors, of first aid men, were given jobs in checkrooms and offices; of one of the reasons for her own advancement, the quality of eagerness-even if frightened-to accept any responsibility. "Plenty of girls skate well, but timidity, lack of initiative, keep them from getting chances to climb."

She told him much of Eddie; he was an artist with the certain something distinguishing him from most of the other instructors, that edge in his work and attitude the others lacked; but he was wild and unrestrained, not always dependable, and so, quite often, in trouble with the management. "I'm not like that, Mr. Easton. I discipline myself, stay out of trouble, and hold my angers in my heart until they go, or their causes have been corrected." When she was speaking of Eddie, even while her face was in shadow, Charles noticed her gaze go drifting, and he wondered at the pang that touched him.

Answering a question Laurel admitted bad features in the rink life. They were, she said, more serious than mere annoyance. The rolling dust continuously breathed, resin, plasterof-Paris, were an unending aggravation of coughs and colds;

it could not be escaped. No records of its results were kept, but certainly it was bad for lungs. Then, too, there was the noise. A grinding roar. Sometimes it was compared with ocean surf, but that was false. The roll of surf held melody, the roll of wooden wheels on wooden floor was harsh, deafening. It tended to spoil the voice, for one must shout in order to be heard; the ear, for the noise was shattering; even the organ must use electric amplifiers in its struggle to be heard. And meals? Hot-dogs or hamburgers for suppers. Between meals nibbling, munching, gobbling, washing down with bottled soda of synthetic flavors. "Then, jealousies, underhand methods, petty plots. Above all, Mr. Brennan dislikes me violently. Oh, well," Laurel concluded gravely, “I'm hardened to all this. I skate right over it." She looked at him obliquely through long dark lashes on eyelids slightly heavy. "You would hate my environment." 

"It is for youth,” Charles said. “Lie down, Laurel. You seem tired." "I've had until now"-she smiled at him-"a rotten day.

Mr. Brennan gave me a holiday for rest and reward for last night. I-" "It was a splendid show.” "Thanks. I sort of spruced up my costumes this morning; then I ran into New York and tried out for the ROLLER FOLLIES OF 1941. You haven't heard of that. It's new. A professional roller-skating review, like the ICE FOLLIES. Beautiful. It opens in March in Detroit, then moves on to Chicago and a score of Mid-Western cities, winding up in New York at the Garden. I tried out to-day, was told to come back for the first rehearsal next week. I can have a solo spot on the program. Well, there it is, months of employment at good pay, exciting work in a picked company of the best, and

having to skate all the time to the very limit of my ability. 

And lovely costumes that I won't have to provide and repair, all expenses paid, and a real gain in prestige. Laurel Easton in the ROLLER FOLLIES. You see, it goes through the skating magazines and rink newspapers. It stands for quite a lot.” "And you're in?” "As far as the FOLLIES company is concerned. But the manager of my rink says No. I don't want to go over his head to 

Stevens, the general manager, who might have sense enough to see that the prestige would help ROLLING AMERICA. Jim 

Brennan would be furious.” "Why won't Brennan let you go?" "The dislike I mentioned and, more important, interruption to my skating classes and routine. There's lots to be said for his viewpoint. Since last spring I've been getting the youngsters into the swing of things, encouraging, as I told you in Trenton, among skaters who dressed rather poorly, the increase of skating costumes, discouraging jitterbug tendencies. Oh, there are many things a rink's head instructress does personally that give the tone and general attractiveness to a rink. She is a kind of reigning hostess. That's a bit flowery, but it's true. The arena in Elizabeth isn't the gentlest place in America. Like all the rinks in our chain, it is controlled, run in orderly fashion, but much of the polish is applied by me. So Jim Brennan figures that if I go on tour for several months, he loses the ground I've gained for him." 

She paused. 

Charles spurred her on deliberately. "You couldn't be replaced?" "Yes. But not at once; professional skaters are not picked up just anywhere, any time. But still, that's just the point. 

In time I could be replaced. And I can not risk that. My

living, my ambition, depend upon employment by ROLLING AMERICA. If I jump the barrier and join the FOLLIES, and the show is a success, then I'd have a fair gamble on several things, teaching jobs in rival rinks, next season's FOLLIES, even movie skating, but, all this hangs on the success of the show. It can flop before it even opens. I know nothing of the backers. And I can't quite take the risk now. It's a lovely dream, but I'm giving it up. Brennan says No. If I defy him, he'd queer me with the whole chain. Of course, I could get a job, as I said, with other rinks, but I'd lose so much that 

I've gained." "I see." 

Laurel sighed. "I'm so unhappy about it. To-day I tried out in a hall-what a place! Tiny, and warped boards in the floor, and gymnasium apparatus scattered around. But I skated well, gave everything I have, even a flash of speed once in a while. That was dangerous, so the director slowed me down a bit. But he liked my work." 

Charles stared. This youngster, brave, decent, on fire. On fire. What matter the cause of the blaze? For violin, for scholarship, for social work, for science, for-for skating. There was no distinction to be made. The blaze was what counted. 

The blaze he had never had, would never have— "What's the matter? You jumped in your chair." 

Charles smiled. "Nothing." He flung himself mentally at the thought that had made him twitch violently. The blaze he would never have? Never? Rot! Here it was right in his formerly cold rooms. Love for this fighting youngster whose voice was so defensively skeptical, whose profile against the dark window seemed the carving of a dream. Blaze. That was blaze to warm him into life. "Go on," he muttered.

"Eddie is taking the gamble. He would, you know. That's his whole way in life. And I lose the best skating partner in the rinks." "Will your Mr. Brennan fire him?” "Brennan won't know he's rehearsing for the FOLLIES, until it comes out in gossip. Eddie will have a dozen trumped-up excuses for being late to rink sessions when they once in a while collide with his rehearsals. Elizabeth is about half an hour by train from New York. Eddie will shuttle back and forth, balancing, juggling everything." "And you? You couldn't do that?” "I'm not very good at telling lies," she said simply. "I'm glad of that, Laurel.” "It's not a moral question with me. It's exactly what I said. 

I'm not clever at lying." "Still, I'm glad. 

Her shoulders rose and fell. "I'm not. At least in this

ROLLER FOLLIES business. I'm sick with disappointment. 

Let's drop it." She turned around on the window-seat to face him. "Now," she demanded, “you tell me about this marvelous coat. At once and with every last detail."

Charles' face was flushed, happy, as he described Camille 

La Tourette's extraordinary personality. When he had finished, Laurel said, "She must have sent the roses to my room. I know no one who would. It would be just like the woman you describe. Have you told her I refused the coat?" "No." "You haven't written to her at all?” "No."

Charles grinned inwardly at the sudden severity in his daughter's wide-set green eyes, on the straightened line of her lips.

"After all the trouble she took!" "I'll write to her to-morrow. The better news. She'll be so glad. What an alarming place she runs-that is, of course, for a man. Some spring day, Laurel, you must let me take her." He smiled in delight. “Perhaps a leading lady of another closed play will have disposed of a spring outfit." you to

Charles saw her face harden, and he recognized at once the danger signal. He must wait outside that citadel. Patience. The maid in armor would call, in time, to him. Patience. "Come, Laurel," he said briskly. "Let me show you around my collegiate lair. Then you may go back to your high ledge."

Monastic bedroom, kitchenette, the living-room again. The tour was made in less than two minutes, and Charles was ironically amused at her lack of even the faintest interest in the few possessions he had collected in his years of respectability. His large work table in the living-room alone caught her attention, evoking brief question and evident approval of its order. "You don't seem impressed with my few favorite pieces of furniture, with my silver tea things, dime-store glass and"his arm swept around-"all the somewhat meager rest." "Owning things? I don't envy you that. I want good skates, costumes for work, and a rented room. These I have." "Spartan maid. At that, you are right, for yourself. For others-not for me-material luxury far beyond the line of mere comfort." 

She smiled. “The fur coat is my exception. Oh, you were generous and thoughtful. In a few minutes from now when 

I leave, how fine the cold night will feel on my face! No more

shivers down my back, chills at my waist, all over. That poor old coat!" "Hanging now in your dark closet, resting after years of honest service?" "I've given it to a girl who needed it badly." "Already?" "This morning. And ten minutes after, I knew I'd been a fool. Sleety, stormy days. It would have saved wear on this-" 

Charles' ferocity amazed himself as he snapped, "Stop that, Laurel! La Tourette's merchandise is not for appearance only. It is to be worn, and the worse the weather the more continuously it is to be worn against snow, sleet, cold rain, winter's whole bag of tricks. When it needs cleaning, repair, there are establishments for these. So don't speak of-" "I won't," she said meekly.

She picked up the coat, slipped into it. "It's time to go. 

Good night. I'm glad I came." Then, as if realizing her abruptness, she plunged into nervous words of vague conventionality, coming at last to a halting end. 

Charles looked her in the face, spoke steadily. "Laurel, there's one bit of awkwardness that is bothering you, but believe me, it has no place between us. It's such a silly thing, quite needlessly oppressive, little unless you allow it to be big. It's this-you don't know what to call me. The Mr. 

Easton seems as false to me, as does Father to you, or-or 

Dad I signed in the Christmas Eve letter. Some day, perhaps, when you thoroughly believe, know, I'm not rushing into your privacy, you may like to call me Dad, but I know you can not do it now. Charles? Somehow that doesn't ring true.

Well, nothing seems to fit. So just skip the whole thing. I'll answer amiably to 'Hey, you.' Right?" 

Her pale cheeks flushed. "Right." "Now, won't you have a stale sandwich and good coffee before you go? Or go with me to the Balt down the street for a very fine sandwich and acceptable coffee? Come on."

She shook her head, now steeply crescented in the gray fur hat. “Bus time. My holiday is over. No, don't come with me."

Charles overruled her, insisting on seeing her to the street corner, where they stood waiting in light gusts of snow that powdered her dark hair waving up to the fur, that caught too on her long lashes, making her blink like a child.

The great bus came to a wavering stop. "Good night, Laurel. You are always welcome in Princeton." "Good night.” Suddenly from the step she looked levelly into his eyes. "And you will be welcome in the rink, any time."

ON WASHINGTON'S BIRTHDAY, by train, bus, car, plane, came into Princeton the annual friendly invasion of alumni, families, friends, and faculty visitors from other universities, all interested socially, professionally, artistically, in the elaborate program offered by the university. Concert, art exhibition, faculty and alumni receptions, forums, theatricals, hockey, basketball, wrestling gave interest and pleasure to all tastes. The duck-board paths squeaked and whined under the pressure of extraordinary traffic. Holiday and open house. 

In the center of the campus, "Prospect," the home of the president, was pleasantly garmented in snow. The fine, simple house in its few acres of fenced reserve held a perfect blending of activity and dignified composure as, at eleven in the morning, the president welcomed alumni and faculty and outside visitors in a general and most informal reception. There were no speeches. Friends, colleagues, talked; visitors to the university were made welcome. The large rooms on the ground floor were comfortably filled. 

Physically quite at home, spiritually restless, Charles made the polite rounds, chatting with visiting, former pupils from the music school, smiling at the impressive Dr. Saunders who never missed such occasions, establishing, he told himself with sly amusement, his dutiful presence in the assem- 113

blage. Dutiful? Well, Charles reflected, Dr. Saunders looked for some show of politic interest. The pleasant relations between Neville and Princeton were maintained by just such gatherings, events, as this one. There seemed almost a family bond. So many privileges of the university were extended to the small, but traditionally rich institution, that social responsibility seemed the least that could be offered in return. 

Urbane, amiable, Charles moved on, wondering impatiently just how soon he might slip away unnoticed. He glanced at his wrist. Any time now. "Hello, Mr. Easton."

He looked steadily at the young man before recognition came. "Ah, Horton. Back to the fold, eh? Glad to see you. 

And how are you getting along in Tin Pan Alley?” 

The young man chuckled. "If the Juilliard could hear that! 

I'm plugging away. Stole to-day from the piano."

Charles' faint smile seemed a puzzled shrug, as if to say, "For this?" 

Now Horton stared. "For this afternoon." "This afternoon?” "Four o'clock, at the Graduate School. Bach sonatas, piano with strings." "Oh. Yes. Yes. I'd forgotten. Of course, wonderful." 

Charles sighed. “I have a strong will. I forgot, because I'd willed to forget. You see, I knew some time ago that I should have to leave town this afternoon, and, because I hated to contemplate missing the quintet, I determined to forget it was to be played." "Sounds impossible to me." "That's good. It ought to be impossible for you. Well, I'm glad we meet again. If you're in Princeton to-morrow, drop over to my rooms, and tell me all about the concert."

"Thanks. I'd like that, but I'm leaving to-night." "Keep up the plugging, Horton; I always liked your work."

They touched hands, smiled, separated, and Charles, moving toward a thinly curtained window, mused idly on Horton's musical ambition, playing with the thought of the many things that could happen to it. So many. So many. "Hey, Easton, let's get the hell out of here!" 

Startled by the hissed whisper Charles looked around, finding no one who could possibly have made the remark. 

Then the window curtain was pulled aside and Charles stood grinning down at Harold Slade. 

The painter grumbled, "You're far too polite to ask me what I am doing in this august company, so I'll tell you. But you won't believe me, though it's the truth, s'help me, God, and a Boy Scout's honor! I got in the wrong column. There's a Reginald Marsh picture in a show opening in the art building. I wanted to see the picture. Marsh is something. Well, these damned campus paths are all alike and the buildings in the snow not so different, and I heard some man at the tail-end of a crowd mention a picture show, so I naturally assumed the column was on its way to it. I was thinking of you and that nice daughter I caught a glimpse of, and of my wife, and of a possible purchase of a pound of candy-oh, of all kinds of nice things-and I was giving no attention to my immediate surroundings, and I didn't wake up, by Jesus, until I was in here. What is it?" "It's a reception for Princeton alumni, some of the faculty, visiting celebrities and permanent dwellers on the fringelike me." "None of which I am, so let's go over to your place, after we find the Reginald Marsh."

Charles grinned. "But I'm not interested in the painting,

.

and what makes you think I wish to leave this pleasant gathering of pleasant people?" "I've been observing you. I know how to observe. It's my business. Come on, you've been seen. That's enough. Every one will think you're with some one else. We've a whole day before us. Remember when you were a kid-oh, ten, eleven years old? An unexpected holiday would come along and out of the house you'd run. What would you do first of all?" 

Harold's massive face was radiant as he hurried to answer his own question. "You'd look for a friend to share the day. 

It would be, 'Hey, Skinny,' or 'Stinky,' or something. Well, here I am. You never come to me, so I've come over to your yard to play. Only let's get out of the yard. Now, that Reginald Marsh-"

So, a few minutes later they were standing before the finely painted lurid interior of a New York subway car. A long silence. 

Then, "That's a painting! Does something to you, eh, Easton?" "Not to me. Just another picture. I like it well enough." "Humph. Let's go." "Now where?" "Stained glass." "The chapel?" "Yep. I'm not interested in the building, the atmosphere of quiet, the medieval altar, the carving. I'll accept as true the statement that it is all flawlessly beautiful, in close resemblance to the chapel in Cambridge, England. All I'll do is stare at the big blue window and struggle to catch my breath. The light through that glass!"

So they plodded to the huge chapel that is a cathedral, and

there in the color of light filtered through world-renowned stained glass Harold did breathe hard. After five minutes he muttered, "Does something to you, eh, Easton?” "Not to me. But it's restful in here." 

Harold snorted.

The hush in the empty chapel was ended when the university organist began to practise. Te Deum. Depth. Richness. 

They listened. Minutes of musical heritage. 

Harold hissed, "Don't tell me that does nothing to you." “It does, it certainly does. Forbes up there at the console is a fine player, the organ is the best, the surrounding perfect for the music; but what is being done to me-as you put it—is to make me think of an organist called Dorothy, playing on a commercial organ electrically stepped-up to demonic power, and a diapason of wooden wheels on a wooden floor." "What the hell are you talking about?" "A place of violent living. Far from this unquestionably lovely hush and sacred musical divinity of most cleverly applied color. The garish and lurid and flamboyant antithesis of these precincts." 

They stood on the granite steps under a dun sky. "Well," Harold snorted indignantly, "we're certainly making a Cook's Tour of the campus. How about the Department of Astronomy? Know any one there? We might squint through the telescope and make an exhaustive survey of the heavens in forty-three seconds." "Anything you like. But I'm leaving Princeton at one for the afternoon and evening. Until then we can-as you saidbe kids on a holiday. There's over an hour. Just what's our next romp to be? You decide."

“Ah, Easton, let's face the mournful truth. We can't go back to kids' holiday. We're old, man, old. Come on, let's walk over to Nassau Street for ice-cream and cake." 

Charles laughed. "Old! Not you, Slade. When you can smack your lips at thought of frozen cream and cake. Norand now I'm serious-when you can receive what the painting had to give, what the window, too, had for you." "And you?" "They had surfaces for me. The music too." "So you are old?” "Yes." "You are a God-damned liar! You mentioned a loud organ, wooden wheels on a floor. And your face was like mine at thought of color and form and chocolate and cake. Well, where's the rink? Get your skates. I'll come and sit watching you." "I don't skate. I just watch." "You're not altogether mad, so I assume you watch some one. Ah, of course, your daughter. That cool young person named Laurel. I've remembered her since that moment at your door a week or so ago.” 

Charles said quietly, politely, "I've not seen her since then.

I, too, have remembered her. While the rink is a public place, I am distinctly her guest. 

"I could go in alone, sit alone, be quite lost in the crowd, never be seen by you or by her." "You're a shrewd chap. Yes. I'm going to see my daughter, the head instructress of a big rink some thirty-five miles by bus from here. I'm quite certain that you guess I am not on informal terms with Laurel. You guess correctly." "I don't guess. Let's call it deduction. Ah, here we are! 

Renwick's. Marvelous place. Twenty flavors of ice-cream. I'm

always urging Helen to come in with me, but she won't walk. She's oriental in her languor." "What's your car for?" "Ice-bound, and three tires. Until spring.".

They settled at a table in the rear beyond the crowd of undergraduates. Tea, a lettuce and tomato sandwich, was brought to Charles, a double portion of chocolate and coffee ice-cream, a wide wedge of layer cake, to Harold. "Don't spoil my holiday, Easton. I'm in a mood for a rink. 

I've heard of this craze sweeping the country and it's time 

I saw some skating. That your daughter is engaged in it adds to my interest. But don't misjudge me. I'm a hog in everything but my skin. We'll sit separately in the bus. Not even your mood will risk a jar.” 

Charles smiled at the tiny, eager, anxious eyes lost in the conglomeration of large nose, wide greedy mouth, hairy ears, cascade of chins. "We can ride side by side,” he said quietly.

I'm not neurotic. It's simply that I'm in delicate terrain in the presence of my daughter. I'll tell you something, not very much, of that on the way."

The cautious telling-accomplished across the aisle of the bus, for Harold's wide and thick body required all but a few inches of the seat designed for two-brought, unrecognized by Charles, the real beginning of the only friendship he had found since the forgotten ones of boyhood. Why he talked he could not have told. There was a warmth in this vagabond artist, this caricature in flesh, that melted the glacial defenses of forty years. And while Charles talked Harold's fingers itched for the charcoal and sketch pad in his capacious breast pocket. To catch what was flooding that urbane, disciplined face! To catch even a fragment of such love. "Fleshless love I've never felt for a living being. Helen?

The slave and mistress of my body. But this, this worn, timesmoothed minor cog in the machine of education, this fugitive from the violent banquet of all senses, has found what is found by few-selfless, fleshless love. Hah! He's a good fellow, and I like him well."

They entered the ELIZABETH ROLLER ARENA separately. 

Eddie caught Laurel in the dressing-room alley. "News for kid. Real news." you, "What?" 

He pulled her to him. "Kiss." "Stop it! Are you crazy? You may not have enemies here, but I have plenty, and I don't let my hair down on the premises." "Do you ever let it down?" 

Laurel smiled from a safe yard's distance. "We-ell, not all the way." "We'll do the dance steps together," he said gloomily. 

At once Laurel became docile. "Of course. Oh, Eddie, I'm so glad for you that you're in the ROLLER FOLLIES, but I'm unhappy about breaking up. I can't see even the beginning of a dance partner. I've done the dance steps with a halfdozen boys, but none shapes up. You and I are really a team that-" "The best in the whole circuit." "Perhaps. But Sue Holle-" "Thirty-four years old, Laurel. She's been head instructress in a dozen rinks out West, and for three years in ROLLING 

AMERICA. She's a big name now, but she's slowing down. 

You'll have her job before long-unless Stevens brings in

some one from the outside, an' he ain't a guy to hesitate on that." "I think he likes my work." "Sure he does. But if grabbin' a big name from the outside will bring in a lot more dough, he'll grab it.”

Laurel shrugged. "I can't help that. All I can do about it is keep on skating." 

Eddie nodded. “So you're goin' to miss your old dance partner?" "You know how badly." "Let's go out and do our stuff. It's almost time." "But your news?" "Kiss."

Laurel glanced wildly at Eddie's dressing-room door and then at the door to the rink. "Quick, then.” 

He kissed her hard, his hand on her breast. 

Breathing fast, she pushed away, rolled to the door. "Laurel, kid, you're goin' to get your break. Sue is joining the FOLLIES. She can't juggle the way I do, so she had to give Stevens two weeks' notice. Old Hank huffed and puffed and stormed, but d'you know what I think? I think he's just as well pleased. The old gal's lost her speed. Now he has his excuse to let her go. Keep your fingers crossed, kid. It'll be you or an outsider."

The end of the afternoon was darkening the window of the passage. Laurel stood in the gloom, lips parted in sudden excitement. Head dancing instructress of all the rinks of 

ROLLING AMERICA. To teach professionals! 

"Your eyes are burning, Laurel. Well, I guess you never were cut out for love. No man could make 'em like that. 

Green fire, nothin' less. You're all skates, a hundred percent.

Come on, the floor's clearing for the last number. Let's give all we got.'

Laurel skated out for the routine line-up in formation. 

Bob skated out to meet the floor crew. 

Dance Steps was flashed on the bulletin. 

And Eddie claimed Laurel.

Tango, Style B Waltz, the Fourteen Step, the Collegiate, the Ice Polka. Rhythm on skates, in swerving, bending sweeps down the floor. Precision and pattern, rigidly stylized. 

Lovely movement, now on hesitant, fluttering pinions, now full and powerful. A dozen couples in the early dances, dwindling to three or four as the more difficult steps were performed, and finally at the very last Laurel and Eddie alone in the demanding Iceland Tango. They stood hand in hand waiting for the music. The cue! They stroked off, bodies together in flawless brilliance, down the center of the floor ringed by the hundreds behind the barrier rails. Laurel's face was set in its habitual professional composure, a faint smile permitted to her lips, Eddie's face was a mask covering the never-failing excitement and delight of public performance. This Iceland Tango, a dance of bright romance in which the sound of their skates was like swords slashing silk. "The best we ever done,” Eddie whispered on their final flight down the rink. 

It was over. 

Laurel skated out for the floor formation. The arena was hushed for the National Anthem. The clatter of skates' removal followed and quickly the building emptied. Laurel stroked slowly down to Charles, who was seated erect in his habitual chair. She was annoyed at a sense of guilt. To avoid him all afternoon, except for nods, occasional smiles from

varying distances, was certainly discourteous, but somehow it had seemed-well-here he was. "I stayed away all afternoon." "A little shy, Laurel?” 

She nodded. "Perhaps." "You to be shy with an old stick like me! And then to hold this crowd with your skating. Preposterous. Come out for dinner?" "No, thanks. I've some free style to practise. I have sandwiches. Share them with me." "I'd love to. Oh, Laurel, the tornado is sitting at the other end of the rink. Look, he's like a giant sea-turtle up for air." "What in the world is he sitting there for? Why not with you?" "Tact. He really does possess that quality. He was afraid of intruding. Yes, I had to tell him something of you and me. 

Not much, Laurel. Just the amount necessary to make sense. gave away no secrets."

"If you like him, what you tell him is up to you." "I think he'd be overcome if you'd skate down to him and tell him to waddle up." 

Laurel frowned. "I-I'm not-things like that aren't easy for me." 

Charles thought, "They aren't. You've had to fight too hard, and ease of manner has been lost in the early rounds." 

He stood up, waved an arm, and a low wide shape moved ponderously toward a second and less febrile introduction.

Laurel was startled at his first words, and then, misjudging them, finding them showy and false and absurd, felt awkward, nervous. The ponderous idiot was saying, “Laurel, you are the exception to my lifelong conviction that women in

hard motion lose charm and beauty. They must be still. 

Women playing tennis, riding horses, singing in night clubs, beating traffic lights, lose, for me, all charm. But you, out on that polished floor, are a gull in flight, a dream, a veritable heroine of what another woman might have made into violence. Do you follow me?" "You like my skating." 

Harold's laugh roared down the cooling rink, and then, struggling for breath, he panted, “Yes, I like it. You are marvelous. I've never seen— 

"Please, Mr. Slade. Some one will hear you. "And why not? They ought to. Your father tells me that you aren't the head instructress of this company. Hah! I'd love to see the woman who is over you. Probably the scarecrow wife of a heavy stockholder, or-"

Laurel reached for her armor, slipped into its coldness. "I must practise now for twenty minutes or so." She moved to the rail. "My father is sharing sandwiches with me. You are welcome too.' 

The painter stared at her. "You are a good child. Now tell me something. You didn't expect a guest to-night, did you?" "No." "So exactly how many sandwiches did you bring?" "Three. They're quite large. Too large. Often I give one away to some one. "You skated all afternoon, you are about to practise, you will skate until midnight or close to it?” "That's right." "And you offer me a third of your supper!" Harold stood up. “Easton, watch your daughter skate. I'll be back presently with provender." 

Charles grinned, waved him away. Laurel went into

twenty minutes of routine: toe-spins, arabesques, spreadeagles. Charles commented crisply when at last she dropped into the chair at his side, "The funny part is that Slade is right. When I first spoke to you, Laurel, months ago, I said that I enjoyed your work. That's about the limit of my vocabulary of enthusiastic expression. But inwardly I felt and feel in the flamboyance of Slade. Damn it, you are a gull out there, there is wild beauty-"

She skated away, disappeared in the dressing-room passage. When she came back she was walking, wrapped in the fur, and the latter, she informed him, was a sacrilege in this dusty place. Charles was aware of her excitement and soaring, fearful hope, but he asked no questions. He watched her take from a bag the three sandwiches, and accepted one while trying to swallow a lump in his throat before taking a single bite. It wasn't, he assured himself, that her supper wasn't reasonably sustaining-indeed, in quantitative sense it was too heavy for her-but surely it was one-sided and unsatisfactory. Bread, a mere scraping of butter, a leaf of soggy lettuce, and a meat he did not recognize. Good enough for the once-in-a-while emergency, but from what she had suggested to him, this was pretty much her customary dinner. 

Of course, she was a grown woman, but, damn it, she was his kid, and she had insides, and insides were not to be abused! "Laurel," he began, and stopped to smile when she arrested the sandwich at her lips. "Do you know the difference between a gourmand and a gourmet?" "No. Is there any?" "Decidedly. But sometimes there is an overlap. Thus 

Slade. He is a little of both, I mean a great deal of both. 

And the reason I mention it is that he will be in here presently with anything from lion steak to tiger milk in quantity

suitable to his own bulk. So don't blunt the edge of your appetite." "Oh, no, he won't. Except the wretched restaurant the rink crowd uses, there's nothing to be had for a mile around. And 

I can tell you that the restaurant offers none of such delicacies as you mention. Hamburg steak and onions and catsup is what your friend will bring.' "Laurel, don't you ever eat green vegetables, drink milk?”

This she ignored, and it was as if she had said, "Your tone changed suddenly to an authority I don't accept," so Charles began to eat, and presently looked up, pointed down the rink. "We'll know in a minute." They did. "There wasn't much selection," Harold grumbled. “I could have brought ham omelettes, but I figured they'd get cold and stiff on the way, so I brought hamburg with onions and 

French fried potatoes. They hold the heat.' 

Laurel smiled as Charles said, “Laurel, you know ritory." your ter The hot steak sandwiches were just right, Laurel remarked, for this cold hour between sessions. Above Harold's protests she ate lightly, and when he scolded her, Charles noticed that she was meek, and, he knew, for a reason quite clear. Slade was an amusing stranger, not a parent who might develop from an early bit of advice a suggestion of authority to follow. He sighed, and then brightened. What a marvelous hour this was! Unbelievable that he was sitting with his daughter, informally comfortable, welcome in her strange and still rather dismaying milieu. Suddenly he wanted, above all things in the world, to touch her hand, her cheek. But not yet, not yet. Slowly was the word. To touch her! He would trade the security of his work, the protection of the conservatory's gray walls and concert hall, for the privilege of

having her return the pressure of his hand. This, he knew, was love defying the putative banality of a thousand "Daddy" songs, defeating the cynicism commonly aroused by their saccharine words, for-"God damn it, those saccharine words are absolutely true, as birth and death are true! 

Cheaply crooned or immortally sung, the words are truebirth, love, death.” 

Laurel laughed sharply. "See the couple near the entrance?

That's the man in charge of the first aid room, and she's a checkroom attendant. Know what they are probably thinking? That you and Mr. Slade are talent scouts, or representatives from some other company, interviewing me. You have no idea of the wild gossip in this life." "A circus, no doubt," Harold commented. 

Charles said gravely, "There's something you must decide before long, Laurel. Perhaps to-night. The matter of my identity. Not on a bet, as you know, will I risk any awkwardness for you; but some tag must be given me. Long ago I've been noticed sitting here; you know how familiar faces become." "I've decided. There's no reason now for not telling the truth. There'll be a flutter of exclamations. June will say to 

Alice, 'Al, that man who stares at Laurel is her father. Can you tie that?' And Alice will say to June, 'Where did she ever get such a handsome father?' And Ben, the doorman, will say about the same thing to the first aid man, and Maizie in the ticket booth will exclaim to Marge Brennan, the manager's wife, and so it will go. They'll all make up mysteries about it, because for weeks a father and daughter under the same roof gave each other no recognition." She stopped. 

Harold said, "Mysteries die quickly without material. An eight-day wonder." "That's right, Mr. Slade."

Charles admitted relief. "We'll begin right at the top; here comes Jim Brennan."

Laurel, turning to the manager, spoke awkwardly. "Mr. Brennan, this is my father, Charles Easton, and his friend, Mr. Slade."

Jim stared, fumbled badly in stiff expression of his pleasure.

Charles was bland. "Possibly you've seen me here in your splendid and glittering rink, Mr. Brennan, on many occasions. I was attempting to convince myself that an accomplished skater was really my daughter from whom life's merciless vagaries had separated me for many years.".. "Whew!" he thought, "take that on the chin, you ignorant bastard, with your dumb dislike of my daughter!" From the corner of his eye he caught what he named for the first time a Sladic grin. ... 

Jim groped for reality. "Laurel. A girl's comin' to-night. 

I think her name is Margaret Murphy. Skate with her a lot, an' try her out for instructress at Trenton. She's applying for the job." "Why doesn't Sue Holle-" "I dunno a thing about it. She brought a letter from Mr. 

Stevens an' the letter said for you to put her through the paces, an' decide whether to hire her." He turned to Charles. "Pleased to have met you, Mr. Easton," and, ignoring Harold, walked away. 

Laurel looked gravely at Charles. "If you do that very often, I'm afraid his dislike for me will turn to hate." But her eyes,

Charles saw, were alive with amusement, and the habitual gravity of her lips seemed a willed pressure upon bright laughter. "Why doesn't he like you, Laurel? I wanted to ask you

when you told me in Princeton, but, well-I hesitated, and didn't butt in."

She shrugged. "I know no reason. Something in my makeup jars on him. It doesn't worry me. Lately, since my last show, he's been better." She looked down the empty rink. "There's that girl, I bet. Good-by. Staying for the session?" 

Harold grumbled. "Of course, both of us." "Thanks for the supper. 

Eddie whirled into the inner oblong to Laurel's side, braked hard, and spoke into her ear. "What did I tell you?

It's just around the corner; you're taking over already on 

Sue's job. The girl looks good, don't she?" "I'm recommending her. Watch her make the turn. Eddie, look at her legs. Grand for a skater. Power, drive. But she is a bit on the heavy side.' 

"She has what it takes,” he said briefly. “Ah, Eddie, is there anything lovelier than a skater's stroking legs. That power, drive!" 

Eddie laughed. "There's nothin' nicer than yours, Laurel. 

They'd have power and drive in more ways than one."

And I must make no mistake. How do you like her?" "I told you Margaret Murphy looks good, but as you said, a bit heavy, fat even. Where's she come from? What's her skating background?" "Don't know a thing about her. But it's clear she's a dancer. 

Brennan just tossed her to me." "Trios next. Shall we take her? Sorta get her used to us." 

The three skated fast, Eddie between the girls, singing as

Dorothy played with the increased tempo permitted. Hun-

dreds of swaying, bending bodies in units of three, circled the arena as young voices sang in sheer pleasure of freedom and unleashed energy. Old favorites of the musical hit parades-Roll Out the Barrel, Siboney, and a medley of more recent songs. The songs swept up to the rafters, the speed increased, and watchful, incredibly skilful, the floor men in their blue and gray swerved in and out of the skating columns, on continuous guard against the danger of falls in this fast number on the schedule. 

Rounding the turn where, behind the rail, Charles and Harold sat, a trio, breaking the rule, tried to rush past Eddie and Laurel and Margaret and to slice back into the column in a bending line. The skate of the inside offender caught, locked the new girl's wheel, and in an instant she was flat from a fall that not even Laurel and Eddie, so long trained, could prevent. Laurel was dragged down too, but sprang up at once, stooped to lift the girl. Eddie was standing to split the onrushing column. Whistles shrilled, and in mere seconds four floormen were diverting, slowing the stream, but three persons had already smashed into the fallen girl and piled up in a whirl of arms and legs. Luck! Youth! Resilience! Legs were untangled from the endangered and endangering maelstrom, skaters pulled to their feet, reassured by the floor men, and sent on their way in reassembled threesomes. The maelstrom was dissipated into a mere eddy and this into the swift main flow. And above the grind of wheels the voices rose again on the organ's accompaniment. 

Margaret, Laurel discovered, was silently weeping as she skated, so she gave Eddie a significant nod and drew the girl through the nearest opening in the rail. "I'll take you to the first aid room." "No. I'm not hurt. Just shaky. I'll be all right in a minute.”

"Come into my dressing-room. You'll sit down for a while."

Straddling a long bench the two girls faced each other, the younger one tragic as she smiled through her tears. "Well, Miss Easton, I guess I've booted away my chance twice to-night." "How do you figure that?" "First the fall, then the weeps." "Were you hurt at all, really?" "No. Jarred." "Why did you cry?" "The job. During a night of testing, to fall with the-the instructress-well, it finished me with you, didn't it? Anyway," she hurried on, "I suppose it did, and I've been jittery for a long while-" "Jittery? That doesn't go in a rink. If Eddie and I, and the floor men were that way, you'd be in the first aid room now, cut and bruised, or worse, and probably there would be others with you." "Oh, I'm calm as a regular thing. It's just to-night. Trying for the job, and then crashing. You know, it adds up." "I see it does," Laurel said gravely.

The younger woman sat, twisting her wet handkerchief. "How long have been skating?" you "On and off for years. Just for pleasure. Amateur, of course. I'm a dancer. At least I was a dancer." "I saw that, Miss Murphy, in the first ten minutes of your skating. Amateur?" “No. Bread and butter and jam. I was a Rockette at Radio

City for two seasons. Then a year ago I began to put on weight. All over me. And then it was all over for me as a 

Rockette. Precision dancing, ballet." She shrugged. "Well, you know what the Rockette ensemble calls for. I was given

the air a few weeks ago. Now there's no jam-although I look as if I live on it-and not so much bread and butter." "I can't hire you, I mean recommend you-but it's the same thing-just because you are in hard luck. It's all a matter of cold business. What about reducing back into the Rockettes?" "Lord, Miss Easton, can you think I haven't tried? It's simply no go. Look at my fingers even." She spread them on the bench. "They look like short fat sausages." "You exaggerate," Laurel said coldly. "And your legs are very good." She gazed steadily at Margaret, up and down from the heart-shaped face under its defiant ruff of brown hair, down the thickening body, to the strong ankles. She noticed the poor skates, and the skating dress as poor. But she noticed, too, that save for the damage by tears, the face was carefully made up; that the fingernails were tended and evenly tinted, that the costume was clean aside from the dragged smear of the fall. "I fall too, Miss Murphy. And I cry once in a while at the end of a rotten day, but I find it wise to cry in my room where no one ever knows about it. I've got to go now. Stay in here as long as you like. Please be sure to close the door when you leave."

She nodded, skated out, wishing her face was heart-shaped like the dancer's, but glad that her fingers weren't even faintly suggestive of little sausages. She found Jim in his office. "Miss Murphy has my O. K.," she said briefly. "Did you tell her?” "No. That's in your hands. Do you want any details about her?" 

The manager shook his head. "Not now. The skating O. K.

is enough. She'll work in Trenton." He scratched his head. "How is it I never met your father before? He said he'd been here plenty.” "Rinks are funny places," Laurel said coolly, instantly on defense. "For me they mean skating. Everything else drifts by." "Humph." "Anything else, Mr. Brennan?" "No. Good crowd here to-night." "Very big.” She rolled to the door, nodded, went out.

It was an hour later when, skating with Beatrice, Laurel felt a hand timidly take hold of her wrist and then give a quick hard squeeze. She glanced around into the eyes of the former Rockette, smiled briefly, and stroked easily on. "That's a new instructress for Trenton, Bea. Nice-looking girl, don't you think?"

LAUREL TURNED from the window and spoke down from the high seat to Charles lost in the shadow of the living-room where the lamps had not yet been turned on against the swiftly falling dusk. "I'm never the optimist, but I really think the next goal is near." The dark gray window behind her made her face seem to Charles a mere form, without features, but the tremor in her voice told him of shining eyes and bright mouth. He waited, knowing she would explain.

She did, in characteristically brief sketch. The ROLLER 

FOLLIES had opened in Detroit, had promised success; Sue 

Holle, head instructress of ROLLING AMERICA was in the show, and Laurel had been given much of Sue's work. “I'm lucky to get my regular Monday holiday. I was supposed to judge a free style contest in our Paterson rink to-night, but almost at the last moment it was canceled, so here I am.

No direct word yet from Mr. Stevens, but there is the fact of so much of Sue's work given to me. And there's one more hint, one great big hint." Charles rejoiced in the sudden, unusual ring in her voice. “A few nights ago Mr. Brennan called me into his office and introduced me to a Mr. White. 

Mr. Stevens was there too. I waited until the name should mean something and then Mr. White took charge. He asked 134

me a few questions, was pleasant, and remarked quietly that he was the president of the company that makes the Sue 

Holle skating shoes. I quaked, and looked, I knew, like a gawky simpleton. You see why, don't you?” "No," Charles said. "I don't even know what the Sue 

Holle shoe is." "It's the official shoe for ROLLING AMERICA. Expensive but, soon or late, every one buys it. Well-what was the introduction for, if not to let Mr. White see me? And why was he to see me unless-" Laurel swung her legs down from the window-seat, drummed her heels on the panel. "Unless it was in his mind that his company was to manufacture a new skating shoe for ROLLING AMERICA called the Laurel Easton? 

Tell me, what else could all the shooting be for?"

Charles paused in the filling of his pipe. “I think you have something there. Nothing definite was said?" Laurel shook her head. "There were the usual grunts from 

Mr. Stevens, and the final one that always means get out." "So you did?" "Did what?" "Get out." 

Laurel laughed. "When Mr. Stevens grunts, a rink is built. Yes, I slid out on fast skates."

Charles breathed out the first fan of gray smoke, and rose to switch on the lamps. "I want to look at the new head instructress of all the rinks." "It isn't sure. I'm-" "All the indications," he said. "Besides, you look like her." But, swinging her legs, she seemed to him the schoolgirl 

Laurel he had never known, careless, relaxed. That, he told himself, was what she should look. Twenty-three could be such a wonderful age, but in those damned rinks of hers

there was a glut of tension. Dancing on skates was beautiful, but it was all strain and anxious effort in maintaining a precise and elaborately stylized pattern. Skates lent wings to dancing, bore it into the breathless realm of lovely flight far above the slow revolutions on a ballroom floor, but the element of ease and dream was missing. "At least, for me,”

Charles thought. "Though possibly I'm wrong. She would tell me so, crisply enough. Nevertheless I've never seen her like this. She's always been strained under her cool, aloof manner. No. My daughter is not a gay person." But the daughter who was not a gay person suddenly laughed. "The joke?” he demanded. "This set-up in your Princeton home." "Eh? I don't get it." "Well, here am I, a young woman from a noisy manufacturing town, having no social life-and wanting none, you understand-invited to a university town for an evening. I sort of expected, if we were to go out, that I'd be taken out to see your music school, to a recital there, or to a Princeton hockey or basketball game, or even to a night lecture.
