Laurel Easton in a college setting of culture, education, of social or athletic activity. Instead, I'm to hike two miles out into the country to take dinner with a-a-I think you called him a vagabond painter." 

Charles grinned. "Not only take dinner with him, but take it to him. I'm buying steak. Yes, he's a vagabond, but he is also a real painter-he says. I'm no judge. He told me to bring you for dinner, whenever you came to see me. Laurel, if you haven't deduced, it's time I told you, that my Neville- Princeton social life is a blank. A dead loss conventionally examined. It isn't that I'm a pariah. My colleagues are

friendly men, the university staff across the street is hospitable and cordial, but I'm altogether, by choice or by social qualities lacked, on the outside of both institutions. I don't visit. I'm not visited. I stalk from these rooms to the conservatory, do my work, stalk back. Students come here when necessary. I've never seen a Princeton football game or any other form of formal athletics during the ten years I've existed so near the stadium. And getting back to the social end of things, well, an invitation to any one's home comes seldom now. Seldom, indeed, and that is to be expected, when none goes from me. I'm sorry, Laurel. Of course I might brush up my life a bit, even polish it, however dully." "I laughed, didn't I? That's not weeping. You're pretty much like me. I stalk from my room to the rink, do my work, and stalk back. So! But this Mr. Slade. How is it he breaks in on your- "Ah, hanged if I know! Actually I don't know. I ran into him in a snowstorm, head-on, was taken to his trailer, was practically, but quite inoffensively, ignored by his somewhat incredible wife. The second time we met was here on the night you came to me. The third time was Washington's

Birthday when he attached himself to me for the rink journey. To-night will be the fourth time. Three meetings only!

Yet I like him in a way. Quite a way apparently, for, as you say, he is breaking down my solitude and general indifference." "Eddie breaks mine down, just a little."

Again Charles felt the pang, remembering the similar one from a month before. "Perhaps neither of us knows just how it happens." "Anyway, I'm glad it's this way with you. And as long as we have to go anywhere, I prefer the trailer. I'm not very,

well, hilarious under any circumstances, and with Mr. Slade 

I don't have to act or even talk. He does it all for me." "He does. For his Helen too."

Nassau Street was dripping with the mists of March and the butchershop was a smuggler's cave lit by lantern, and odorate with carcass of illegally slaughtered sheep, cow and fowl. Mr. Howe, jovial as a smuggler at ease, carved out a large steak before the equally uninformed eyes of Laurel and her father, wrapped it deftly in waxed paper and assurance of its tenderness and nodded his two customers from the lurid cavern.

Charles felt helpless before a mounting rage as he walked along in the fine and substantial products of Princeton's clothing emporia. Suit of English tweed, a warm sweater, pigskin gloves, a light raincoat, a Homburg hat, and stout brogans that rang on the pavement presented him from head to foot with weather-defying comfort and quietly correct appearance. Not a fussy note, not a studied note, simply the appointed things to wear on a damp March night. So much for him.

Glances lowered to the girl at his side found her, in contrast with him, inadequately clad; not that there was anything definitely wrong, he admitted, from the viewpoint of neatness; nor was her gray dress shabby. "And her fingers aren't protruding from split gloves, nor her toes from her shoes. The hat is crazy as any woman's in nineteen-forty-one, and the glazed raincoat is probably but three years old. 

But she is not wearing one single thing of good wool, good glazed oiled fabric, good leather. Her clothing gives bravely enough the best that cheapness allows. That precious Eddie of hers would probably approve if he noticed at all. Stevens, Brennan, Marge, Pete, Harry, Jerry, June, Alice, the whole

damned merry-go-round would not, among them all, emit a single sniff. Yet here I am swinging along in, hm, let me seesuit forty-five dollars, sweater five dollars, hat five, shoes seven, raincoat ten, that's seventy-two. Now add gloves, shirt, various accessories, oh, I'll say eighty dollars. 

He looked at Laurel more carefully. More mental arithmetic. "Dress a bargain somewhere at five dollars; shoes not more, certainly, than four, probably less; raincoat robbery at a cent above five dollars; hat from a crammed dollar window. Accessories four or five. Humph. Daughter in a penetrating mist clad from head to foot for twenty dollars or less, father in repulsed mist clad for eighty dollars. Four to one! And the grim laugh is that I don't shop for mine. I barely select. I simply go into a college shop and a supersalesman covers me with raiment proper for year, season, time of day. I don't fuss, I don't give a damn, but I emerge well dressed, and secure in that fact. She peers into the windows of cheap shops, struggles with conniving salesgirls, fingers this, rejects that, and emerges like-oh, God damn it, the hell of it is I don't dare offer-" "You are muttering. "I'm choking with fury!" "Why?" "Never mind," he snarled. "We're far out of Princeton now, and it's beginning to drizzle. And a taxi would be a diamond found in the slag heap of a West Virginia coal mine." "I've never seen you like this. Fussing with trifles. Think of the steak, and the warmth of the Slade's trailer. Are your feet getting wet?" "My feet! Why, God damn it! I-” "Oh, mine?" She laughed up at him. “They're tough.”

"Oh, yes, I suppose you are tough all over. Grippe, flu, pneumonia haven't a chance." "I don't know what chance they have, except that they have the same with me as with other people, but this much I do know, that working in ROLLING AMERICA actually is toughening. On sudden calls for exhibition in other rinks 

I have to leave a warm place, sometimes-not usually-perspiring, and rattle thirty or forty miles in cold car or cool bus, and change into a costume in a sometimes cold dressingroom. All that sort of thing. You ought to feel the temperature of my bedroom when I come into it late at night! Then, add the powdery dust of the arenas, the swift changes in winter weather, and you'll realize that a mere walk in drizzle with gradually dampening clothes and shoes doesn't mean a thing to me. I get my quota of coughs and colds, but 

I seem to throw them off, or skate them off. So stop fidgeting about me, please. I'm tough. Let's change the subject." "So be it, Toughie. Have you any idea why you use good English?" "What?" 

He repeated slowly. "That's a silly question. After all, I graduated from high school." "You've been in rink life for about four years. I assume that the staff of one rink is much like that of another. Yes?" 

She nodded. "Well, then, in the midst, all the time, of 'them things,' 'I don't say nothin',' 'I done it,' I should look for a-a contamination." "I don't notice. I speak as I always have. School training evidently has lasted. My grandmother spoke well enough.

Many of the rink people left high school before graduating. 

And at home they probably heard poor speech." "Your Eddie, too?" "Yes," she said coldly. “It's the least important part of him.

I admire him. He's doing a good job in the ROLLER FOLLIES, too. You-really you mustn't-are knocking my life a little too much and-" "Laurel! I'm not. In its contrast with my own phlegmatic life, my bow to yours is deep and sincere. Each of these two lives has its advantages and disadvantages." "That's true, but there's no changing all that. I'm a professional skater. You're a teacher." "Do you read much?” "Just skating magazines and textbooks, figure skating, the dances, that sort of thing. Do you?” "No." 

"That's funny." "Why?" "Wel-l. You tell me you are given the privileges of the university. There's the library. Bookstores. Oh, I don't know; 

I'd just expect you to, that's all.” "I'm slothful." "Perhaps you are.” "You've a queer father, Laurel. He's all façade. Probably the drunken pianist was the true Charles Easton. In respectable environment he's just a store window dummy, useful, but inanimate. You are the alive one of the pair.”

A sudden question about Harold was a signal to him that she was uneasy on this persistent trail into personal terrain, and so he talked vaguely of the man he knew so little of, and then fell into silence and the steadiness of their walking. 

His stick sank into mud, struck sharply at times on the mace-

rated stones of the country road. Trees and bushes were mere blobs darker than the fallen night and his thoughts for a while bore to them a dull resemblance. The trailer. Its bland atmosphere of lurid carnality. What would candid young eyes make of that? Perhaps nothing beyond a lazy surface. Yet Laurel was twenty-three. That first long talk in the Trenton restaurant. "I've been on my own since I was a fourteen-year-old girl." Nine years. Clinging to the back of a newspaper truck. Helper in a cleaning and pressing place. 

Dime-store salesgirl. Her playground the streets. Humph! 

Candid young eyes had seen much beneath surfaces. Could mind, could body, have remained virgin- "Is it much farther?" "We're almost there, Laurel. I think I recognize this slope and bend; I've been there only once, you see." And there it was, the windows yellow and welcoming with light of oil. At their knock the door in the side opened. "Welcome, Father and Daughter, to the resounding halls of Castle-in-the-Mire. Laurel, this is Helen." The hoarse voice boomed on in vast quantity of nothing, while coats and hats were removed and the steak inspected, approved and turned over to Helen. "What a gorgeous pair of barnstormers we are!" Harold declaimed. “Playing countless rôles. Thus, last night I was the travel-weary patron of this inn of Araby, and there Helen, the Mistress of the Inn, captivated by my looks, confident of the man beneath the stained habiliments, became without ado the ardent plaything of my amorous mood, and this morning and afternoon I was the furious painter, and she the silent, adoring and-I hasten to add-adorable wife and model, and now I am the host and she the lowly wench at the stove-"

"Don't listen to him, Miss Easton. He's a little crazy-" "Woman! I'm not a little anything. If I'm mad it is in a big way, the Slade way. I'll slit your voluptuous white throat-" "This is a fine steak, Mr. Easton," Helen said lazily, “but not quite up to Harold's standards. That's not your fault. He simply scowls and thunders at the butcher and to get rid of him, or in sheer fright, the poor man gives him the best and a relieved good-by." She went to the stove, completely dismissing husband and company, not even turning when Harold moved to the studio end of the trailer and lifted an unframed canvas from a group facing the wall and placed it on the easel.

He said, “It is finished. That, my friends, is a naked of 

Helen. Not a nude! I despise the word. La di da, a nyude. 

Tho thweet. Tho chaste. Tho thircumthpect for the art department of the Woman's Club. Bah! That's a naked. That reclining woman on the day-bed is flesh and form and sensual promise and accomplishment. A man and his wife will stand before it in a gallery, a couple, mind you, that has been for years on breakfast-newspaper conjugal terms, separate rooms, locked doors, even two cars, and after the glacial chap has caught his breath and looked his fill, he'll whisper to the poor frozen statuette, Hum, my dear, I think, yes, I really think, er, er, I think I'll not go to the ballgame to-day, instead-' "But it's the Dodgers, Cuthbert. Your Dodgers!' "And gathering firmness, Cuthbert says, 'My dear, you and 

I are going home for the afternoon.' "Oh, Cuth, you're terrible.' “Exeunt. Curtain. Incidentally, Easton, that's a damned fine painting. And you, Laurel. You annoy me. It's this. There on the easel is an example of my better work. I'm proud of it.

And I tell you it's good. Ought I simper and murmur that it is lousy? But you, when I praised your wonderful skating, your art, simpered, twisted your fingers into a braided pigtail, dug your toe into the maple floor of the rink, and stammered-I remember your very words-Please, Mr. Slade. 

Some one will hear you."" “I never simper. I don't wear pigtails. My toe can not pierce hardwood, and I never stammer." “Child, you are wonderful, literal, and my feeling for you is a father's adoration." 

Charles said dryly, “One father at a time, Slade." 

Laurel laughed. "Go on chattering. I like it. At the rink we're serious. Even when I'm with Jeanie and Beatrice and the other kids, gabbing away, it's serious because it's about skating." 

The hilarity in Harold's porcine eyes vanished. “And there, Easton, my musical friend, is a sharp admonition to you and me. Your kid is the non-frolicking, non-wasting artist. She'll go farther than either of us." “I'm not an artist,” said Charles. “There isn't a gleam of that in my cosmos." "You like the picture?" Harold demanded of Laurel. "Yes." 

Harold indicated Helen with a wave of his thick arm. "Does it look like her?" "I don't know." 

Harold's laugh was roared. When his chins stopped quaking, he gasped, “Of course you don't know. Literal again. 

Marvelous." Then a deadly seriousness came into his eyes, onto his mouth. The whole man changed instantly. "Helen," he said quietly, “take the pose on the bed." "The steak?" she wailed. "It's half cooked."

"Do something with it. Shove it aside for three minutes.” "Clothes?" "Off.”

Badly startled, Charles saw her strip off the few things she was wearing, leave them in a heap on the floor, and stretch out on the bed, lying on her side, facing them with open eyes, as she was on the easel. Her soft heavy breasts were close together, one leg was flung over the other. Her dull blond hair was crushed on the pillow. "Now," Harold said, “forget the sensuality I spoke of.

That's the obvious part of the picture. Forget the glaze and the polish. Forget the bone structure. Now forget the whole painting. Look at the woman on the bed. What do you see?

This. The woman who has been my wife for many years, who has worked for me, with me, who has changed from a girl, from a very young woman, into this splendidly faithful comrade, lazy, attractive and, beyond everything else, real. 

That's a female, real and loving. That is my refuge and my zest." His voice grew even hoarser in its depth. "Now look at the painting. Look at it for a while. Then tell me what you see."

Laurel stared, her painful embarrassment before these men, before the woman too, destroyed by the flaming truth in the unarmored man, her own integrity in work recognizing the basic integrity of this other worker. She studied the faded, still pretty face in the picture, the attractive body, lethargic, brooding, the submission, the heavy moodiness of a slumbering ardent desire. Yes, that was the painting of Slade's woman on the bed. She turned at last from the picture. "I see," she said briefly, "what you see." "I'm glad," Harold said. “And that's the end of that. You

and I have crossed another bridge into friendship. In fact, it's the only long bridge, and we have joined hands." He turned to Helen. "O. K., sweet. Put your clothes on."

But first she ran to the stove, rescued the cooling steak, and replaced the wire broiler over the coal, shook up the potatoes.

Charles, quite unimpressed with art, integrity, wrestled with the twisting elusive forms of indignation aroused by this lurid exhibitionism in the presence of his daughter, and because of a hated stirring in himself of senses long dismissed. "Blast this crazy guy! I plunge her into this writhing nest. God! It takes me back to the black years when 

I banged out the tunes for studio freaks. Look at the woman!"

Satisfied, Helen stooped, picked up the mound on the floor, and, dressing quickly, returned to the stove. 

The dinner was a definite success. 

Charles forgot his feeling of vicarious offense when he saw that Laurel was accepting Harold without effusiveness or reserve, obviously liking him and extending to him a cool respect. But the vanishing of offense did not mean for 

Charles the return of equanimity. Surely a father, fifty-five, hm, fifty-six last week, ought to have, if even in attenuate degree, a social background to offer his daughter. That Laurel did not desire it had nothing to do with the matter. Background ought to be there. Conceivably a day would come when she would welcome a quiet withdrawal from the febrile rink, a few new friends, and among the daughters and sons of his colleagues these friends should be found. He sighed heavily. But what could he do?

The prospect of losing his whole retreat in life by floundering into a strange and, because it was strange, a hostile

social pond, was dismaying. Even if Laurel were willing to coöperate it would be uncomfortable for him, although he would do it for her, but in the face of her definitely expressed preference, for instance, for an evening in this blasted trailer to an evening in any conventional corner of the local social realm, there seemed no sense in his arranging for his own upheaval. "Am I scurrying around mentally like a fussy old duck? And have I any choice, if it comes to that? Laurel is fiercely independent, instantly on defense when she scents an interfering mentor."

Charles took a last look at the social pond before abandoning all thought of it. These children of his colleagues, acquaintances. Of Laurel's generation. They were not in any valuable consideration her superior, and, to their credit, he knew that they would not think so, but it was clear that their surface lives were smoother than hers. Different in sharp degree. Most of them were leaving campus surroundings, prepared for promising marriages and careers along conventional lines. (Laurel's love of high school study seemed pathetic when balanced against the cool acceptance these other intelligent young persons gave to the prospect of more advanced education.) Their talk, ideas, recreations, were fairly common to them all, adumbrating the accomplishments to come in the professions and arts. 

It wasn't, Charles hastened to add, in the least degree that they were snobbish. Quite the opposite, as a matter of fact; but it was simply that Laurel would not be captured by their interests, or they by hers. A lovely perfect arabesque? 

What would it mean to them? What could it be expected to mean? As an accomplishment in the weak, old-fashioned sense of accomplishment, yes, it would be a graceful trick of a young lady. The modern equivalent of dabbling in

water-colors. But as work, the object of endless hours of study and practice, these young persons aiming at their futures could not take it seriously. Laurel would be exposed to sheer lack of comprehension, however courteously concealed.

Charles sighed, this time, with the relief of an arrived decision. “I am not cut out for a social conductor, and Laurel is not cut out for one to be conducted. So if she likes this steak-devouring paint slinger, it is up to her. She's seen, I trust, the worst, as far as the wife goes. Mrs. Slade, broiling a steak, while all of her clothes are on the floor, marks the limit of whatever pseudo-Bohemianism this marriage flaunts. 

But I wonder if that's quite just. I have no reason to believe 

Slade a faker. And if I've seen a representative of sincerity, dull obedience, and sheer carnal receptivity, it is that middleaged, fairly pretty woman opposite me, chewing potatoes with relish. Well, that's settled. If Laurel wants it so, Princeton life will mean my rooms and the trailer while it remains at anchor. To hell with a society that, I'm afraid, would crack my urbanity, firmly established as it is." 

Suddenly his spirits soared in wild flight of sparks. Whatever limits were imposed by his nature, and by Laurel's, the gold fact was shining in his heart, the fact of Laurel's cautious acceptance. There she sat, quietly eating the bread of his introduction. And, by God, spring was coming, and whether she wanted to accept or not, she would be raimented in spring colors and brightness, if once more he would have to brave the intimidating salon of Camille La 

Tourette on Thirty-fourth Street, and seek connivance. 

He gazed across the table at his quiet daughter. How lovely her eyes were under the heavy, long-lashed lids! 

Would they ever look at him with warm forgiveness, and

bright welcome? Would her hand reach out with spontaneity to Harold emitted a loud and conquering belch, lowered his four chins and, as his eyes closed, was asleep. Helen stared blindly into space. Charles thought, "This is where I came in some ten weeks ago. Exactly the same tableau." He smiled at Laurel. 

An hour passed. 

Harold slept. 

Helen remained in her beatific trance.

Laurel talked in her quick, almost dismissive way, of skating and rink items. Dorothy had left the rink a week ago for marriage in Florida. The new organist, Alexandra, was capable and fiery. Laurel, true to form as she admitted regretfully, was slow in getting to know the newcomer so important in every feature of the skating routine. "I miss Dorothy, although I've always known she did not really like me.

She thought I was, you know, bossy. I don't think I am. It's just that when I'm working I'm thinking about work, and I'll yell up to the organist to stop while I explain something about a dance step, and since I'm in earnest, I don't remember to smile. Anyway, that's the way things go with me. I guess I've no knack for making friends. In spite of the professional publicity I've been getting lately in the skating magazines, pictures, articles, items, I'm not popular. In a way I'm sorry, of course, but mostly I don't care. Lack of friends leaves more time, much more, for work. What do you think?"

Delighted by this first direct, if unimportant reference to his opinion, Charles said, “I think you are quite right. Popularity is pleasant, warming, but often it is a time and energy waster. I see that every year with so many of the

student body. Mind you, I don't state it as a generality, but 

I do believe this, that when one lacks, as you say you lack, a knack for making friends easily, then the effort required to make friends will take much time from your work, and even throw you off your stride by altering too drastically attitude and definite time schedules." your "I'm glad we agree. Living alone is the right thing for me-I mean literally living alone, room, meals, the days offbut deciding everything alone isn't so good. Even about the theory of popularity. I get shaky. A question will come up. I'll make my decision. And it works out as far as my work is concerned. But then I'll feel opposition form among the others. It's as if they mutter, "That cold, egotistical Laurel 

Easton. Always pushing, grabbing."" "Come now, Laurel," Charles protested. "It's not that bad. 

You're building up a fear." "Fear?” Her eyes narrowed. "No. Not that. It's more a loneliness that once in a while I see as something pretty black. 

Not loneliness in the sense of the parties, pleasures, dates. 

I don't want these. But loneliness in the rink itself among the other professionals. Outside of the kids, Beatrice, little 

Jean, the others, I skate through a chilly company." "The youngsters worship you." "They're swell." "How is the manager treating you now?" "Jim Brennan? I don't understand him at all. He was openly against me until the show in February, really pushing me around, putting on the heat, but for the show he deluged me with publicity, recognition that counts in the rinks, and since then has been tame enough. But I don't trust him. Oh, he likes my work, my skating, I'm sure, but he doesn't like me." She shrugged. "What do I care?"

Charles thought, "But you do care, a lot, under that carapace the larruping La Tourette threw into my illiterate face. 

And the woman was right. You are a lonely, shy kid.” He said, “That's the way. Don't give a thought to clods like Brennan." "I have to work with, for him. I can not oppose him." "That's true," Charles said soberly. "But you are getting ahead in spite of him, in spite of all like him, Miss Head Instructress soon-to-be, who is to have skating shoes, mass production skating shoes, named for her. Humph! You've come a long way." 

Her face brightened. "Perhaps the promotion, perhaps the shoes. I am living for these."

And again he contrasted the steady fire of his daughter's will, ambition, with the cold ashes of whatever strength he had once possessed. "Shall we go?" he suggested. "The entertainment here seems definitely over. Both of them are asleep now.” 

Laurel looked doubtfully at Helen, whose head was on arms crossed on the table. "Don't we thank them, say good night?" “And disturb their idyllic slumbers probably irradiant with dreams of each other's great gifts? No. Let's steal away into the night. I hope the drizzle has stopped."

It had, and they began the tramp to Princeton under a sky of ragged clouds with slashed moon of bright, washed silver.

His offended sense of propriety, dormant, almost non-existent for a lifetime, and now only recognized in relation to his daughter, returned to plague him. Laurel and two men staring at a naked slut! That, if you please, was his sole social contribution to a shy, reserved girl.

So he spoke haltingly, suave manner lost, of his dismay. 

In early years, he told her, he knew many artists, musicians more than others. "Most of them were screwballs of one sort or another, but they were far from the musical comedy stock figures of exhibitionistic pseudo-Bohemians. That's why I distrust Slade. He flings everything, himself, his appetites, his wife, his work, into one's face. He shows off." 

Laurel said, "I don't think so at all. He simply is without reserve. It seems to me that he has a natural dignity." "Dignity!" "Dignity," she said firmly. "Of true kind, because he doesn't think of it. He has to maintain no stuffed-shirt conception of Harold Slade the artist." "But you can not associate with an outrageous fellow without being outraged in some way. A man can swallow him, but for a young woman- 

"But it's I who do, and you who don't!" She went on quietly, “And I know that the strip business was done for a purpose. 

"What purpose?" “He really wanted me to understand his painting.” "But the woman? At the stove attending to the damned steak, unclothed. She was your hostess-" "She was his model. It's a studio as much as a house. Perhaps more. There was no harm done to any one. You're making so much of really nothing at all. I've studied at life classes when I was very young. Cheap ones where we all chipped in for a model. I dropped out because I couldn't afford even the small cost. Now I use what I learned. The sketches for costumes in my shows. My own costumes.” "Well," he said doubtfully, "you make it seem all on the

up-and-up, but I still view that wheeled studio with distrust."

Laurel smiled in the dark. “In the days when I loved to read, enough to make time for reading, I read lots of plain wisdom. Of course I've forgotten most of it, but once in a while something pops back. I faintly remember a character in a long novel who was pretty bad, very bad in his youth, and who, when he finally reformed, became stricter than a Methodist missionary. A sort of loop-the-loop from a joyous Devil to a bitter God." "And I suppose you preferred the Devil." "No. Neither of them. I'd like a human being.” "You think I am bitter, strait-laced, narrow- "Heavens, no! You're just getting a little fussy. I remember also a jingle: "King David and King Solomon 

Led merry, merry lives, With many, many lady friends 

And many, many wives; 

But when old age crept over them- 

With many, many qualms, King Solomon wrote the Proverbs 

And King David wrote the Psalms." 

Charles flung his head back, sent a shout of laughter to the moon. "You win, Laurel, you skating philosopher. And Slade and his slave remain our friends."

They tramped on, silent. His ash stick slashed the air in triumph. "This," he said to himself, "is my great private victory. Another barrier falls. For the very first time, instead of resenting

my anxious interference in her life, she becomes impertinent, fresh, slyly mocking. And how I love it!"

After the ten o'clock bus carried his daughter away, Charles returned to his rooms in tranquilly singing mood. 

The night, however peculiar, even shocking, had given him much. A real barrier had certainly fallen. Laurel had been deliberately impudent! That girl, he felt, would not be that to one for whom she felt no degree of trust or liking. Charles was too cautious to use the term affection. Not yet. That might come in time. From the mail basket on his door he took the sheaf of circulars and letters he had not bothered to open while Laurel was there, and sat down in his armchair to skim and toss into the waste-basket the usual chaff. Circulars of music publishers, an announcement of a concert in the McCarter Theater, the movie guide, the announcement of the opening of a piano-shop in Princeton. One after another, ripped across, fell into the basket. Then a large square lavender envelope with large writing in purple ink, giving faint scent of lavender, surprised him. "Eh, what the devil's this?" He ripped open the heavy linen paper, and grinned at the engraved personal stationery that recalled the sharp generosity of an evening spent in some anguish three months before.

C. La T. 

Dear Professor-[Charles stopped to chuckle. Professor, indeed! And just like her Nebraskan impudence to add no surname.] I was so pleased one night last month to see your daughter leave the Elizabeth rink in her new coat. So all her brains are not in her feet! Really the coat-and I am surrounded

with coats!-gave me a thrill. Perhaps it was a latent sense of the dramatic I've never suspected in myself. That evening I'd seen 

Laurel skate so wondrously, in her royal blue and gold uniform, with the jaunty blue military cap and gold visor, her beautiful legs white and strong, and then later she was just a slip of a girl cloaked in rich gray fur! Oh, yes, maybe my dramatic sense is not buried so deeply.

But there is one thing dramatic I do possess and it is in the open. If the newspapers only knew what a sure bet they are missing for their theater sections: "The Theater, by our expert 

Camille La Tourette." It isn't, my dear professor, that I can judge the quality of a play, but it is that I can tell-I do tell-just about how long one will run. There I sit as the curtain goes up, lost, unknown, in the pontifical company of Anderson, Nathan, Watts, the ghost of Hammond, and all the rest, and I say, after an act, or perhaps two, “Hm. Into a second year, with three road companies," or, "Poor things, they may stagger on in that awful piffle for a month," or "If this runs a week it will be a greater miracle than Tobacco Road." 

Why am I deafening your academic ears with this rigamarole of the magic boards? 

Can't you even guess?

Simply because last night I attended an opening of an opus called Venus Sniffs at It. Now, the important thing is not that 

Venus sniffed, but that I did, for that means that the play will close pronto, as they are supposed to say in my home State. Yes, it will close. And it may come to pass that into my parasitic hands will fall a certain ensemble of warm yellow, buttercup yellow, my sweet, that would make your daughter as flower-like in drawing-room and street as she is in that bedlam. It's a twopiece tweed suit, yellow background, with faint blue plaid. The pleated skirt is cut on the bias-which won't mean a thing to you. 

Muddle over the idea, and then come in and see me. There's no hurry, the play still has a few nights of torture for the patrons of the cut-rate agencies. Perhaps the friends of author, producer, leading lady, and the rest add up to a week's attendance. Who knows? Not I. And certainly not you.

By the way, I've been skating quite a bit in New York. It's too bad Elizabeth seems far to me for the return at night. Otherwise

I'd see more of your shy and vernal Laurel. The next time I do go out to the hinterlands I'll say hello to her and accept her thanks for some marvelous roses I sent her Christmas Eve. Cordially,

MARGARET PETERS 

Charles grinned, leaned back in the chair, fumbled in his pocket for a cigarette. The deep tolling of the bell on Nassau 

Hall stole with further challenge into his mood.

WITH THE ROLLER FOLLIES on the road successful, the tour extended to California, the date of the finale in New York indefinitely postponed, it became apparent to every employee in the seven rinks of ROLLING AMERICA that Sue Holle's place had to be filled without further delay. The classes for advanced amateurs could be handled by the head instructresses of the individual rinks, but the professional classes could be resumed only by central control and instruction. Some one must skate in Sue Holle's shoes. Rink managers, floor managers, floor men, organists, skate boys, first aid crews, everybody, speculated with varying degrees of interest, and the general conclusion pointed to Laurel. A slim minority, persons friendly to her, told her so; a majority, indifferent and unfriendly, remained silent in her presence, but Laurel felt the resentment and she retreated still further into her aloofness, working hard, practising, studying, judging contests, handling more and more of Sue's work, and, she felt certain, skating more deeply every day into the approval of Mr. Stevens.

Save on her rides to other rinks and on the daily ride from her room to the rink, and on the nightly return, she saw no evidences of spring's gentle approach. The drab line between her room and the rink donned no gay garb of green and 157

yellow, and the long bus rides to other cities were usually taken with her nose in Rawson's figure skating articles, or in pamphlet or magazine of new instruction. Once or twice going through Princeton she looked out the window, but it was toward the rooms of her father, and the lovely greening campus across the street flashed past unnoticed. 

At the rink on the nights Charles came she found little time to spend beside him. This was the season of amateur dancing contests and Laurel was coaching in the center oblong reserved for practice and feature skating. She dismissed with an impatient shrug her father's complaint of her obvious loss of weight, but she knew that he was right, that she was very tired, and taut out of all proportion. "Any day," she kept on repeating to herself, "it will come. Head instructress of the chain." She could not miss. There it was, earned and reserved for her. The general manager was waiting for some reason of his own that his grunted comments would never reveal. Beatrice, Jean, lifted their fresh young voices in duet of excited assurance. Their skating goddess was at the top! And in spite of the caution established through long experience Laurel could not find reason for doubt. She was a better skater now than Sue. She knew it. 

Stevens must know it.

The one possible barrier was her lack of general popularity and this was, in a sense, superficial. The public that came to know her in work, made up of pupils, their parents, skating couples from other rinks who came to her for lessons, really seemed to like her after her early constraint was broken. 

They trusted her. Laurel could tell this. The dislike came from rink staffs antagonized by the manner they considered cold, but that was actually a result of concentration upon immediate problems. Oh, yes, she thought, just the old ques-

tion of her lack of immediate tact. Well, it couldn't be helped. She was the kind who must depend entirely on skates and work. She simply was not a politician. 

A soft April evening fell without vernal color around the outside of the huge concrete rink; only the air, and that in mere whisper, in fume-fouled fragrance, suggested the shy green loveliness of April beyond the business and manufacturing limits of the city, where maple trees were swelling balloons of spring yellow, and new grass rustled a million blades. Barnyard smells, meadow smells, rode the breeze into the hearts of the passersby. Spring again, in dance, in light sleep, in awakening, when the orchid sky of dawn grew pink, flushed red, burst into gold.... But now evening brought long shadows, and soon the darkness came. A tilted slice of moon silvered the countryside.

A mile from this hush and fragrance a queue of impatient persons, for the greater part youthful, waited outside Maizie's ticket booth for the opening of the window. The queue stretched down the shallow steps and halfway down the long block beyond the glare of the entrance and façade illumination.

Sniffing the fumed air, blinking against the wall of colored lights, Charles recalled the Princeton he had left on hour or so ago. Princeton bathed in moon silver and velvet shadow, scented with grass, flower, tree. "And in a few minutes, if this damned ticket booth ever opens, I'll be in a blaze of light, breathing dust, deafened by the grind of countless wheels. 

And I'll love it! Why? Because it is the home of my little girl.

She's still that to me. The little girl I never knew. The young 

Laurel who is so cautiously beginning to accept me, who can not yet call me 'Dad' or 'Father,' but who has gone long ago beyond that strange 'Mr. Easton.' There's an organ

in there that my Neville colleagues would call terrible, and yet I prefer it now to the authoritative musical splendor of the Princeton chapel organ. Booming, blaring, drumming, whistling, waltzing, encrusted in tinsel and chromium, vulgar and strident, it gives happiness daily, nightly, in accompaniment to hundreds and hundreds of young persons. The organ in Princeton that I'm permitted to play? Dignity. Inspiration to a handful. Religious solemnity to, probably, a considerable number, but to very many a musical background for thoughts of resumption of games, activities and general interests outside the imposing and beautiful windows and walls of the white cathedral. Whereas this organ inside the rink will be in the spirit of every skater, and the last chords of the last round of the night will be heard with regret by these youngsters who save their nickels and dimes and quarters for joyous nights of skating.'

Charles looked up from his thoughts to see the door man beckoning him. It was, he saw, a summons, and he left his place far down the queue. "Go ahead in, Mr. Easton. No use standin' out there." "Er-er, oh, thanks so much. It's very-" "O. K."

Charles went in, aware of a satisfaction that went beyond question of free admittance. Ben, the door man, had given him something more than this. For the first time it was as if the rink world seemed ready to recognize him as one who, without payment of public fee, belonged. True, it was only 

Ben who recognized him, and next time he would probably have to wait in line and pay, but still it was the beginning, the first step into Laurel's world. Again he thought of the two organs, and a grin twisted his lips. Another comparison.

A card of admission to some Princeton event of national, perhaps of world, significance. A great man visiting Princeton. Tickets at premium. But to him, a member of a neighboring faculty, a complimentary card of admission. The grin widened. Now, invited to this skating rink, he felt warmer, more grateful, than he had ever felt when accepted into the presence of the important and august.

From far down the dimly lighted expanse of polished floor Laurel waved to him and continued her talk with the organist. “Five minutes still, before the hordes invade, Alexandra. 

I wonder how my father beat the gun. Well, this is what 

I meant. I'm tired of Hungarian Dance Number Five for my free style routine, but I can't find anything in that tempo that I like better. The slow beginning, the whirlwind following, are exactly what my skates need, but-oh, you know how it is; I'm just tired of it.” 

The dark young Alexandra nodded. "Who could understand, if not I? I like my work, but Lord God how tired I get of grinding out this popular parade of hits!” "You don't grind it out. You really play. You don't know how the music has improved since you came. Why, Alexandra, you're the first rink organist I've ever met who plays before a session, and after too, for the sheer desire to play.' "It's then I really play. No question of timing for skates.”

Alexandra gathered up the long skirt of her black evening frock and mounted the steps behind the glittering wings of the console. Laurel gazed up at her frail form, wondering how it had the strength to play through the long sessions of matinées and nights. Why, Alexandra looked like a doll! 

Alexandra gazed down at Laurel's rich young body, wondering how it had the strength to skate through the long ses-

sions of matinées and nights, and lesson hours between. Why, Laurel looked like a mannequin, her legs so white against the edges of the royal blue uniform.

The blinding flood-lights were extinguished. 

Laurel nodded in general to the eight floor men lining up before the organ. Alice and June swept into line. The group stroking, swaying as one, skated to the center of the arena. The floor manager shot out to stand facing them while

Alexandra played the theme song of ROLLING AMERICA. The color wheels spilled pools from fairyland into the twilight ringed by the shadowed faces of the hundreds impatiently clanking their skates behind the rails. The last deep bar was accented, held, the color wheels were stopped and the batteries of flood-lights turned on. All-Skate flashed on the sign on the organ stage, and the night session roared in on the tide of skates. 

Stroking slowly, Laurel felt a hand clasp hers. "Hello, Beatrice." Her other hand was taken. "Hello, Jean."

They made the huge round of the rink several times and then separated in the center oblong for free style and dance step practice. Laurel became engaged with an awkward young giant who told her largely that he was determined to master the waltz. For ten cruel minutes she helped him on the early way toward his impossible goal. Couples released her. No man asked her to skate; it was June's and Alice's turn to stand at attention in the emptied center; so Laurel found a chair beside her father and dropped into it with a deep exhalation. “I'm tired to-night," she confessed. "Before I even begin to skate. I don't understand it.” "Overwork, of course."

This she dismissed with a frown and a brief, "Work is nothing new to me." She spoke of Eddie. "You never met

him. I think you two would get along. He's nice. All fire and wild bounce." "What makes you think I crave fire and bounce?" 

She smiled faintly. “I was just thinking out-loud." "Well, go on." "He writes once in a while. Of that marvelous tour. I-I wish I'd gone instead of playing safe with this job.” 

Charles looked at her closely. "That's rot." "Why is it?" "Because you are a success here. Definitely. Because you love your work." "Oh, Eddie will come back. Mr. Stevens will hire him again. He always does. Eddie has that recklessness that wins through. If Jim Brennan balks at taking him back in this rink, Mr. Stevens will grunt and send Eddie to another rink. 

He might even make Brennan take him here." 

Charles said quietly, "Then, too, there is the head instructress of all the rinks who must soon, you tell me, be appointed. The tour would have canceled that for you. Her wheels tapped nervously on the floor as she said slowly, "I don't know. One morning I wake up and think, 'I'll be appointed to-day,' and then when the sleep is all out of my eyes I think of the twisted road I've come-" “Not twisted, Laurel. You've run straight toward-” 

She made an impatient movement. "You don't know what goes on under the surface of this skating business." "I can guess, and you've told me quite a lot from time to time, but I also know this: that you have run straight, which doesn't mean that you have dully plodded along. Your skating course has been brilliant. As for the seamy side, well, it doesn't really concern you." "It concerns me in my salary. And for the first time since

I've been professionally in the rinks I'm beginning to think of skating at least part way in terms of money. Before, of course I've wanted money, I'm not crazy, but somehow the skating itself crowded everything else from my mind. The money, beyond what was necessary for food and sleeping and costumes and tooth-powder, was just something in the background. Now I'm beginning to wonder a little." "What suddenly caused this?" "Many weeks ago I had to pass on the qualifications of a girl applying for an instructress job. My say-so would decide whether or not she would be hired. She had been, until a comparatively short time before, a member of a crack dancing ensemble, the famous Rockettes of Radio City—” Laurel waited for Charles' nod and, receiving it, continued: "She put on weight. It just came and she couldn't stop it. They let her go. Remember, she was a gorgeous precision dancer, a 

Rockette! Suddenly she was nothing. Just a young woman trying desperately to get a start in a new life. From the bottom. Suppose something happens to me? I feel I must save some money. It can't be done in any important amount on my pay-have I ever told you what it is?" "No." "Twenty-five dollars a week, plus the private lessons I'm permitted to give. In all, about thirty-five. I pay five for my room, eat on about ten, bus fares are regular, and there are my clothes and costumes and repairs-this item is surprisingly expensive-to my skates. Skate wheels become flat, bearings wear out, oh, all sorts of things. And I subscribe to skating magazines, go to skating shows, patronize a dentist, and once in a long, long while an osteopath for my not quite yet aging joints. So it goes. And remember, I've had the thirty-five a week for only a short while. I began at eleven

dollars a week. That was four years ago. Now I'm doing well, but I want more money. "More than the joy of your "No!" "Ah, that's good to hear." 

work?" "But I can't forget Margaret Murphy, the ex-Rockette.” "Did you recommend her?" "Yes. She's working now in our Trenton rink."

The floor manager braked hard before them, nodded to 

Charles, said, "You're wanted in Brennan's office, Laurel." "Thanks, Bob." 

Charles watched his daughter skate down the floor behind the rail to the manager's office, thinking of the four years it had taken her to advance from eleven dollars to thirty-five. 

Four hard, clean years. The present salary was not so bad, considering the times, but he could understand her tension over the promotion she would surely win. And he knew her well enough to realize that while she talked of the added money, it was actually the position, authority, but above all, the opportunity that she would possess as Head Instructress of the chain. Opportunity to skate, to teach others to skate, in the wide range of her knowledge and ambition. To work without strings entangling her effort. Men like Brennan would have merely token authority over her. The petty hostilities she complained of would sneak away. She would wing her way, free of interference, in lovely butterfly costumes. How her eyes had sparkled-yes, her cool aloof eyeswhen she had mentioned teaching professionals. To teach the instructors, the instructresses of ROLLING AMERICA, those wizards and enchantresses on wheels! "It closed," a voice snapped above the clatter behind the rail.

Charles, lost in hope for Laurel, stared out to the arena as Couples ended and the flood-lights blazed down, harsh and white, for All-Skate. "I said it closed."

Vaguely he was aware of some one seated in the chair Laurel had left, and he turned a little, but deciding the fragment had not been intended for him, he returned his attention to the arena. Soon she would appear in the center space and take up the interminable practice of spins, arabesques, dance steps. Those nice kids, Beatrice and Jean, would come close to her, going through their own fine, young performances, asking her for criticism, suggestion. Why, they were out there now, in brief crimson satin costumes with silver mesh in their hair, rehearsing their duet in free style. “Nice kids," he thought. "And they like me because I'm Laurel's dad, not because I buy them candy and drinks." He stared, losing them beyond the rushing rings of the throng. Then he would catch glimpses of them through the skaters, and he smiled when he saw that they kept looking toward the railing obviously impatient for the coming of Laurel. "Dear God, give me patience. For the third time I'm telling you, Mr. Easton, that it closed." 

Charles jumped. Turning quickly, recognizing instantly 

Margaret Peters, he rose. "Sit down, Mr. Easton. I'll take your good manners as granted. Some one might slip into your chair. When wheels are on persons' shoes, no one ever does anything at reasonable pace. So! We meet. It closed; get out your check-book." "Oh, you mean that play, Venus Did Something or Other? 

Er-leading lady's spring costume. Yellow, I think you wrote." "That's right. Have you a pocket check-book with you?"

"My dear Miss Peters!" "What?" "Well, good Lord, aren't there any preliminaries? I mean, my daughter hasn't even seen the things." "Had she inspected the fur coat?" 

Charles shook his head. "Well, then. But if you are determined to get uppity about a La Tourette personalized, personalized I comment, bargain, then just forget the whole affair. Others will be eager to snap up the cream of-" 

"You mean lap up, don't you? One doesn't snap up cream. "My dear man, I've no time for idle repartee, even of the doubtless fine caliber of Neville's smoothest. Do you want the ensemble? Possibly I've been mistaken in your intentions toward your daughter. I thought you seized avidly any chance to give her really fine things that she could not, herself, buy." "It's just what I want. But there must be things to consider, colors, material, fit.” "And don't you realize that I've considered them? Even to the detail of Laurel's level shoulders. Look here-you teach music, piano?" "Yes." "Imagine yourself going through a bale of sheet music, good modern stuff I mean. Forgive my lacking terminology. 

Well, you find something. You run through it, recognize at once its charm. Then you remember a favorite pupil. Favorite because of superior accomplishment. Now would it be necessary to go into a huddle with him? Wouldn't you know the music would fit him, interest him, please him?” "Perhaps." 

Margaret smiled, said gently, "My analogy isn't very good.

Music and clothes! Now, my friend, I'll drop my too brittle manner. Let's get serious. Have you arrived yet at the time when you can say to your daughter, 'Let's run in to Camille 

La Tourette's and look over spring outfits"?" "No," he said sadly, "I haven't yet. She is rather fiercely independent." "So, you see, the surprise must still be your method. A fait accompli. "She may refuse." "Not after yielding about the coat." 

Charles drew a long breath. "You are right, no doubt. 

Well, then, Miss Peters, you just do the whole thing. I'll leave everything to your skill and nothing to my blundering. Send me a bill to-morrow, or when the skirmishes are over, the battle won." "You're a sweet man." She paused. "And now what do you think I am?" Without waiting for his reply she plunged on. "You think I'm a detestable supersaleswoman. A smart gal. 

A driver, a hustler. Out for the money. A gangster's moll in business-" "Please. What the devil is all this about?" “Oh, yes, you do. I'm a vulture-eyed harpy who—” "Your eyes are charming." "Listen," she hissed. "I liked you the minute you wandered into my shop, or whatever you want to call it—a den, a lair, probably. I liked you more when you told me your quest." 

Charles interrupted. "No one has ever really liked me; that is, not since my young years and you can easily guess how long ago those were.' "That's a lie. I liked you. Or am I a liar?” "Certainly not. You're just-shall I say it?”

She nodded. "You're just a little crazy." "Possibly I am. But let's get back to the detestable supersaleswoman. I want to slay her once and forever in your eyes. Mr. Easton, I do not use the method I've used with you and your confounded Laurel on others. Believe me,

I would not take the trouble. I'd rather go bankrupt. But I liked you, and I like you. I liked your daughter and I like her now. I know your problem and I want to help. That

I make a modest profit on my help is surely no indication of avarice. But if you think it is, I'll sell this spring dream in yellow for cost, I'll return to you the profit I made on the fur." "My dear girl, actually I'm horrified. I'm not speaking academically, or in hyperbole. It's the simple truth. I'm horrified. To be accused of considering you-the things you've said! I know how kind you were at Christmas. I know how kind you are now. Do you think I was born with the silver spoon and all that, and that I take generous service for granted? Believe me, Margaret Peters, I've found little softness in my life. And you have given me the most outstanding bit. I-so you like me. Well, I like you. Let's see, that is friendship, isn't it?” "It is." "Shall we drink on it?"

They went to the refreshment counter, drank Coca-Cola, returned to their chairs. She pointed to the skates that continued the graceful line of her silk legs. "Where are yours?" "I'm too old to learn," Charles said. "Boyhood? It would come back to you.' He shook his head. "I never learned-for many dull reasons."

"I'm going to look for Laurel, ask her to skate. It's time we really know each other. I don't see her." "She was called to the manager's office."

The colored lights were turned off; the white flood-lights rained down while the floor was swiftly cleared by the floor men. The routine line-up followed, with the floor manager facing the guards, and Alice, June, and Laurel. Then, instead of the electric sign's designation of the skating number to follow, instead of the organ's resumption of play, there was a prolonged silence during which the rink crew remained at rigid attention. A slight flurry occurred on the stage as Jim 

Brennan appeared at the microphone and requested silence that was given after restlessness of clattering skates died away. "Attention, please! An important announcement, the biggest announcement of the year. After the most careful consideration of benefit to you and to the personnel of our rinks, final decision has been reached in the appointment of a head instructress for our entire organization." Jim paused, cleared his throat.

Tense, filled with joy, Charles muttered, “Get on with it, you idiot! Don't drag out this prelude to her happiness." 

Margaret Peters chuckled as Charles' hand tightened on hers in a grip he was, she knew, quite unaware he had taken. "As you all realize, this position is of the utmost importance in our skating world. All instructors, instructresses in the rinks, are taught, given polish, by her. Thus our professionals as well as our amateurs depend on her for advanced instruction in free style and figure skating, in dancing. Ladies and gentlemen, representing Mr. Stevens, the general manager, I take great pleasure in introducing to you the new head instructress of ROLLING AMERICA-"

He paused in the sighed silence of the crowd. 

Charles muttered to Margaret, “She'll skate forward to bow. I wish I could see her face." “—a superb skater, Miss-Yolanda Juarez, of Detroit.” 

A fanfare on the organ crushed Charles' heart. Slowly his hand relaxed on Margaret's, withdrew. His eyes were dull as he saw a ravishing girl in white tulle ballet costume, with sequins in her hair, glide from a side entrance to the center of the rink. She was all sparkle and froth and champagne delight, and Charles' heart touched bottom. His stare slipped away from this white and silver triumph, backward to the slim figure in blue and gold uniform so rigidly upright in the wretchedness he suffered with her. He swallowed hard. "Poor little Laurel. Earnest, too severe and rigidly minded in her course for this God-damned merry-go-round. They want that beaming showy hummingbird. Laurel doesn't beam, perhaps she can't. Maybe she never learned how because I was a son-of-a-bitch who didn't stay to encourage her to breathe delight. Jesus! If this moment is fatherhood, you can have it, I don't want any of it." 

The applause was deafening, undying. And Yolanda Juarez bowed and bowed and waved and finally withdrew in a swift backward arabesque.

It was over. All-Skate was signaled. Routine was resumed. 

Charles sat, stiff, dry-lipped, dreary.

Margaret raged at her own inability to speak. She the voluble, the imperturbable. What were these persons to her?

Barely acquaintances. This fading man on his dull quest of love. This daughter in her dull evasion of it. Hitler the mad dog on his rampage. America forming an army. America augmenting a navy. America producing material of war.

Great Britain fighting on, holding back world ruin until that

American army and navy and production machine could be ready. Great vistas of world cataclysm. And on the personal side there were her own friends, prominent figures some of them, on stage and screen, in opera. “Yet here I sit, in a damned rink, silenced by the misery of a subordinate teacher of music. By his misery? No, God damn it, by my own!

I don't know how or why, there isn't a speck of logic in it, but I am almost as unhappy as Charles Easton. Poor young Laurel."

So they sat side by side, silent, staring blindly at the whirling ring of hundreds of skaters. "Hello." "Laurel, my dear." Charles was on his feet, adding nothing, knowing that on his face was written the whole story. He held her hand for a minute, gripping it tightly. Then he said, "Laurel. This is Margaret Peters, the Camille La Tourette of sewn labels, and, as she said a few minutes ago, it is time you know each other. Here, sit down for a little, I'm going for cigarettes.” He walked away. "You sent the roses." "You towed me around this rink months ago. Frankly, Miss 

Easton, it was really for your father that I gave the flowers.

I've seen him just twice in my life, counting to-night, and 

I love him. Maternally, you understand. Miss Easton, you've been crying, you're crying now." 

Laurel stood up to go, but a strong hand drew her down. "For the robbery? Laurel, quit that. You know it's just delay. 

A rotten disappointment, yes, but a finality of any sort, no. 

My God, girl, run your hands down those lovely powerful legs of yours, realize the control of your body, its sway, its tirelessness. You know you have skated your way into deserving the job that other girl was given. And you know,

you must know, rink politics. For some fool reason, known only to Stevens-you notice I'm leaving out the usually included God-you've been cheated. Can't you see why I'm sorrier for your father than for you? He can do nothing about it except suffer for you. But you! Laurel, you can skate and skate and skate. Here, until you find a better place, a more appreciative employer. What do you want most of all in life?" "Skating." 

Margaret's smooth shoulders rose and fell. "You have that. 

Now I won't tell you what I want, but I will tell you that I haven't it. Then, your father. What does he desire above all things? You can guess and know you are right with your first guess. But he hasn't it, and, it seems clear, he doesn't expect to win it. So, youngster, skate on, bite your pride back, don't steal away into your dressing-room to cry. You're lucky. You're doing what you want to do, and you are doing it well. Just because a thick-headed boss-oh, let's skate. Feel up to it?"

Stroking fast into the outer ring, hand in hand in rink fashion, they began to talk of calm and healthful nothing. On the third round Margaret flashed a look toward the chairs they had left. Charles was there. She smiled at him and, freeing her hand, significantly put her arm around Laurel's waist. 

Then when they were half down the rink she said crisply: "Your father's a kind man, but he's also an idiot. He thinks you are heart-broken. Laugh at him next time round. Now what am I doing wrong, is it my hip, or my legs, or all of me?" "It's your timing with the music more than anything," 

Laurel said.

"OF COURSE they are lovely. I've never had such things on me before, but they're a waste of my father's money. You know, Miss Peters, and he knows, that I practically live in the rinks. I return to a bedroom around midnight. So this spring decoration will be wasted." "I'm being extraordinarily, no, miraculously patient, Laurel; my fingers are driven into my palms, I'm chewing my tongue. Happiness, in this tarnished life, is never wasted.

Now we'll erase you completely from the picture. There's just your father. On your next night off, run down to Princeton in this ensemble. You'll see something in his face that's just as strange to it, as you say this material is to your back. 

Now let's get on with this fitting you really don't need.” 

This, greatly deleted, Laurel related to her father as they sat at a window table in the little French restaurant around the corner from Charles' apartment, two weeks after the appointment of Yolanda Juarez. Her name, inevitably sliding into their thoughts, was given detailed expression. "Feeling any better about things?" Charles asked. "At times." 

He nodded. "I understand. How is she to work with?" "To work under. All right." "Any idea why she was appointed?" 174

"She's a one-woman wave of popularity. Every one likes her from the moment she says, 'Hello.' And she's a flashy skater." Laurel's eyes grew dark. "Flashy? In what sense?" "A wonderful appearance." "Well, isn't that all there is? I mean professional skating depends on appearance." 

Laurel said grimly, "You may be right. At least you are partly so. But there's more in it than that. She skated at 

Elizabeth last night, in solo free style and with a partner in the dance steps. Her free style is breath-taking, but her technique is weak. Her dancing is graceful and charming, but it is wrong. Her body lean and leg position, among other things, are wrong. You see, there is a blur under the flash of everything she does." "I have had several pupils like that. Marvelously adroit. 

Clever as the devil. Their parents-not musicians-actually think they are prodigies, ready for Carnegie Hall. But to the professional ear?-b-r-r-r. They're hollow." "Miss Juarez isn't to be marked quite so strongly. She really can skate. But she is a performer, not a teacher. Her ears will be deafened with applause, yes, yells, when she gives exhibitions and she'll deserve this, but when, on some Sunday evening, in a half-dark rink, where there is no gay and enthusiastic crowd, she faces a tense group of ROLLING 

AMERICA professionals, instructresses, Alice, June, Hilda,

Margaret, the other girls, myself, the floor men who dance and give exhibitions, then, instead of cheers, I think she'll hear mutters, catch questioning side remarks. She will not be able to fool those people with flash and glitter. They need technical instruction. That's why they go long distances for the class, give up their free time to it. She'll give them a

dazzling smile, blind a few fools with it, but the majority will resent. Sue Holle had lost speed, and a lot of flash, but she could still teach the foundations. And without foundation?" Laurel's shoulders under the smart yellow tweed with the faint blue plaid shrugged. "I hope you are right.” "Why?" "Miss Juarez may not last." "But she will. She'll draw the crowds, fascinate them.

That's money in the till. Inner disturbance, disappointment in the rinks, among the professionals, well, that's just another headache in the business. No. If she leaves, it will be for the show business in some form. Night club, or revue. 

And if she does go, old Hank will reach wildly out for some other head instructress. He's forgotten me, except as a girl to grunt at when I come under his eye." "Do you think of quitting this outfit, going to another?" "No. I couldn't do any better for myself, now. And you mustn't think I'm knocked down. The slap came two weeks ago, and it's over and done with. I've put the idea of Laurel 

Easton skating shoes on the shelf. And that's that." "Have Yolanda Juarez shoes come out yet?" 

Laurel shook her head. "They won't for some time. Money.

It costs quite a lot to introduce a new label. The Sue Holle shoe is firmly established. The shoe company, before making a change must make certain that Yolanda Juarez is likely to stay on the job." "How does she treat you? Superciliously?" "Heavens, no! I like her. She almost dazzles me along with the gapers." Laurel smiled wryly. “She asked me to help her with-oh, with certain technical details.” 

Charles stared. "You mean she, the new head instructress

of all the rinks, the whole damned seven of them, is being taught by you?" "Not exactly taught. Coached a bit. I respect her for asking. She's not petty." 

Charles flung his hands in the air. "I give up. Yes, it's a mad world." 

Laurel laughed. "Everywhere, except at Princeton." "Madness is stealing in here too; it's not yet in the cloisters, but the outer precincts are threatened. A one-man invasion." "Oh-Slade?" 

Charles nodded. "How is he?" "Booming." "Isn't it time for his caravan to start going? In some direction?" "He's doing a couple of portraits of townspeople. One of the butcher's wife, in payment, I believe, for the innumerable oxen consumed this winter by him and by his somnolent wife." "I like him. Besides, I think he is good for you." 

Secretly delighted that Laurel considered anything good or bad for him Charles said, “And she?” "She doesn't really exist for me." 

“Come," he said, “we've finished. I've something for you.' "Where?" "Anywhere. Here, or in my rooms, or in the street, or in your rink. You probably won't like it, but we'll see.”

In the street he began to hum very softly, ignoring her demand that his conception of a riddle, however weak, be solved at once. "What is it? And how can you have it for me anywhere. On street, in rink. All that foolish talk."

Charles continued to hum, excited, hoping, trying to kill that hope lest its unfulfilment bring black disappointment. 

Oh, she must like the gift! But there was danger in intimate gifts. Clothes? Ah, they could be bought, and if wisely selected, must please. But this—

In his living-room he faced her, nervous, wondering at the intensity of his feeling, lost for the time in the sudden flight of his calmness and urbanity. He thought, "God damn it, I'm like a gangling schoolboy! Well, here goes!" He pulled out a chair, almost pushed her into it. 

Her smile was uncertain, a little cold, but amused too. “Laurel, for once in my life, and for the last time in my life I sincerely hope, I wish I could speak with the-oh, let's say the gusto of Harold Slade. Then I'd orate, 'Laurel, my girl, I've painted you in music, caught you in lyric bars and enmeshed you forever in the strings of a harp.' But I'm a plain fellow, an old bum from the sidewalks and cellars of New York, and what I've done can be simply told.” He stopped, obviously floundering. "Then tell it simply," Laurel said crisply. "Back in the restaurant-thanks so much for the good dinner-you said you could present this gift in any place, yet here you are at home and unable to speak coherently. It just doesn't make sense to me.” "Several times you've complained that you are tired, of your exhibition music, Hungarian Dance Number Five, and can't seem to find another that inspires you and, of terrific importance, one that is suited to the peculiar requirements of a skating rink, so I've written a piece that describes you as you skate-that is, of course, it does in my eyes and ears. Perhaps it will be music you can use. We shall listen, and you will know."

"Hum it," she said. "I did, in the street, but so quietly that you could not hear it. Humming it would be harsh treatment. I've composed nothing for many years." "The piano," she said, pointing quickly to the corner of the room. "Too insipid for the job this especial bit of music must do. Come." "Where to now?" "Out."

They entered the campus through the grillwork on Nassau 

Street. Around them in the early night, dormitories waved gold windows to a star-filled sky. Nassau Hall, enameled with history, glowed in its solid setting, the university library was sleepily lighted, and presently, looming in height and bulk, the huge Princeton chapel blocked out the northern sky. "We'll go in the side door," Charles said. "I've a key.

You know this edifice is called a chapel. Physically, it is no such thing. It's a cathedral, vast, filled with hush and color from magnificent stained glass. The altar is medieval-I sound like a guide. But I'm preparing you for the emptiness of this hour inside that door."

They were in the chilly dark entombed in glass and stone. 

Charles told her to wait. He moved away, found a switch and the chancel was a sparkling area of light. Calling to her, telling her to sit in a choir stall opposite the organ, he seated himself at the console. For a long minute he stared down and across the marble floor at his daughter. 

Then he said in a tone that sounded low because of the dark spaces beyond the chancel, "Pride is, I think, one of the clearest characteristics of your skating, and strangely it is most apparent when you stand still, out in the arena, be-

fore going into motion. You are then erect, composed, yet on edge, your legs are placed with a studied precision, and your head is high, attentive to your own challenge. Listen.”

Like a bugle sounded the clear high notes of the introduction. "Now, Laurel, comes your fast beginning. You skaters figure it as slow beginning, but that's because of the whirlwinds to come. As a matter of fact, it is fast from the first turn of your wheels."

Again the organ, but this time breathing to the depth of its great lungs under the flight of song. Charles played without attention to the playing, his imagination engaged exclusively with: There she flew down the center of the floor, bending, swaying, jumping, turning gracefully in the air, landing into rolling speed. Now she was slowing, going into spiral arabesques that led her into the center of the arena to stand erect for an instant. Charles thought, “I see her. Does she see herself?"

He called out, “Now, Laurel, the flood-lights are extinguished, a spotlight bathes you in white and the second half of your act begins. The knife of light, following you, slices the dark rink, and you whirl on, turns, jumps, spins, cartwheels, and-" He stopped talking, wryly aware that in the rich crescendo of this second half, the pinnacle of the finale, no word could be heard. He played on in the shuddering reaches of the cathedral, a dwarfed figure at the console, looking at his daughter in the choir stall opposite. 

Two persons in a music-room of light with walls of dusk and darkness.

The music ended. Charles could see her skate out in the applause to make a bow, to retreat finally in swift arabesque. "Well?" he asked with a sharpness that amazed him.

She gave no answer. The gold sparkle of the chancel light flooded her face, but made it expressionless, unreal. 

Charles laughed. "I'd better stick to my teaching, Laurel.

I know its cloisters so thoroughly. The effrontery of a parent!" He ripped into halves the penciled score he had not glanced at during the playing. "What are you doing?” she cried. “Stop it, Dad!" 

His hands, placed for the second rip, were arrested. Dad!

She'd called him that. And from her heart, in a split-second of excitement, not from some decision derived from study or sense of duty. The halves of the score drifted to the floor, two ignored patches of paper whiter than the marble, but seeming no whiter than the faces of Laurel and Charles. "You've interrupted my skating with your demand for my applause. Your music, once heard, is in my head for a year's performances." "You-you mean you can use it?" "It's good. Oh, I can't use words lightly, like Mr. Slade. 

If I could I'd be bellowing, 'Perfect,' 'Colossal,' all the rest, with all stops out. So I say it is good, but I mean the things, almost, that Mr. Slade would say. "You don't use words lightly? Sure of that, Laurel?" "Yes." "You called out, 'Stop it, Dad.' 

Even in the gold light he saw her face flush dark as she said gently, "When you found me in my rink, when later you bought me the dinner in Trenton, told me who you were, it meant nothing to me. I mean, emotionally. When you persisted in coming to see me in the rink, when you bought me the wonderful coat, gratitude made me come to see you. When you were at my side in Elizabeth the night of Yolanda Juarez and I saw how unhappy you were

for me, again I was grateful. Gratitude? Yes. And a sort of strong friendship. Now you've written this music. That, that reaches into the past, crosses a wide dark valley. Because this music is the best of you, and it will support the best of me. So-"

She was out of the choir stall in one movement, and then she was walking across the floor, mounting the shallow steps of the organ. 

Stiff, unbelieving, Charles felt her arm go around his shoulder. "You win, Dad, or lose. Anyway, you have a rambunctious daughter on your horizon." “Laurel dear, I-I promise I shall not get in your way."

He rose, stood bent over in the narrow space, and 

Laurel looked at him closely. Then she swiftly kissed him, and, jumping down to the floor, picked up the torn score, stood waiting. "Go out," he said. "I'll turn out the lights and follow."

Shaken by happiness, for, he told himself, the first time in his life, he swallowed hard in a sort of sickened reaction to the strain and hunger of months. "That little slip of a thing is my child. Packed with courage. Look at her back and shoulders framed in that shadowed arch, and the cant of her head. Pride! In her work! And you, Easton, old ghost of a drunken piano thumper in low bar, low dance-hall, yes, in a whore house or two, you, even you, to-night have a sort of incredible pride in a score of music your daughter takes for her own." 

He switched out the lights, stumbled down the chancel step, and moved along the rail toward the door where Laurel was limned against an outer night a shade lighter than the night of cathedral interior.

PART TWO 

10 DR. SAUNDERS looked quizzically at Charles, stuffed his pipe, tamped it, and applied the match. When it was going brightly he put it on the taboret, where it promptly went out. "I think," said Dr. Saunders, "that you are crazy." 

Charles grinned. "You don't mince words." He looked around the familiar study so rich in the proper credentials of music: piano, four metal music-stands for quartet nights, library of music, marble busts, of Beethoven on the mantel, of Mozart on a pedestal beside the open window through 183

which at the moment the drowsy evening sounds of a late 

June came like a securing caress. Campus security! A sudden misgiving touched Charles with icy unseasonal finger. Beneath the quizzical attitude of this faculty authority was deep disapproval. No doubt of that.

Old Saunders had always-and quite justly, Charles thought-suspected the fitness of this instructor who for a decade had neither sought nor even shown interest in promotion. That was a poor attitude in any instructor in the realm of music, where enthusiasm was of such importance. 

Enthusiasm, dream, hope, tenderness, all based on firm technique. Charles knew that he gave technique only. What, then, he wondered, looking at the pontifical Dr. Saunders, had saved him? Technical values, and the inexplicable response from students. Dr. Saunders was a just superior who, unable to like a man content to cling year after year to a static berth, yet would not get rid of him as long as his teaching remained up to standard. "Crazy men, Dr. Saunders, never think they are that way.

I think I'm doing one of the few genuinely wise things I've ever done."

The pipe was lifted again, attended, and this time smoked. 

The words emitted between puffs were crisp. "Neville men, your colleagues, are going to various places of musical study this summer. Blaine, Kalinof, Shubinsky, Curtis. They will study, teach, compose. And over at Princeton my friend

Sampson of geology is to spend this two months' summer vacation digging with students in Arizona, Carlton of history is to travel in the Northwest, following historic American trails, Blake of astronomy tells me he is going to Mt. Wilson Observatory in California, Leslie of genetic psychology goes from zoo to zoo, museum to museum, through

July and August, and even the football coach, I hear, is to attend athletic conferences somewhere." The blue eyes grew colder, the voice a little sharp. "These men, and many others of both faculties, can not be dismissed as being merely eager to enjoy busmen's holidays. No, Easton, far from that. They are continuing their work, their spirit. They exemplify the integrities of scholastic engagement. And you, in a land where music festivals will flourish from the Berkshires to 

California, are going to play six hours a day, mornings, afternoons, nights, you tell me, a music machine-I will not call it an organ-in a roller-skating rink. Cheap popular songs for skaters! You are not only turning your back on your music, you are injuring it!" "I'll need only a monkey in a red cap, Dr. Saunders, to be an organ-grinder. Why don't you say it? I'd see the point."

Dr. Saunders' eyes warmed into their habitual kindness. "There's no need for acid now, either in you, or in me. Certainly your vacation is your own affair. I'm just mildly shocked, a little disappointed." "I could be hypocritical, Doctor. Charles Easton is going to study the effect of loud, louder, loudest music upon the rhythmic sensibilities of American youth on spinning wheels, with the ultimate objective of a thesis." 

Dr. Saunders snorted. “But you are not a hypocrite.”

There was a long silence in the study. Charles reached deep into himself for a decision. Should he break now the aloofness, the established "outsidedness" sufficiently to explain to this decent superior the wisdom of his plan for the summer? If he should do this, surely Dr. Saunders would understand the matter clearly, would be armed, so to speak, against any currents of antagonism or mockery that might

circulate at Neville when it reopened in the autumn. While the great roller-skating rink at Seaside City, that raucous summer resort fifty miles east of Princeton, was not likely to attract faculty members, it was quite within probability that music students might drift in, and see that cool urbane instructor Easton banging out Chattanooga Choo Choo or

Pop-eye, the Sailor Man or some other item similarly remote from atelier precincts. 

And he was friendless at Neville. A berth holder for ten years. This thing might be the last straw, and so forth, and so forth, and he would find himself at fifty-six unemployed, possessing a thousand-dollar bond, a few hundred dollars in the bank, and adequate clothing. Humph! 

He looked out the window into the perfect June night.
