Probably not a cloud in the sky. Just silent night on the beautiful enclosure. Here, within ten minutes' walk of this study, were his rooms, his snug life. He must not subject his continuance in this security even to the slight risk of changing mild faculty disapproval into blunter opposition. 

No. He must plunge. "I think I want to tell you, very briefly, a few things about my life that will explain this—this hegira into musical limbo." "You don't have to, Easton. There is no reason to make this hour confessional. Naturally my duty to Neville, faculty and pupils, makes it imperative that I have confidence in the teachers. This summer your plan is, as I said, slightly shocking to me, simply because I think it may impair your future ability. I'm not talking from the ivory tower. Not a bit. But the rink playing is, physically, mentally, even emotionally, bad for your professional, tutorial ear. But I assume you have a reason beyond inexplicable whim."

"I have a reason. 

"Then I accept that, of course, as truth." "You've always been kind, Dr. Saunders, to a queer fish." "You've performed your duties. It isn't a matter of kindness. Frankly though, I've regretted your lack of ambition, enthusiasm. "I'd like to tell you my reason for this summer plan. 

I think you ought to know." "Very well, let's have it." "In capsule dose, Doctor." Charles' mouth tightened. "My wife died nearly twenty-four years ago. There was a sixmonth-old child. A daughter. I saw her last fall for the first time since her mother's funeral. I found her a professional skater, an instructress in a chain of roller-skating rinks called ROLLING AMERICA. She is doing well at the Elizabeth rink." "Hm. On the faculty of this-shall I say?-college of skaters." 

Charles gave a twisted smile. "That's right. But she is the opposite of me, in her work. She possesses the qualities which, as you said a moment ago, I lack. Ambition, enthusiasm. I mustn't bore you with details. Well, I want to work for a while with my daughter. I applied a week or so ago for the summer position and was accepted at once." "Naturally," Dr. Saunders said gravely. "I should be humiliated for Neville musical reputation to hear otherwise." "It's a queer world Laurel is in. I don't like it very much." "What a lovely name." "She is lovely too, Dr. Saunders, in my eyes.' "But you said she teaches at Elizabeth. You are to play at Seaside City."

"She is to be transferred to the coast resort to-morrow, the first of July. These transfers are common in the company. 

Seaside City will be extremely busy in the season now opening. "You say, Easton, that you don't like your daughter's world very much. Do you like it at all?” 

Charles hesitated. Then he said without conviction, "To some extent. There's a happiness, a kind of singing happiness in these well-run rinks. Actual singing too, when girls and boys skate fast in trios and foursomes. There's quite a thrill in it, Doctor, youth with cynicism scraped off, or not yet coating young minds. They rush along on their wheels, singing, laughing. I like that. And I like the attitude of instructors and instructresses toward their work, a kind of madness of enthusiasm." Charles grinned. "That sounds queer, doesn't it, coming from me?” "It does, I regret to say. What don't you like about Miss 

Easton's environment?" "General effect of truncated education; a good-natured commonness. Possibly my jaundiced eye exaggerates this. 

I'd like something better for Laurel." "Why don't you bring her to Princeton? To live with you, to make new friends." 

Charles flung up his hands. "Leave her rink! Would you leave your music world for any other?" "I see your point." Dr. Saunders laid his charred pipe on the charred taboret. "Hm. She won't come to you, so-Mahomet and the mountain, eh? Well, why not? I'm glad you told me about her, because while what I said about a man's vacation being his own affair, still many things are more or less expected of Neville faculty members beyond their strict curricular duties, and there may be disagreeable

echoes from your incredibly, horribly noisy summer. I can still them as far as any definite criticism brought to me." "That's why I told you, sir, about Laurel." 

Dr. Saunders nodded, thinking, "How his dead eyes come to life when he speaks that name! Strange chap. Music never does that to him. I wonder if it ever did, in the very beginning." "So you understand?" "Yes." 

Charles rose. "Thanks. I'm going to my rooms to pack.” 

They stood facing each other. "Is your daughter excited about it?” "She doesn't know. I'll be at the console when she arrives at the Seaside City arena to-morrow night. That will be a nervous moment for me.' 

"Why haven't you told her-if I may ask?" "I-I think she'd try to stop me. She doesn't think a father necessary to her-after twenty-four years without one." "Do you think one is?" "No. But a daughter is necessary to me." "Then go to her, Easton. Good luck to both of you. Andby the way, what's a popular skating tune now?” "Chattanooga Choo Choo," Charles said grimly. 

Dr. Saunders chuckled. "Well, some day when you are engaged upon that opus, just give a thought to that gentleman on the mantel.” "I'll give a thought to you, sir, to your understanding of my summer's necessity, but even more to your forbearance with me during a decade." "Nonsense. You are a good music teacher. Your indifference to music has, miraculously, it seems to me, never been revealed to your pupils. Good-by, Easton."

"Good-by, Dr. Saunders. And thanks. A pleasant summer vacation to you.' He was out in the plush of the small, almost deserted campus.

In his rooms he wandered around like a dispossessed cat. 

It had been such a long time since he had left them for more than a day or two. During Christmas, Easter holidays, summer vacations, the times when men, whole families, left Princeton in droves headed to every point on the compass, he had remained in his quarters, content to let life roll by until the academic population returned for work. Sitting on the high window-seat reading detective stories. A sluggish, comfortable cat. And now the cat was on the prowl. He grinned at the flaw in the figure. Tomcat on the tiles? Not he. He was just a man grown respectable, a parent called back to unfamiliar duty and pleasure. 

Now as he walked slowly up and down he realized more clearly than ever before how removed from conventional life he had been, and in many respects still was. There was the matter of leaving the premises. How many other men could leave a ten years' domicile simply by packing a trunk and suitcase and turning a key in a door? If he forgot to pull down the shades-why must they be pulled down?—the janitor of the building would attend to them. There was literally nothing to do. No papers to sort, books to pack, possessions to be stored. The turning of the key? Even that could be forgotten with safety. In Princeton as many doors were left unlocked as there were locked. "Even you," he was saying to Harold an hour later, “itinerant rebel that you are, can not leave a place with as little effort as this. You have to get your land cruiser in working

order after such a long time in drydock. You have to plan your next stop-" "I don't have to look forward to playing a God-damned juke-box two sessions a day- "Three at Seaside City. Summer rush of a resort. There's a morning session at ten.” "Jesus!" "Meaning?" "That you are crazy. "9 "The first man to call me that to-night changed his mind a little, I think. Is going closer to Laurel madness?” "Of course not. But why the slavery. You must have saved some money in ten years of this cushy, dull job. You could take rooms in Seaside City, near the rink, spend your days on the beach, and watch your daughter." "I said closer to my daughter. Ever hear of the bond of shared work?"

The hoarse voice of the painter rumbled out over four tremulous chins. "Yes. As a matter of fact, I think you are wise. Many cranks are. Look at me. Painting and my everblooming rose. Helen's no orchid. She's a rose in full bloom. 

Caught, arrested in my vision, as the flower would be caught, arrested on my canvas. Well, there you are. In conventional judgment, I'm cracked, but I've whipped the savage sorrow of life with two allies, my painting and my woman. You're cracked too, urbane as you are, and I've gathered that you've never even tried to whip life-until a few months ago. Well, you've only one ally, a girl. No, you aren't as well armed as I am. One ally only and that one not exactly warm to the alliance. But the point is that you've begun, however late, to fight for something. I hope you win, but I don't think you will."

"Why not?" "There's no goal. Parenthood, -dom, or whatever it is, is a losing game when you start in at the very point when parents say a sad farewell, however fond, to their offspring. 

You've lost what must be, are, the joys. Tucking a kid into bed, picking it up when it's hurt, sitting by its bed when it is afraid of the dark. Buying toys for it. Clothing it. Saving money at personal sacrifice to send it to college. All the rest of it. Feeling it turn to you for love, comfort, protection, advice. That's lost, Easton, blown away on the cold wind of your past decisions." 

Stung, Charles snarled. “And you? A child toddling at your knee?" "Helen. She's my child. Just as her charm is held unbruised in my imagination, her childishness is held in the cement of fact. She's not, er-well, quite-" He stopped. "You mean, actually?" 

Harold nodded. "Society would do with her what it calls institutionalize. She could not earn a living. She must not bear a child. She has no place in life except"-the small piglike eyes glittered-"except the perfect place I give her. A tiny garden in a man's heart. Jesus, I'm lucky! Jesus, so is she!"

Charles stared at the gross form of his visitor. "Know any one in Seaside City?" Harold asked abruptly. "Not a soul, but the peculiar thing is that I feel as though I do. The answer to that is easy enough. The pattern of rinks in ROLLING AMERICA is so close to exact. In Elizabeth I'm familiar with a building, a personnel, a routine. At Seaside 

City I shall find a replica. The floor manager, the door man, the ticket seller, the instructors, will be strangers only for a few days, because they will be figures going through ex-

actly the same movements as do the ones I'm come to know more than a little. I've never been a recluse, you know; that is, on the surface of things. I can mix superficially without effort-when I care to. Then there's Laurel." "Yes. Decidedly there's Laurel. And Helen and I." "What!" "In a week or so. Is Seaside City your private summer resort?" "Say, Slade, that's grand." "I knew you'd be overwhelmed with joy. I'm going to attack again the artist's great challenge, the sea. Salt air is fine for Helen, and she loves to loll on the sand. That, by the way, is what your Laurel needs. She's too thin, too pale, too tired." "I'm afraid I won't be able to anchor her on a beach. A little in the mornings, maybe; I don't think she skates the morning sessions, but she may be giving private lessons. Of course, she has Mondays free, but whether she'll rest-" 

Charles sighed.

Their talk was desultory, comfortable, for an hour. Then 

Harold, creaking, stretching, got to his feet. "S'long, Easton. Tell Laurel I'll be in a spectator's seat again before long. How in blazes can I watch her skate and listen to you play at the same time? I never have found out how to divide attention." of 

Charles hooted. "I don't think there will be much question your focused interest. You'll be seeing an artist work and hearing-God help you-a hack on a treadmill." "Humph!" 

Charles stood at the landing, felt the old stairs shake under the tread of behemoth.

Then he dragged a trunk from a closet and began to pack,

very neatly, a summer wardrobe, rather expensive, quiet in tone, but cheerful enough for a man of fifty-six. .

At about the time Charles was packing, Margaret Peters was, to judge from Laurel's cold expression, giving advice vaguely resented, and definitely not wanted by the younger woman. Hand in hand they were stroking along in the inner band of All-Skate. "But, Laurel, Yolanda Juarez is all fluff and powder and temperament and volatile spirits. "I can't change my character." "Who says anything about character change? I'm merely suggesting more care for yourself. More careful powdering. 

Heavier lipstick. You are when you smile, rather close to beautiful-that is, early in the sessions when your make-up is still fresh. You don't believe that? Why, right now you are actually blushing, and your face is, yes, lovely. But the color will ebb, and your skin will be taut, pale, and, I'm sorry to say, a bit shiny. That's where powder comes in.” "I depend on my skating," Laurel said coldly. "Don't be idiotic. Pons depends on her singing, Irina

Something-or-other from the Monte Carlo Ballet on her dancing. But they are never caught before their paying public without make-up. Look here, you were telling me of the young Rockette. Why was she fired? She could dance as well as ever. It was because she looked fat. Appearance! 

Don't you think you must consider yours? You're a pretty girl-" "I've no looks. None. Even my grandmother who brought me up never suggested that I had. Boys, later men, never have bothered much about me and I'm glad they haven't. I

can't clutter up my career. Look here, Miss Peters, I've lived a hard life, come up the hard way. I'm not interested in the butterfly way. Almost twenty-four, I've never had a real boy friend. Oh, of course there have been skating partners who surged toward me now and then, but none who counted. 

Maybe, Eddie, a little. I'm plain, and that's all there is to it." "You are attractive, and if you'd make the most of it, you'd be lovely." "I can't skate and be a mannequin at the same time.' "Yolanda does." "She doesn't skate!" 

Margaret laughed. "Her fans think she does." "I have no fans." 

"There are a few. Your father, Beatrice, Jean, I—and the youngsters in your shows. There could be more." "Cosmetic fans?" "If you want to call them that. I'm amazed, Laurel, at your stubbornness. You aren't stupid. You're just wilful. My

God, you're in the show business, and you know what appearance means. That's evident in the care you lavish on your striking costumes, your always whitened skating shoes, the ornaments in your hair. But you won't trouble with your eyes, your mouth, and—you will skate with a shiny face."

Margaret felt her hand released with almost flung violence and, with regret, skated on alone. "The blasted little fool! 

Why do I bother? Of course I know why. It's because she's a grand kid. She's worth advice, support. There is in her the grim integrity that is in every worthwhile public figure in large or small circle. She has that integrity, tempered steel, but not sense enough to know how polish will, of itself, cut

a path." So thinking, muttering to herself, Margaret skated along in the crowded All-Skate number. 

A rush of wind at her side, her hand reclasped. "This what you mean, Miss Peters?"

Margaret turned, skating sideways, examining carefully the grave, sweet face of the girl. The lips were defined, vivid, blood-red, a suggestion of color surged under the pallor of the cheeks, the forehead was smooth, cooled by powder. "That is exactly what I mean. But it won't be worth a thing if you don't, an hour or so from now, skid into your dressing-room for repair. Laurel, you are a dream! Yolanda? 

Phooey on Yolanda!" 

They skated on. "Have you seen much of your father lately?” "No. Oh, he's been over once a week, but I gather that his work is holding him rather close to Neville in the final weeks of the term. Once in a while his new friend comes with him. 

You've never met Slade, have you?" 

Margaret shook her head. "Well, he's something. A kind of nice horror. I like him. I don't suppose I'll ever see him again. Trailer stuff.” "What's your father doing this summer?" "He hasn't said." "And you don't ask?" "Well, I take it for granted that he'll spend his vacation in Princeton. He always does." "Seaside City's just another point in a triangle. It's not much farther from Princeton than Elizabeth is. You'll see him planted in a corresponding seat.” "Probably. He's written music for me; just what I need and was afraid I'd never get.

The organ sounded the end of All-Skate. The floor was cleared. The floor crew took their formation. Trios was signaled. The blaze from the ceiling was dimmed, the color wheels revolved.

Laurel's last night at the rink came to its ending, and at midnight she left without regret. Excepting Margaret's warm, richly smiled good-by and promise of coming to SEA- SIDE CITY ARENA, and a parting with a tearful Jean, and a choked-up Beatrice, with more promises, there had been little fuss. Sitting in the bus, laden with wrapped costumes, Laurel thought of this, and of what a contrast a Yolanda 

Juarez departure would have been. A ballerina in flight! 

That's what that champagne blonde would have made of it.

Jim Brennan would fuss around her, the floor men would circle and kid her, the few patrons who lingered at the entrance waiting for cars, would stare at her and point, and many would wish her luck. Laurel shrugged, and her thought, leaving rink and lurching bus, flashed to the room she was to sleep in for the last time. There, before going to bed, she would pack her trunk. And to-morrow she would mend, press costumes, study Perry Rawson, and rest in the afternoon. Then the train, taking a room at a boardinghouse, a routine reporting to Manager John Hedges at the SEASIDE CITY ARENA just before the night session, ten minutes in the new dressing-room, and a swift, unannounced glide onto the floor for the summer's work. 

She left the bus, walked quickly to Mrs. Green's door. Bed. 

Bed. But a figure sprang suddenly from the floor of the shadowed stoop and Laurel, recognizing Eddie, forgot sleepiness. "Eddie! In uniform! How, when, why? I'm so glad to see you."

"Let's go inside. For once in my life I think I'm tired. Dog-tired." 

Laurel hesitated. Mrs. Green belonged to the old school.

Then she thought of the Eddie of rubber and steel and whiplash admitting that he was tired; she thought of his being in uniform. "Come in," she said, "but come quietly.

I'm breaking rules, and it's long after midnight, besides." 

They stood for an instant with linked hands in the darkness of her room; then she went to the windows, drew down the shades on the summer night. The light flashed on. "Laurel! Gee, you look sweet.” 

Laurel gave a hushed laugh. A woman scolded me tonight like a Dutch aunt. I'm to be a Powers model from now on. Well, sit down. Now tell all."

Eddie slipped out of his army jacket, loosened his tan tie, sat down on the side of the bed, patted the bedspread at his side, but Laurel dropped onto a chair. “Not much to tell,” he said. “The FOLLIES closed. I was pretty certain to be drafted, an' I wanted to get into the damn army and get my year done. The sooner I was in, the sooner I'd be out to skate again. So I joined up six weeks ago. I'm in a Pennsylvania camp. Got a three-day leave, went up to Connecticut to see my only close relative, an aunt, spent all my money, so 

I'm thumbin' my way back to camp. Due there to-morrow noon. O' course I had to see you. Here I am.” "After we talk a while, Eddie, I'll give you bus or train fare back to camp. You can sleep on the way. The short rides you and I have dozed on buses coming back to Elizabeth from our exhibitions in Trenton, New Brunswick, all around the chain!" "How's all the gang?”

"Same as ever." "Say, Laurel, I'm sorry what Stevens done to you. The rat! I seen that about Yolanda Juarez in a rink paper some guy had in camp. I meant to write you, but you know I'm no writer. Once a week to my aunt is all I can manage.' 

Laurel moved impatiently on her chair. “It was a blow. 

I've had others. I can take it. Eddie, do you think America is going into the war?" "Well, bein' in an army, you get to think so. Can't handle guns, trucks, tents, K.P. and the rest of it without thinkin' war most the time." He leaned down, ripped his shoes off, and flung himself on his back, all in a characteristically continuous movement, tired as he was. "I'm leaving for Seaside City to-morrow. Head instructress there for the summer-but under Yolanda.' 

"Oh! Glad, ain't you? The seashore an' all." 

She shrugged. "Neither glad, nor sorry. I don't really care where I skate just so I skate. But, Eddie, I've missed you terribly; partner after partner has come along, and I've done the best I can, but the old teamwork just isn't there.

Not one has even come close to you. Now I'm just doing my free style solo. I haven't danced for a couple of weeks, except with a guard in the regular numbers. I won't until 

I find a partner. Stevens has been grunting at me that it's time I developed one, but, Eddie, I'm used to your fire, and other boys just don't have it. But I've got to find one soon, and perhaps I shall at Seaside. I must." 

Eddie grinned. "Miss my skating, eh? Listen, Laurel. Take any passable guy an' work with him. No matter how poor he is, it won't do you no real harm. Then in less than a year, I'll be out o' the army and back on wheels. We'll burn up the

floors, Laurel, the way we always done." He moved restlessly. "Come here, Laurel, we can talk quieter.' “No, Eddie, I—I think you'd better go now. You see—” “Come on, Laurel, just a few minutes. It'll be like old times. A little pettin'. We've never gone further than that. 

Come on, please. God knows when I'll see you again."

Laurel sat up very straight, and her thought was as straight as her body. This that she wanted to do, must not be done. The controlled pleasures she had taken with Eddie had always been taken in stolen minutes, in a parked car, or in some other prosaic place. The conclusive denials had always been quite naturally guided and brought about by her. Eddie and she had been on the way to a professional engagement or due at the rink. Somehow, without planning, their limited sensual pleasures had happened in places that were-she looked obliquely at Eddie-well, that were never, never like this. A room, a bed. This was different, unsafe. "There is something in the brooding summer night, there is loneliness only realized this minute in my heart, there's that tired boy in his uniform. But this isn't enough to make me lie down with him. I don't know what love is, I don't even know if I could ever feel love, but I do know that this isn't it." "Come on, Laurel," he whispered.

She turned her head to look at his spare muscular body, at his freshly tanned face-the skin which had always been colorless in rink life-at his shock of blond hair on her pillow. 

His eyes were narrowed ovals of intense blue, excited and, she knew frightenedly, exciting. "Get up, Eddie." "You won't turn down your old partner and your partner again once this damn year is done with. It ain't just me

wantin' to neck, Laurel, like I used to. It's more like I love you. Bein' away from you all this time-" "I can't," she said. "If I lie down with you now, we wouldn't just-do what we've done before. You know that, Eddie. And-and-" "You're cold." 

She smiled timidly and, realizing, resenting a rare timidity, drew down the corners of her mouth. "If you think that, well, it's true for you. Let it go at that. You know how much 

I like you, Eddie, but love—that's something else. I don't know anything about love." "I'll say you don't," he muttered, half sitting up, propped on his elbows. "Jeez, Laurel, I want you. To go back to that camp, thirty, forty thousand men, without havin' you to remember- 

"But you have," she interrupted. "I-I like you better than any one I know." "You know what I mean. I gotta crawl into an army cot for almost a year more, an' get up every morning when the damn bugle goes off. An'-here, I'm on a soft bed, yours, and you're here, an' we've played around before this together. So I know you ain't exactly frozen toward me. 

Laurel, let's get married." He sat up straight. “Listen, I cuss the army a lot. Most of the boys do. But it's just general cussin' an' don't mean much. Grousing, griping. That's it.

The fact is the army's decent enough to us. If a soldier wants to get married, the army'll give him time, I mean leave, furlough." He was standing, putting his arms around her. "Come on, Laurel. Wedding bells for us." 

She twisted from him, backed away a few steps. "You're sweet, Eddie, but we must, we simply must keep the truth between us. We always have. I like you thoroughly, warmly.

I trust you. But I don't love you, I don't want a man, not even the man women dream about. My dreams don't run that way. Maybe that's my bad luck." "Maybe it's your good luck. Well, I'm licked. I offered you my all, as the guys say in the movies, an' you laugh it off." "Laugh! Oh, Eddie." "Cryin'?" "Trying not to.' "Come here." 

She shook her head. 

Eddie moved to her, put his arms around her. "You don't have to cry. Maybe I'll do that, on my way back to camp.

Listen, when I get outta the army, I'm comin' straight to the main office of ROLLING AMERICA, sign up, and then head for you." "You're a dead game sport," she said against his lips, wavering a little in her hard resolution as his hand held her breast. "Good-by, Eddie. Good luck. I'll write to you, and you just better answer." 

Then he let her go. "I hate myself," she said. "It's my fault, I guess. A hell of a thing to do, puttin' you through this when you go to a new place to-morrow. Get your sleep, kid.” "Take your bus fare out of my bag," she said, pointing jerkily to the bureau. "The hell I will." "Eddie dear, you must." She saw him standing on street corners, staring blindly, hopefully at rushing cars that would not stop, waiting interminably for the one that would, that always in the end, did. Then he would be dropped somewhere in the dark night to walk on and stand waiting again

for another ride. "Eddie, open that bag. We've always been partners, haven't we?" "Yep. Partners. Not woman an' gigolo. I want you, not your money. دو "Take it!” She rushed to the bureau. "Sleep! I'll get none if you-oh, I'll get none anyway." 

The ten-dollar bill fluttered from his open hand to the floor.

Laurel thought that the next few moments were the worst of all, and she wondered, if Eddie again reached out to her, if she would lie with him without further drawing back. 

Poor tired boy. But he did not reach. He only sat down on the edge of the bed to put on his shoes, stood up to straighten his tie, slipped his jacket on, and put the cap on his blond head. "Even now," she thought, sad as she watched his simple preparation, "he might-oh, go on, Eddie dear, get going, Soldier." 

His mouth, saying his good-by, was crushing hers, but hating her own strength she kept her lips closed against the last desperate thrust of his tongue. Then there were no arms around her; a door creaked, closed softly; a click-clack of nails and leather on the pavement died away. Laurel opened her eyes and saw on the floor the crumpled green of the ten-dollar bill.

THE ONE WINDOW in her top-floor room looked down on a cluttered yard behind the huge mansion that had come so far down in the world. This small chamber, Laurel thought, must have sheltered successions of under-maids in the years of the house's private glory. Now for three dollars a week it would shelter her for the summer. Walls bare, save for a mirror above the pitcher and bowl washstand, furniture limited to narrow bed, rickety bureau, limping, hostile chair, and the washstand, were of little consideration to Laurel. The room was cheap, the house was but two blocks from the rink on the boardwalk. Those two were the facts that counted.

She changed quickly from the yellow tweed she had worn on the train to a white cotton frock. In an hour she was to report to Manager John Hedges and a few minutes later to skate out to work. Now making up her face she smirked sardonically at her obedience to Margaret Peters. Silly fussing! 

Better spend the time lubricating her wheels. But-Laurel's thoughts raced to the rink. Yolanda Juarez was there. 

Lovely, blond, light as the foam of the sea outside the plateglass of the rink. “Oh, Miss Peters is right. At least I must look smooth. I suppose I have let myself grow sloppy in matters of paint, powder, lacquer." She frowned, bending low to shave her legs. 204

Officially this summer she would be working under Yolanda, but actually she would be in charge of all instruction, dance, figure, free style. Yolanda would be the hostess, the performer on continuous display. "I'll do the teaching, the groundwork of the rink." Yolanda, Laurel knew, was not doing well in her teaching. In the various dance competi tions of ROLLING AMERICA, Yolanda's home rink in Trenton had not been well represented. Her pupils had reflected her glamour, gracefulness, verve, but the technique necessary to impress the cold eyes of the judges, had been, to a large extent, lacking. Laurel's Beatrice and Jean and two other representatives of the Elizabeth rink had won medals; youngsters from New Brunswick, from the other rinks, all had defeated Yolanda's pupils, and this, Laurel knew, was the real reason that she was called to the seasonally important SEASIDE CITY ARENA. "It's a good reason." The frown left her pale face. "Teaching is the backbone of my job. 

My exhibitions are my delight, but their real value is in prestige, and lure for youngsters to join the skating classes."

It was seven when she walked out into the heat of this first evening in July. From the roomy veranda of the faded mansion she looked down the long street to the gigantic rink built over sand and sea. Carrying skate box and summer uniform in paper wrapping, she joined the vacation throng on the street and her spirit rose to the tide of mob pleasure. 

The sun was so bright on her back, the sky of such azure purity over the sea, and the breath of the water such soft caress that, for the short walk, she forgot rink, skating, ambition, the ghost of Eddie in her heart, everything. There were just this bodily bath in salt air, warm sunshine, blue haze and the slow crash of waves on the sand. But the dreamer fled, the professional skater returned when she

crossed the famous boardwalk and nodded to the woman in the ticket booth. From there on into the office it was a matter of hellos, nods, to floor men, check girls, many of whom she knew from all around the circuit. 

John Hedges looked up from his desk. "Hi, Laurel. Sit down." 

She faced this young manager she liked. "I guess you know why you are here. The Arena is all set for a huge season. Yolanda's here, o' course, to dress up the summer's most important rink, but she's all eye appeal. You gotta get our dancers an' free style babies organized.

They're comin' for the summer from all over the country an' you're needed. Solid teaching, Laurel. I'm glad you're here." "Thanks, Mr. Hedges. I caught a glimpse of Mrs. Hedges in the first aid room." "Yep. Elsa's workin' there." John looked at the clock. "Seven-fifteen. Roll along to the dressing-room, kidlet. In fifteen minutes you'll be out on the floor." He rose, stepped around the desk and walked quickly to her. "How's it for a quick kiss, Laurel, just to start the season off?” 

Laurel moved to the door. "I'm here to teach skating, Mr. Hedges. Why do you try this with me? You've asked me," she spoke seriously, but the manager grinned at the cool amusement in her eyes, "a dozen times in the past year, here and there around the circuit. Don't we understand each other by this time?"

His pretended snarl turned into a laugh. “Go on, get out of here, you wax virgin. Here, shake my fin anyway.” 

They shook hands. "Just the same, Laurel, I'll catch you on the beach some moonlight night. You ain't all ice."

"You won't, and I am.' 

"Maybe so. We'll see. Beat it now. I'll have you announced on the loud-speaker when you all line up.”

Laurel hurried through the hundreds checking skate boxes, shoes, sitting on benches, putting on skates. It was, she knew, as though she were back in Elizabeth. What a marvel of standardized efficiency and equipment and architecture was in these links in the chain of ROLLING AMERICA. She smiled, her hand on the doorknob of the dressing-room she was to share with Yolanda. "Yet the differences stick out. Hedges. That tomcat! But I like him because professionally he's on the level. Working for him I know where I stand. He backs up his staff. As long as I do my work he'll stand by me. Butpoor Elsa Hedges. She must know him by now." 

Laurel opened the door. "Hello, Laurel." "Yolanda." 

The bare powdered arm of the flower-scented girl waved casually and then was lowered to the adjustment of skates, laced white shoes. Laurel, changing quickly into the summer uniform of white sharkskin with maroon edging at the neck and rimming the brief skirt, thought grimly of Margaret Peters. The advice of that smart woman was sound. "For three months I'll be skating side by side with this lovely doll. Not once will her make-up smudge. That rosebud mouth will be always the same vivid orange-red. 

Well, I'll just have to watch my face." Through the seaward window a tongue of wind licked into the hot little dressingroom. "That's nice," Yolanda sighed. "I'd like to sit at this window for the next four hours instead of skating." "I wouldn't."

The two simple statements would have held for more acute ears the ring of opposed swords. A long silence followed, laden with disconcerting tension. At last, “Let's go,” 

Yolanda said.

They skated out the door, down a short alley and through the opening in the rail onto the floor. The flood-lights, dimmed, poured onto the empty arena. Yolanda and Laurel took their places a stroke ahead of the line of instructors, their backs to the wide crimson curtains that hid the organ now beginning the overture to the night session. The curtains opened. The signal to skate forward. In perfect unison the group moved out to the center oblong, stopped at rigid attention, facing the floor manager, who winked, without smiling, at Yolanda. The two girls in white sharkskin, with bare legs and arms, were spectacular in this routine effect of frozen action, but discipline did not prevent Yolanda from whispering to Laurel, "New organist to-night. Marvelous, isn't he? Listen to those chords! I don't know who he is. Jack Hedges only muttered that he was a new man, and an 'extra special, by God!" "He is good," Laurel hissed back to the frozen Yolanda.

The overture ended with the invariable long-emphasized bar, but the flood-lights did not come on in full glare for the first All-Skate. Instead John Hedges' voice rasped on the loud-speaker from the organ stage. "Ladies and Gentlemen. Before the session begins I have two short announcements to make which will be of interest to you. ROLLING AMERICA takes pleasure in announcing that its instructress, Laurel Easton, long known to so many of you, is joining the SEASIDE CITY ARENA for the summer season." The voice paused. A cool patter of general applause came from behind the circling rail. Then Laurel's stab of

disappointment was dulled, for a small group was wildly clapping. "My dance kids, show kids," she thought. "They're glad I'm here." The manager's voice resumed, "And we take pride in announcing that at our organ this summer will be a man of real musical reputation. Ladies and Gentlemen, join me, please, in welcoming Charles Easton, from Neville

Conser-Conse-College.' 

There was rain of wide applause, the floor crew whirled to face the stage. Laurel, stunned, utterly unbelieving, stared across the rink and up at the console to where a man stood at the side of John Hedges. The man bowed once, sharply, and sat down. The flood-lights flashed. All-Skate was signaled. The opening of Chattanooga Choo Choo crashed out. And the public streamed from the dozen rail openings to form the joyous, laughing, singing torrent. "I-I can't go to him now. I shouldn't know what to say. 

Scold? Welcome? I don't even know what I feel. The fool!

He's cut his own throat! God knows I know nothing of Dad's world, but I do know that faculty members of age-encrusted music schools don't play organs in skating rinks. But gee! 

I-I guess I have a father, all right.” Laurel slipped into the inner oblong, skating its area slowly, trying to adjust her realization to this mad act of her father. Yolanda swerved to her side and they skated on for a few rounds, arms around each other's waist, presenting in their identical costumes, twinlike flawlessness of bare legs, pliant bodies, erect heads, in fluent motion. Only in face and hair was contrast: 

Yolanda, smiling, animated, blond, blossoming; Laurel, dusky-haired, pale, reserved, bending like a young birch in the breeze of increasing speed. Both knew that many spectators behind the rails were staring at them, following the picture they made, and now again there was contrast, for

Yolanda understood precisely what good business this was for ROLLING AMERICA and deliberately continued it when Laurel, indifferent to theatrical values outside of skating itself, would have dropped her arm and skated away still stunned by the professional appearance of her father. She was surprised at Yolanda's evident desire to team with her. 

They skated on, talking, Yolanda suddenly all questions about Charles, Laurel withdrawing in guarded answers. 

Yes. Charles Easton was her father. Yes. He really was on the Neville faculty. Hm. Probably this was a form of vacation for him and his plan of happy surprise for his daughter. "But, ye gods, Laurel, aren't you going up to talk to him?" “While he's playing? Don't be a dope, Yolanda. And between numbers I'm in the line-up." "But that's awful. Poor man. To have done all this and you don't rush to him!” 

Laurel gave her a slanting look. "You'd do it right now, wouldn't you?" "Of course! To hell with discipline, duty, all the rest of it! I'd go up and give him a smacking kiss. Besides, you strange girl, it would be swell publicity. Hank Stevens is here to-night. He'd give at least four grunts of approval." 

Laurel dropped her arm from the slim soft waist and swerved a stroke away. "I'll talk to him later when he turns on the Clarovox for his twenty-minute breather." She nodded, moved off, and went into a spiraled arabesque. Yolanda shrugged slightly, muttered, "Queer girl. Dull, too. But I wish to hell I could skate like her." On impulse she slipped through the crowd, skating backward to the stage, where she bent swiftly to release the skates from her high white shoes. A vault to the stage, a run up the steps to the console, and she was standing beside the organist.

Charles from the edge of his glance was aware of a figure in white sharkskin, and he was badly frightened. He bent over the keys, changing into the St. Louis Blues. What was 

Laurel going to offer? Anger, nervousness, approval, welcome, pleasure? What parent with his grim record could look into the heart of a youngster? Was this wild step he had taken the very proof of what from the first she had feared-interference? That confessional dinner in Trenton seven months ago! So much had passed since then, such good rich things. Gifts, association, visits, Margaret Peters' aid and generosity, even Slade's grotesque but warming friendliness to her, and last and greatest of all on the road toward reconciliation and love, his composition of her exhibition music. That last had brought "Dad" from her lips!

Charles pressed down now into the St. Louis Blues, desperately anxious to look up, but horribly afraid. All these good things! But-but? “I may have obliterated them tonight. With this one wild move. That cruel dinner seven long months ago! I'll never forget her words, 'Don't look so wretched, Mr. Easton. All this has been just impulse. You know that. And you'll get over it quickly. Your life is in 

Princeton. Mine is in the rinks. Good-by. I wish you good luck.' Oh, Laurel, now grinding out these popular measures 

I'm afraid to look up at you! You're all life's meaning to me, and if you lash at me now-I'm through. I-"” "Mr. Easton, I simply had to come up and tell you how grand your playing is. I mean it's superb!"

Amazed, he turned his head to stare, while his hands rippled on over the keys. "I'm Yolanda Juarez."

He wondered if dull antagonism was in his eyes, as it was in his heart. This gay chit who had, with a shrug of her

white, soft-skinned shoulder, thrust Laurel into disappointment and dark eclipse. Yet it was she who came to welcome him, his child who stayed away. "Thanks so much, Miss Juarez. It is kind of you to tell me that."

If there was antagonism in the gray eyes it was, for 

Yolanda, lost in the strained unhappiness she saw. Impulsive again, impulsive always, she cried above the boom and blare, "Laurel's the scariest thing in the seven rinks of Hank 

Stevens' chain! She won't break routine order enough to jump up here and interrupt you with a hello. She's waiting for your rest period. The trouble with her is that she's too darned conscientious and serious for this honky-tonk whirl. 

She belongs in the choir of a church. But, Mr. Easton, how she skates! I wish I did, like her. No, I don't at all, come to think of it. If I did I might grow serious too. And what does seriousness get you?” "You-you are saying she is a better skater than you?" "Of course she is. Any fool can see that." "Mr. Stevens?" "He's not a fool." "He appointed you head instructress of all the rinks. Why, if he believes Laurel is what you say?" "Oh, he wanted a head instructress, a widely known performer and an actress, all in one, and all for the same pay. 

I'm all three and so-here I am. But I must run away now or the old turtle may turn his beady eye on me. Good-by, Mr. Easton. Glad you are here."

She was away in a movement of white flesh as smooth as the sharkskin that briefly clasped it. "I ought to resent, even hate that girl,” he muttered, “but, God damn it, I like her! Poor little Laurel. What chance

have skates against the devastating personality of a Yolanda?"

In the center oblong Yolanda was saying to Laurel, “I adore your father." She added, mockingly demure, “He is so sad, I wanted to lay my cheek against his.” 

Laurel said coldly, "Why didn't you?" "I thought it might disturb his playing. I shall next time. 

Did our bellowing at each other interrupt the organ?" 

Laurel snapped, "Not in the least. He could play that thing blindfolded." She skated away. A few words interrupt him! Her memory flew back to the story of a pale young man, drunk, exhausted, playing the piano night after night in cabarets, cellars, dance-halls. It would have taken something close to a murder to have interrupted then his mechanical work. And now this fluttering ballerina was flattering herself that her empty chatter could make him miss a note. What rot!... A reckless skate locked hers and she went down in a crumpled heap with a young man who, crimson with dismay, offered quite incompetent help. For an instant a murderous glance slipped from her, but rink etiquette produced a smile on her lips. She grimaced narrowly at the floor men who had followed the shrill whistle to her side, where two of them were helping the young man to get up, and one was standing in the rear stemming the onsweeping columns. The floor man who had blown the whistle invariably sounded at a fall, winked at her. “Hurt, Laurel?" "No. Just stung knees.” The little whirlpool in the plunging stream vanished. Laurel's arm was touched. She nodded, swung into slow stroke with the manager. "I've been wondering why you don't go up and say hello to your old man.”

Exasperated she shot him a venomous look. "You're the second person in the past few minutes to tell me how I'm to act with my own parent." "Who else?" "Cream Puff."

John laughed. “She welcomed him immediately. She's sweet, Laurel." "And I'm vinegar. Oh, well. Probably he already sees her as a second daughter. He's a softie. The born father-" "Not according to a yarn I heard last winter, he ain't a born father." "These rinks! Talk about goldfish bowls!" "Yolanda would make a sweet daughter to any elderly man." "Don't you know any other descriptive word than sweet? 

It gets a bit sickening." "More than cream puff? You used that." "I meant that to be sickening." 

John sighed. “Oh, Lord. I knew it. The minute I was told that you and Yolanda was to be here together this summer I says to the missus, "They'll get along like a couple of panthers.' What's the matter? Don't you like Yolanda?” "Sure I do, well enough. But-" "Does she high hat you? Is she stuck up?"

Laurel said evenly, “She's very nice. I agree with you that she's sweet." "But let her keep hands off your dad, eh? You prefer bein' an only child. Laurel, you're jealous, a mean little jealous kid, an' I'm ashamed of you.' Calmer, she skated slowly on with him, realizing again why she liked this manager better than she liked any manager on the circuit. He was intrusive, impertinent, noisy,

inquisitive, everything she disliked in a man, but still she trusted him, and this trust, she knew, was based on the fact that professionally he was outstandingly honest. He never stabbed in the back. If you worked hard for him he stood beside you. The tale was told that more than once he had stood up to Mr. Stevens himself and given shout for grunt in defense of a maligned employee. And often he struck the heart of a question with seemingly uncanny aim. "For instance, now, I-I really think he's right. I am jealous.

I don't like Dad's surrender-of course it is surrender. Yolanda is so sweet-to the young woman he'll see as—as he sees me a mere child who ought to be in college or finishing school or nursery. I'm his kid. She isn't. But she'll blind him and he'll want to stick a lollipop in her baby mouth.” 

The manager said, "For the third time I'm telling you that your old man's a wow at the organ. ROLLING AMERICA's never had his equal." "That's quite true." “Well, let's get down to business. There's the making of a swell dance class right here on the floor. Yolanda-bless her sweet heart and even sweeter legs-has-and it ain't taken her two months to do it-made 'em look mighty nice in the dance steps as far as the public can see. But they'll look lousy to the competition judges in the fall. Edges, timing, back lean, all wrong underneath. You know what I mean." "Does Yolanda know?" Laurel asked. "Probably. I guess so. Or maybe she don't care. Or maybe she wants 'em all to skate like her and depend on speed, glitter and er-er-" 

Laurel's voice was demure. “On sweetness." "Shut up, Laurel. So to-night near the end of the session

I'll announce a class in elementary dance steps for after the session from eleven-thirty until twelve-thirty. You'll take over." "She won't mind?” "I manage this rink." "Still, I don't want trouble with her." "You needn't worry about that. She's skating a midnight solo show at a roadhouse this whole week. Nice dough for her. Easy work. And, maybe, nicest of all, a hell of a goodlookin' master-of-ceremonies calls for her in a shiny car, an, I suppose, after her show, takes her home to her roomor to his. Nope, she won't begrudge you takin' on a class of her skaters. Listen, Laurel, about this panther business. You and Yolanda. The fact is, that with all my talkin' about her, she's a good kid at that. There's nothin' mean in her. Try to get along with her. See, I'm begging you." "For heaven's sake, Mr. Hedges! Have you ever known me to quarrel with any one?" "No." "Well then?" "You'll be good?" "I'll be-sweet.' She drew her arm back from his friendly pinch, and just then the end of the long All-Skate was sounded with the full power of the organ. Laurel looked up at her father's bent head, and a faint pride touched her. His silver hair was smooth, his face was, in some way she could not analyze, distinguished, his clothes, as always, were immaculate, good, without being weakly fussy or dapper. He looked, she vowed, to be what she vaguely considered aristocratic. "That's Dad up there. And he's there because he loves the kid that tortures him in a dozen remembered rôles-a de-

serted baby, a shivering girl delivering newspapers from a crazy truck after school hours, a sweating helper in a cleaning and pressing joint, a five-and-dime store slave, a frightened climber into the skating world, and even to-day a girl unhappy because she's been cheated out of promotion in position and reputation and money. Oh, all those early shadowy figures and now the real, disappointed person, send him up on that stage. Poor Dad, he wants to pay back the costs of desertion, and he can't, because there is nothing to pay with. Oh, I—I wish, I almost wish he had never found me in Elizabeth. Then he'd be back in his rooms facing the quiet gray stone walls and wide campus and not edging into a life that must always be just noise and cheap tinsel to him. Why, he's like an old horse contented on an easy treadmill with a kind master, suddenly sold to a circus.”

Laurel stood, frozen, beside Yolanda, a step ahead of the line of floor men; the flood-lights were shut off, the color wheels turned, the pools of crimson and blue and gold spilled onto the polished floor. And as the couples streamed out for the waltz number Laurel heard the lovely Skaters' 

Waltz played as she had never heard it played before. This time the pride in her was neither faint nor vaguely touching. 

It was authentic, deep, full-swelling in her heart. 

A young man asked a little timidly, "Will you dance this with me, Miss Easton? I'm afraid I'm not good."

She gave him her brief shallow smile, quite unconscious of its cold quality, and the young man was amazed at discovery that he could really dance. Later that night when he had another partner he was deeply chagrined to find that he could not really dance.

Charles arranged the stacked records for the automatic player, turned the electric signal to All-Skate, and went to the little room at the left of the stage for a cigarette and a rest beside the window facing the sea. Descending the few steps from organ bench to stage, walking. the few feet to the door, he admitted a weariness beyond anything he had experienced since he had climbed from the gutters of New York almost a quarter of a century before. It was, he knew, for the greater part, physical—and he had been playing for only one and a half hours! Physical in the sense of result. 

The causes? “I suppose it's the strain, excitement, and that's peculiar, for I'm normally a cool chap." Then he struck it. 

Laurel. "Yes. It's fear. Suppose I've lost her by this reckless move! She hasn't come up to see me. Why not? I was a fool not to tell her what I was going to do. But of course I knew she might have stopped me. Besides, I wanted to surprise her. And that was stupid, for, near as I have come to her, she doesn't yet want me right under her eyes, every day, every night. That fierce independence she-" He opened the door.

Charles saw her standing at the screened window, noticed instantly her nervous fingers smoothing down the sharkskin, pressing bloodless lines on her legs bare below the rim of the short skirt. He stood still to meet directly her long stare. 

At last, "Well, Laurel?" 

She gave no answer. "Your eyes give the verdict, Laurel. Purity of green ice.

I'm sorry. Laurel looked away, turned again to him. A quick nervous smile touched her lips, vanished instantly. She seemed to him like an embarrassed schoolgirl, stiff, awkward, and suddenly, his weariness forgotten, he was happy, for there was

nothing to fear from an attitude he could understand. That aloofness, instinctive withdrawal, was merely characteristic of this daughter he loved so fiercely. She just could not unbend. Only once, at that wonder moment in the Princeton chapel when she had been swept by his composition, had she melted, rushed to him, put her arm around his shoulder, given her kiss. It was not in her character to be demonstrative of softness, warmth. She wasn't angry now. She was shy. And shyness drove her in upon herself, forced her to rely on harsh armor. He remembered now that word Margaret had sprung on him in New York the day of his first desperate shopping expedition. Carapace. "Mr. Easton, under that carapace your daughter is an extremely shy girl." 

By God, she was shy, self-doubting, save in the power and beauty of her skating. 1 "Come here, Laurel."

She moved slowly to him, stopping an arm's-length away. 

He said, "It's great at the organ. This is going to be the best, the only, vacation I've ever had. I'm crazy about it.” "You-you are crazy, all right. There's no doubt of that." "When my chief at Neville said that, I told him just a few things about myself, why I was doing the thing. He listened. 

Let's sit down." 

They sat on straight chairs. Charles smiled wryly. "You 'know, Laurel, there is such popular fallacy about professors. 

Comic-paper stuff. Business men's prejudices. So-called practical opinion. The old professors are supposed to be so simple, foolish outside of the lecture halls." Charles shrugged. Then he went on lazily, thinking that desultory talk would calm her nervousness, break down self-consciousness. "Well, of course some of them are blinking old codgers who offer rich material for jokes about absent-mindedness.

But I assure you the majority are pretty sharp judges of humanity. Among these is my chief. He listened to me, set his imagination to work and, when I had finished, wished me luck for the summer and his manner withdrew the charge of madness. No, child, there will be no trouble for me at the Neville end. The question is at this end-whether I'll be satisfactory at the organ, and whether you will resent my being here." “Satisfactory!” she stammered. "Why, you're the best organist ROLLING AMERICA ever heard. Mr. Hedges told me that during your very first number." "I'm gratified,” Charles said with a grin. “And your possible resentment?” 

Her hand jerked at her skirt and the raspberry lacquered fingernails were like bright drops of blood on the pure white sharkskin. "I don't mind your being here,” she said. “I-I only thought the life might kill you." 

He flung his head backward in a laugh. "If Slade heard that! Oh, Lord, how terrible that music machine is! I think 

I'll cut my rest and go back and play." "You sit where you are. Don't give an extra minute's work to the company. "Why, Laurel, that from you!" "Oh, I'm different. I'd skate until daybreak. That's my career. I'm skating for myself, not for Mr. Stevens. You're playing just because I'm here.' 

"That's true.” He paused. “By the way, how badly we all misjudge people. I've been hating that Yolanda all this time since she was given your rightful place. She seemed to me a piratical, intimidating monster. To-night I met her for the first time. Did you see her fly up here to welcome me? I was

amazed to find her just a child instead of that calculating adventuress I'd seen in the distance." "I saw her," Laurel said sharply. "And, as far as I am concerned, she is a pirate. And you call her child!" "She was very sweet to me." "Sweet! Ye gods, that word again! Mr. Hedges thinks she's sweet. Yolanda thinks she's sweet. Now you. All the world thinks she's sweet. Well, maybe she is. But I wish she'd break a leg." "Is this my daughter?” He laughed. "You really liked her?" "I couldn't help it.”

Laurel sniffed, gave him a disgusted, slanting look. “She gets around every one. That's her way.' 

"She told me you are a better skater than she. That doesn't sound like a pirate. Did she mean it?" "She meant it. I am. Oh, she's honest." "Hm. An honest pirate. Queer blend.” 

Laurel said, "Is my face shiny? I must go to my dressingroom for repairs. And is my mouth red enough? And aren't my nails just too, too divine? All that is the fault of our sweet Yolanda. I used to be satisfied just to be decently neat. 

Now I have to be a portrait." "I notice the change. I like it. Why is it to Yolanda's credit?" "I didn't say credit. I said fault. Your friend, Miss Peters, shares it. She gave me a talking to, I can tell you. Last night in Elizabeth. B-r-r. I was a sloven, a slattern, a drab wreck. 

I must paint, powder, enamel, glamorize. Why? Because this Yolanda you like so suddenly and so violently-" "Now," Charles protested, "she just seems a nice, warmhearted kid. There's no violence in my-"

" is so gorgeous. I'm in dark eclipse, and Miss Peters flings lipstick and the rest at me. Bah! It bores me. Oh, well, maybe she's right. But you see, Yolanda has a foundation to build on. Genuine beauty. I'm too plain to-" "Plain! Are you serious? Laurel, you're lovely."

She got up, hurried out the door, but stopped to look back, speaking over her shoulder. "There are just three persons in the world insane enough to think that. Miss Peters, you, and—and Eddie.” “Ah, Eddie. Do you miss him much?”

Laurel, leaning against the door, turned to face Charles. "When a skating team is formed, after a short while it either becomes a unit, or it breaks up. Pros can't be halfway about it. In dances, double spins, lifts, the whole bag of tricks, partners are just glued together. One knows every move, swerve, the other will make. Teamwork accurate as a machine. Once Eddie and I were sent to Coney Island to give a pair skating show. I took one look at the place and nearly died. Dad, it was a platform like a prize ring, a twenty-foot square out in the middle of a gigantic swimming-pool. Not a skating floor, but a mere platform surrounded by deep water. We were hustled to bathhouses for getting into costume, and were rowed out to the flood-lighted platform. At the time, I was still shaky from a week of grippe and I'll never forget my feeling when I stepped out of the boat onto the platform. There was no railing around it. Nothing between the edges and the water. The boat left. Eddie and 

I were marooned. And as far as I was concerned I mean marooned. After a deep curtsy on four wheels I was to open the act with a slow deliberate cartwheel, and then go into a fast split. I did the cartwheel and landed with one skate over the water. The split was impossible from there. I was so

nervous and scared that I was crying. Eddie saw my eyes and mouth and he made one of his incredible leaps from a standstill and grabbed me. For ten delirious minutes he spun me, whirled me, lifted me, laughing away my fear every minute, and automatically at first I just went through empty, limp obedience to his speed and power. He went on laughing, coaxing, studying my face as he told me after, and the moment came when he hissed, 'Cartwheel and split, kid. You're O. K. now.' I was. My legs, skates, floated over my head and with inches to spare at the edge of the platform, I went into a split, and rose to dance in Eddie's arms. The hundreds lining the pool were in our pockets for the brief remainder of the show. See, Dad? That was teamwork. That is what a skating partner means." 

Charles nodded, lighted a cigarette. “Go on.” “The rest is not spectacular. Just routine show business. 

Eddie and I a team. Well, he went to the ROLLER FOLLIES. 

Since then I've been digging for a partner. Of course I haven't expected to find one like Eddie. A promising young man that I could train is what I want. But so far-" She shrugged. “I'm performing in solos only.” "But he'll be back soon, won't he? The FOLLIES closed." "He came to see me last night. I talked to him in my room in Elizabeth. He's been in the army for six weeks. He enlisted. Wanted to get the year over as quickly as possible and get back to skating." Laurel sighed. "How does he like the army?" "Says it isn't so bad, but he misses skating. I'd die.” 

Venturing one deep probe Charles said, "Perhaps he will!" 

She winced. “What a morbid thought! America is not in the war." "Not yet," Charles said quietly.

"You think it is coming?" "Of course." "Eddie will come back. He always lands on his feet.” "The arena of war is not a skating rink." "He wants to marry me when he comes back."

This time it was Charles who winced. 

He looked up sharply, but the door had closed on her flight. 

After the Fourth of July parades, inspection, Eddie rolled out of camp in the sedan of a soldier friend who, besides giving him transportation, lent him three dollars. They quickly left the dust-choked camp behind, tore along fifty miles of concrete, and left the car in the vast parking lot of an amusement park in the suburbs of Philadelphia. Eddie and Bill for the next few hours were mental twins.... A good dinner? Sure. "Steak, home fries, corn fritters, beer,” said Eddie to the counter man in the diner. "Make it two," 

Bill muttered.... "There's a skating rink," Eddie said; “it's small, but me for it. What you goin' to do, pal?” “Skate too," Bill muttered.... 

An hour later Eddie said bitterly, "T" hell with this place. 

Of all the lousy floors!” “Yep,” said Bill, "let's go." They went out into the lurid night colors of the amusement park. “Let's dance," Eddie said. "Who? You and me?” “You and me," 

Eddie explained patiently, "an' a couple of frails we'll find."

They stood against the wall in a dance-hall and presently were talking with two girls who had homely faces, flaunting unbound breasts in halters, and nice brown legs bare to the farthest hundredth of an inch prescribed by law. After dancing for an hour they paused for beer. Bill heard Eddie tell

his girl that it was too hot in the crowded dance-hall, so Bill said to his girl, "It's too hot in here." Eddie said, "My friend's got a car. How about some cool country air?" Bill said, “Babe, I gotta car. Let's get some air....' The sedan whirled over low hills, crossed lakes and streams, thrusting a golden tongue across the bridges, sucking it back as the shore was reached. Eddie had bought a quart of whisky and drank deeply of it, the girl at his side drinking shallowly, and the bottle was passed to the couple in the front seat, and then for miles passed back and forth. "Slow down," Eddie said. “Let's have a look at the stars an' the moon." The car was barely moving. Emma said, “Look at that cute brook." Eddie said, "I can't let you go, long enough to look at any brook," but he did tear his mouth away long enough to say, "Bill, let's stop at Emma's cute brook." "Right," said Bill, and the car turned into a narrow country lane, lurched to a stop. 

Lois and Bill became a single shadow on the front seat, but Emma and Eddie separated to engage in a little serious drinking. The bottle, not quite emptied, flew out of the car in a hilarious curve to splash into the brook, and then in the back seat, too, there was a single shadow. Throaty laughs followed feeble protests from the girls. Emma, laughing drunkenly, huddled limply against Eddie. He caught his breath. But memory of a few nights ago swept him for an instant from the Pennsylvania countryside dark with this warm, panting, giggling girl slumping at his side. He was in 

Laurel's room, lying room, lying on her bed, begging her to come to him. 

He was looking at a virginal girl who was refusing him, not, he knew, because of frost, but because of a certain level of character she stubbornly maintained against a feared desire. 

He was begging. He heard his suggestion of wedding bells,

her distressed refusal, his promise to come back to her. He had left the bed, moved to her. He was kissing her mouth that stayed closed against him. Her breast, clothed, was soft in his hand. Sweet, straining breast. Clothed, not offered.

Eddie opened his eyes in the blackness of Bill's car. Here was offering starkly frank. 

Then Emma, her sweat and boredom in a factory forgotten, her poverty in the shack of a coal-mining father forgotten, murmured to a man who was leaving quiet sad dream, for fever and delight.

SINGERS, DANCERS, radio comedians, Hollywood stars, radio top-flight performers, flocked to the army training camps, to the naval training stations, to the airfields, throughout the country, to give their professional skill to the military forces, paying their own expenses, working harder than they had ever worked for pay. Pay was out, except the vast riches that came in the laughs and yells and whistles of American men preparing to join the Chinese, the British, the Russians, the Free French, and the gallant remnants of the countries that had gone under the German plague. Big names at the camps! Helen Hayes, Bob Hope, Joan Crawford, Walter 

Winchell, Carole Lombard, Albert Spalding, Joe Louis, the 

Barrymores, the rest of the glittering roster and on down into the reservoirs of less-known entertainers. Magicians, tapdancers, mind-readers, jugglers. Great entertainers, humble entertainers, all in the same class of those who gave to the armed forces the best that they had in them. 

Late on a mid-July night a bus from Fort Dix rolled into 

Seaside City. Charles looked at the sleeping girl whose long lashes penciled the pale skin below her eyes. He was reluctant to wake her. In the gymnasium at the camp she had been a whirlwind. Quickly Charles ran over her day. At nine, shortly after breakfast, Laurel had taught a small 227

figure-skating class; at ten, because Yolanda was sick, she had skated the morning session that lasted until twelvethirty; at quarter past two she had reported for the afternoon session; at five she had taught elementary dance steps until just before the bus left Seaside City. Then she had given her solo show at the camp. Now she was asleep on the uncomfortable seat. And he must wake her. He admitted wryly, without marked interest, that he was damned tired himself. He, too, had worked the morning and afternoon sessions at the rink and had played the piano for her exhibition at the camp. Now he smiled, thinking how well the music he had written for her was bearing the strain of continual repetition. To-night Laurel had been billed as a "Symphony on Wheels" and she had fulfilled the descriptive term, a flame in flying crimson satin. But there was nothing flame-like now about the huddled girl on the bus seat.

Charles sighed, took her hand. It lay in his, heavy, unresponsive. A deep tide of responsibility, of quiet happiness, flooded him. Here at last, almost a quarter of a century latebut not too late!-he was holding the limp hand of his sleeping baby. What was it Slade had said a few weeks back in Princeton? "You've lost the joys. Tucking your kid into bed, sitting with her until in sleep she has lost the fear of darkness. Toys. Providing for her education. Feeling her turn to you for advice, help, tenderness, love. That's lost, Easton, flown away on the cold wind of your past decisions."

Well, lost it was. All those delights that had never been born. But this hand he was holding now. Ah, that was his own daughter's flesh and bone and blood. Grown-up she might be, his superior in life, independent, sharpened by her planned accomplishment, but at this very moment he was holding the hand of his tired-out baby. He stared at her.

Her eyes opened narrowly, closed again, her head fell against his shoulder. Charles whispered, "Laurel dear. You must wake up. We're here." 

The long dark lashes fluttered. The eyes, blurred with sleep, looked at him without recognition. Then consciousness came, and a frown, and a small hand was withdrawn. "I must have dropped off," she muttered. 

In the midnight bedlam of Freda's Pizzeria Harold Slade was devouring huge slabs of tomato pie, washing down the delicious concoction with tall glasses of amber beer, talking, laughing, belching, with mouth crammed and awash. "Easton, those murals in the public library are something, let me tell you. That Bannard, whoever the hell he is, can paint. 

I wish I'd done 'em. Why haven't you seen them?" "I've told you again and again that I haven't time for anything. Don't you understand that I have no day off? 

Seven days a week! You might take Laurel to the library on her day. I'd like her to see good painting in the informative company of a painter." 

Harold stared around the small restaurant. "You know,

Easton, I think this country is going to war. Look at the soldiers and sailors in here.' "In the rink too. More and more of them. The giant is stirring in his sleep.' 

"How will it affect a big slob like myself? Impractical, independent hobo for so many years that I've forgotten how to give or take orders. I was fireman second class on the

Mount Vernon in war one. The best God-damned transport on the Atlantic. But from then on, paint only.” "You'll do war posters."

The vast shoulders rose and fell. "I'd rather shovel the coal all over again." 

Charles laughed. "Try it for ten minutes. Lord, you're my age, more or less. Well, the war isn't here yet. What are you going to do this summer?” "Paint a couple of portraits, try to paint the ocean, maybe give a show late in the season if I can horn in on the local art scene. Talk about labor unions! You ought to take a gander at the political set-up of the art departments of 

Women's Clubs. But I've got a reasonable reputation, and 

I'll pump it up to unreasonable size, and lo! there'll be a string of Slades on the chaste walls of a gallery. Next winter 

I'm going back to Princeton. Nice place." "You're a lucky man." "And you think I don't know it?" His great shaggy head turned toward the door of the pizzeria. "Here's your gang.

Why do they hit on this place? It's a couple of miles from the rink." "Freda's a good egg. Lets 'em make all the noise they want. And the pizza is the best." 

A dozen or more youngsters banged into the room, followed by John and Elsa Hedges, floor men, a skate mechanic, and an incredibly lovely blonde who, seeing Charles in the corner booth, seemed to float swiftly to him on a ripple of her own sweet laughter. "Hello, Yolanda.” "Charles darling. Oh, do sit down. This is a relaxed place."

She sank onto the chair around the table corner from Charles and with round eyes stared at Harold across the ruin of tomato, cheese, anchovy. 

The painter snarled, "Never seen anything like me, have you? A vast overturned tub of jelly. Hideous-"

"I never argue," Yolanda murmured. 

Harold roared a laugh. “Now I'll stare for a moment. It's not that I haven't seen girls as lovely as you. They're a dime a dozen all over the land, but still they make my hands itch to get at them." "Well, really!" "You mistake me. I want to paint you. And as soon as possible. A rose can wilt in a day. I want to catch you while this silver dew is so incredibly on you. You must come out to my trailer home and studio. It's in a tourist camp not ten minutes' walk from here. My wife will give you tea, a euphemism for protection." "A what?" "You'll hang on the wall of a gallery, centered too. I might even give you a wall to yourself if I can really catch dew on the petals of a rose." 

Shocked into anger, Charles slipped quietly from the booth, dropping a bill on the table, looking back but once at the girl absorbed in flattery, at the man lost in honest creative impulse. Clearly neither knew that he had gone. 

Even at midnight the street was hot, but Charles walked fast. Anger of any sort was rare in his crusted heart, but now it rose to rage he knew was futile. That tramp artist! 

One close look at the sparkling, commonplace candy-box girl, and his fingers reached for brush and paint. While the integrity, depth, uncommon beauty of Laurel lay undiscovered to a painter's eyes. Yolanda again. Head instructress of ROLLING AMERICA! And now the captive model of a painter who knew Laurel and did not see her. The fabled clarity of an artist's eyes! Bah! There was no more judgment in them than in the eyes of the front row at a musical comedy!

Charles walked on, high rage cooling to depression. Poor 

Laurel. He tramped the streets, passed his own lodgings and stood under a tree before the dark bulk of the old mansion of his daughter's rented room. Looking down the alley that led to the back yard he could see a yellow gleam from her room on the top floor. "Poor kid. Not in bed yet. Probably pressing her uniform for to-morrow while a blinded man probes for fool's gold in a-"

He stared at the faint yellow gleam so high and mournful and lost in the summer night. 

My dear Dr. Saunders,

Seaside City Arena 

August 14, 1941

If I did not know that you could replace me in a jiffy to the advantage of the school I should have made my decision to resign some time before this middle of August, so that you would have had ample time to look for another man, but I know that instructors of my slim attainment are as plentiful as the bass in the well-stocked Maine lake you are now, I hope, pleasurably fishing.

In June when you were so kind and wise in your acceptance of my temporary move into my daughter's world, I gave, unasked by you, my motive. You listened, understood. Now, remembering your years of patience, the security I have enjoyed under your authority, I want to tell you why I am leaving permanently the teaching world for Laurel's world. Fundamentally, of course, the cause is the same-my desire to be close to the youngster who has grown up from childhood I deserted. But there is a secondary reason, one of no small importance to me. 

It is what I have always called my feeling of "outsideness." In my grim drunken years I was outside life. I was outside my abortive attempt at suicide; I was outside my wretched marriage, few months of parenthood. Then, after my struggle up from

the gutters, my settling at Neville, the outsideness marked me again. Social life, campus activities, even-perhaps most of allmusic was beyond me. I was not unhappy in my retreat from life. Then I found Laurel. Rather painfully I stole toward her. 

Now I have found a world in which I am becoming-have already become an insider. I am accepted by all kinds and levels of girls, boys, women, men. Not without a note of self-mockery do I look at myself as an admired accompanist of grinding wheels. Definitely I am the best organist in Rolling America.

And for, perhaps, the first time in life, I am catching a share, however slight, of enthusiasm. Dancers consult me on selections, plans for shows are discussed with all the gravity of Princeton's football coach plotting destruction for Yale and Harvard. Mister

Easton is now unknown. Easton has almost gone. Charles is vanishing. And lo! Charlie has arrived. Yes, Charlie. The manager complains, "Charlie, I can't get no one to tune that organ good,” and a skate mechanic says, "When you goin' to put on skates, Charlie?" and the ticket-booth girl when I pause to bid her a good morning, laughs, “Hi, Charlie, that fat friend of yours is waiting inside." Do I like it, Dr. Saunders? I love it. Because 

I'm inside, now. You could say with justice, "But Neville, too, would have welcomed you to the inside." That is true, but you see it is all built up on Laurel. Without her-there would be no inside anywhere for me. 

So this letter expresses my resignation from the Neville Faculty.

I have written so fully because a sensitive man doesn't, with a mere shrug, break many years of association with a just and generous superior. 

With warm regard,

CHARLES EASTON 

Charles dropped the letter in the post-box and the clang of the iron lid was the final beat of the spade tamping down the grave of a period. Almost blithely he strode toward the rink for the afternoon session....

"Laurel sweetheart, "California, August 20 "Well, after a short stay in a Texas camp, your old skating partner is out here on the Coast. Pennsylvania to Texas to California moving west. I'm driving the best damned army truck in the whole army. Take it from me, she's a honey and she don't balk-much. I'd rather drive her through three feet of water and mud than write letters, which is why you haven't heard more from me. But you know I'm thinking of you all the time and when my time in the army is up I'm going to sit in one last crap game and win enough to take a plane to you from wherever I am. 

Your to be waiting for me! In the meantime your to go out on dates when you want to. Maybe I say that because I know that your not much for that. I don't get any chance to skate now. I just get a six inch cake of mud on the truck, and scrape it off again, so that the blasted sargeant will keep his blasted mouth shut. I like the army a little. It gets better as you learn the ropes, and as you get to thinking your really going to fight.

Most of the guys think we are. The talk now is that our outfit will be pulling out of here in a few days and headed for a sea voyage. The talk is all about Hawaii or the Filipeens. Some gabby corporal claims he knows a warehouse guy who spoke of the outfit's stuff being sent to a place called Pearl Harbor. That's where the girls wear grass skirts and dance the hula hula. Well, take it from me, honey, the best looking of the bunch could shake her grass right in my face and I'd still be only seeing you on your skates. I'm trying to keep this letter happy, but I'm kind of down because I'm missing you. Oh, Laurel, we must get married the day I come back, and we'll be the same old dance team we always were. We'll knock 'em dead around the circuit, and then when the rink lights are turned out we'll go to our room and have each other. "So long, Laurel, and keep your rolling and your kisses for me. chin up, and your skates "P.S. Write to me when I send you a sure address." "EDDIE

Laurel's voice, reading to Charles, sounded artificial, falsely precise. "Do you love him?" Charles asked gently.

The artificiality broke. "No. But I-oh, I don't know what 

I feel about him! He is just my old skating partner. But something more than that too. He's-he's just somehow-my Eddie...." 

The summer's swift flight amazed Charles. Once or twice a week he spent the midday two hours on the beach, swimming cautiously with the out-moded breast-stroke of his generation, but for the greater part, lying on his back blinking up into cloudless skies of blazing blue. He recognized with startled resentment that he was badly tired, not so much by the playing on the organ, as by the long hours in the heat of the stage, augmented frightfully by the blaze of the lighting display concentrated on himself. Each session was opened with half the overture played behind the heavy crimson curtains and during these few minutes the console was the center of hell. Sea breezes whispering through the open windows of the huge building helped the skaters, but it was a tired beeze indeed that curled around and up to the stage. Energy seeped out of Charles and, quite helpless, he watched it go. At times he felt a dizziness. Cool autumn, he told himself, would come in time, and then he would go to Elizabeth with Laurel as Henry Stevens had agreed. "If I last that long," he grumbled, and grinned to Harold. "How is the saccharine portrait progressing?" 'Melted away to nothing. Yolanda apparently confused me with a photographer. Regular sittings in the sunny yard

of the trailer confronted her and confounded her. She fled my accursed company." "Well, I have to flee it too. Time for the afternoon overture." He moved away, so slowly that Harold an hour later said to Laurel as they drank orangeade at the counter, “Sit with me for a few minutes. I want to talk to you."

She hesitated. "It's almost time for me to rehearse my pair exhibition with Yolanda. Special exhibition to-night. "The
