Four Flashes' from Long Island and Yolanda and I are to give a half-hour show. The Governor and his staff have reserved seats. A swanky affair. Photographers. Everything. 

Vacationing State executives don't make a practice of dropping in on skating rinks. Mr. Stevens' hand and grunts are mixed in somewhere." "Well! What's your act?" "My Shadow and I.' Yolanda is my shadow. Everything

I do is duplicated by her at shadow-length distance. It's a striking act. The luck is that our two ways of skating are sharply contrasted in just the way we want. Yes, that is good luck. Mine, as you may have noticed, is stylized, solid, formal. Hers is fluttery, fleeting. So as my shadow her movement will be fast, blurred as a shadow's is. Her faults will contribute to the act. And don't think she doesn't know this. 

Yolanda is extremely honest. And she'll accentuate it. We're on for seven minutes." "I'll come in to see it. What time?” "Ten. At half-past, public skating is resumed." "Look here, Laurel, have you looked closely at your old man in the past two weeks or so?” "Don't be silly. I see him all the time.” 

The four chins were thrust close to her face. "Well, I suggest that you take a deep look. He could make your shadow

duet with Yolanda into a trio by adding his shadow, for that is what he is getting to be. He's lost his color-" 

Laurel thrust one chin close to the pendulous four. "That's crazy talk. He's tan from the beach!" 

This Harold dismissed with a wave of his heavy hand as he scolded, "Sun tan! Merely a scum of paint on cracking parchment. I tell you the old fool is drained out, washed out, dehydrated. It's the heat, of course, and the long hours and no day off. I know you aren't God, so the heat can not be attended to, and I know you aren't Hank Stevens, so the hours can't be changed, but you could take a gander at his off-hours, his eating habits, his sleeping quarters. Seriously, Laurel, your father could do with a bit of watching. Have you seen his bedroom?" "No." "It's a furnace a few feet under a roof that has been baked all day by the sun." "So is mine." "You are twenty-four. He is fifty-six.” "That isn't old." "Hm. Much you know about that. Well, I've spoken my piece. Take a close look at him the next time he comes down from the stage." "Mr. Slade, you are one of two friends my father has. I would speak as frankly to Margaret Peters as I shall now to you. He has done an extremely foolish thing. To spend his summer in this-from his view-madhouse, was bad enough, but at least there was the prospect of his return to quiet life. Now that retreat is lost. He leaps from here straight into the larger rink in Elizabeth. I can't realize such an insane act. I have come to-to-" She stopped. "Go on. Say it. To love him."

"To be fond of him." "Christ! You're an honest kid. But honesty is often brutal." "I've grown fond of him, but I wish he had never found me. I travel alone, Mr. Slade. I always have. I don't ask help, and I-I don't know how to give any. I have no room in my plan for nursing any one." "Who speaks of nursing?" "You suggest supervision of eating habits, sleeping quarters. I tell you I have no time, no extra energy for that." "I'm sorry I brought it up," he said hoarsely. "You're not a daughter. You're a cold young bitch." 

Her hand stung his face in a single slap that was neither seen nor heard in the crowd of skaters pouring off the floor, milling around them. The heaviest grasp Laurel had ever felt pulled her down again on the bench from which she had sprung. His forearm lay like a steel bar across her bare legs. "You are a cold young bitch-on the surface. And you are soft and warm in your heart. I don't believe you are fond of your father. I believe you love him. If you don't, why the hell are you crying now? Here, take my handkerchief."

But Laurel dug her knuckles into her eyes. 

Then she muttered, "Yolanda, in one easy flight, has crushed down the ambition of-she is blocking me. I'm forced into a rivalry that never lets up. Not for a minute. 

My kids stick by me, but the public-Mr. Stevens' paying public-goes gaga over her. In every little contemptible detail I have to fight." Her voice rose sharply. "Listen to me!

After the session is over this afternoon, I have a private lesson to give. At six I rush out for coffee and a sandwich. At

six-thirty I'm coming back here to rehearse with Yolanda and "The Four Flashes."" "You could eat more than coffee and a sandwich in that half-hour. Why rush?" “Because I am going to a florist's shop to order flowers sent. Can you guess for whom the flowers are to be sent to the rink? The flowers whose card will be signed Bill, or Tom, or whatever name pops into my mind? Go ahead, guess." 

Harold stared blankly. “No. I can't guess. Not Yolanda?” "She! There'll be armfuls for her. The floor men will skate out to her and load her with blossoms.' "You-you don't mean-Laurel dear, you can't mean—”

She nodded grimly. “But I do mean just that. I'm sending flowers to myself, and flowers must have a card-so, Tom, or 

Bill, or some other empty name. Don't look so shocked. Don't look as though you are going to cry. I'm not sad about it.

I've done it before. It isn't sad. It's just part of my fight. It's just cold common sense. The few flowers I can afford will be dwarfed by hers, but think how it would look to the manager, to Mr. Stevens ogling the Governor, if I got none!... "The Four Flashes' won't get any. It's not their home rink."

Laurel glided from the floor in a backward arabesque, one leg extended high behind her, arms like poised wings, and ten feet away, a second later, Yolanda followed. And even the girls' smiles were contrasted, for Laurel's was printed by her lips, Yolanda's was lavish, free expression. The Governor and his party applauded, the crowd clapped and whistled, and, still in character, the girls skated out slowly to stand in front of "The Four Flashes." Laurel saw floor men gath-

ered at a corner entrance. "Here come the flowers," she thought grimly. "My half-dozen roses will be lost in Yolanda's, but-my God, she's getting a carload!" 

Applause swelled and fell and swelled again. Yolanda's flowers were placed in her graceful arms, laid on the floor before her in bright cornucopias. She was laughing, glancing helplessly at a few cards, sticking her pretty nose into fragrance. A floor man stopped before Laurel, held out the half-dozen roses she had bought and then a larger sheaf in green and frilly paper. Laurel sensed that Yolanda was looking at her, and a cold resentment touched her. Why was she staring? Laurel searched quickly for the card. It couldn't be her father's, for she had carefully avoided, in discussing the show with him, the slightest hint of flowers, thinking of his new life and necessity for strange economies. 

Incredulous she read the narrow card. "To reality from a shadow, Yolanda." Her eyes were hot, stinging. From head to toe she seemed hateful to herself. "Damn you," she sobbed, “for sneering at sweetness. You sneered at that girl for being sweet. You sneered at the word. Sweet. Sweet. 

And that's just what Yolanda is. And she's lovely. And you're what Slade called you, a cold young bitch. A bitch about your father, a bitch about this successful rival. That girl knew she'd get a whole hothouse of flowers, and she knew you would not, and she saw to it that you did." 

Laurel turned to look at Yolanda and tried to smile, but the smile was wrecked on working, bitten lips. A hand touched her arm. Another floor man was handing her two huge fountains of yellow roses. She glanced at one card. 

Slade. She found the other. Slade.

Blindly she skated at Yolanda's side to the dressing-rooms, in her arms Yolanda's flowers. The floor men gathered the

garden the girls left and put it on the stage. The spotlights were turned off, the flood-lights blazed. All-Skate was signaled. 

In their tiny dressing-room the two girls stripped off the costumes and in a moment were naked, ready for the rickety little shower in a curtained corner. Their bodies were gleaming white with sweat. "Yolanda, that was-I guess it was you. All the way. I-I've never made friends with a girl. I want to-with you. A man called me a bitch to-night. He was right. Out on the floor when I got your flowers, I called myself a bitch. I was right. 

We're friends now, Yolanda, if you'll have me.' 

A bright pink flushed Yolanda's face. Her gentian eyes opened wide. "Why, we've been friends all along." She stared. "Unless there's been something underneath that I missed." 

Laurel said grimly, "There was. But there isn't any more.

Then cool splashing in the hot room, and two fast-drying bodies, and enveloping clouds of fragrant powder. 

Yolanda said, "I'm staying in here for a cigarette." "I'm going out to look for Slade.”

IT WAS Margaret Peters who found the furnished apartment in Elizabeth: two small bedrooms on the ground floor, each with a window facing the street, between the bedrooms a small living-room, behind this room a kitchenette and bathroom. "Here,” she said to Charles one afternoon in mid-October, "you will be happier than you have ever been in all your life. Now, for God's sake, don't move into it to-night as though you were coming into the waiting-room of a bus terminal. You and Laurel. What a pair! Born migrants." 

Charles protested. "Migrants? Why, until last summer I've never stirred out of Princeton. And Laurel never leaves her rinks. Migrants!" "Austere bachelors then. Neither of you has seen one material value in the result of my exhausting search in a city strange to me. I've found the perfect place, but you two must make it a home. You, Charles, dropped in yesterday and, fatuously happy, could visualize only Laurel, who didn't even bother to show up for a preview. When she comes in after the night session, let's see, six and a half hours from now, she'll look indifferently at the nice livingroom, demand, 'Which is my bedroom?' bid you good-night and vanish. Honestly I think that girl would curl up in a 242

corner of an empty warehouse and fall into sleep of dream skating. She's impossible. Well, I expect it in her. But you are worse, for you'll be sitting in that comfortable chair while she gives private lessons, class lessons, builds absurd properties for her shows, and munches sandwiches at the rink." 

Charles laughed at Margaret's indignation. "I'll bet you're sorry I ever barged into that cut-rate emporium of yours on Thirty-fourth Street."

This she shrugged away. “Breakfast, midnight snacks, will be all you'll use the kitchen for, of course, except," she glared, "when I come over from New York to dine with you two. And I'm coming at least twice a month." "That's grand. But-it'll be for delicatessen-store dinners." "It will not. We'll eat decent food cooked on the premises. 

"By whom? I can't cook. And if Laurel can, she won't." "By me." "Margaret, I'm a fool.” "You are. All kinds of one. But just now what?" "I ought to marry you." 

Waiting for her gathering reply he appraised silently this woman's vitality, brains, smooth good looks, warm friendliness, and for an instant he wondered why he was making a dull joke of such a thought. An instant of serious speculation. Then with relief, with regret, he watched the instant vanish.

Margaret inquired coldly if this was a proposal. "It is not." "Then I won't have to reject you. Now look. I've purchased linen, towels, other necessities that Laurel has ignored. You'll get the itemized bill in a few days. But there's

one thing I haven't done. Stocked a microscopic medicine chest. That's up to you. I know nothing of this part of life. 

My health is disgusting." "Mine too. And Laurel's. All she ever needs is sleep, and a grain of common sense in her eating." "For her I agree. No medicine chest. The first aid room at the rink attends to her cuts and bruises. But you-Charles, frankly, I don't like your color. It used to be almost florid.” "Rot." "Perhaps. But there are, I read in the papers, vitamin pills, and vitamin concentrates of all description." "Let's run out now for dinner. I noticed a restaurant down the street." "Don't cut me off that way. I don't like it.” 

Charles grinned. “Come on."

They ate in a quiet, dimly lighted restaurant. 

While they lingered over coffee, cigarettes, Margaret asked how Laurel was getting along with Jim Brennan. 

Charles shrugged. "As you would expect. That jelly-fish! 

And the fool resents the very best that is in her. Her work. 

God, he is stupid! Instead of welcoming her professional energies, even using them to his own advantage, he resents them and just continues his sullen thwarting. When she goes over his head once in a while to Hank Stevens, oh then he yields and seeks cover. But afterward he comes out and nags again." "But why?" "Can one understand imbecility? Jealousy, I suppose, about prerogative. If Laurel needs light on a dull afternoon when she's giving a private lesson in the empty rink after the session, Brennan expects her to come to his office and go through a formal rigmarole of request, and if he isn't

there, then she should search the building for him. Well, there the pupil waits, and you know Laurel. She skates to the light control room and pulls switches, and goes to work. 

Brennan rages. One dark day last week he cut off the lights in three-fourths of the rink and snapped to her, 'You don't need the whole length for figures, Laurel. Light costs money.' Laurel went on with the lesson and, presently, teaching dance steps, was in the gloom of the vast unlighted part. 

She was angry and discouraged and, worst of all, humiliated, for that day's pupil was an unpleasant person who hinted sharply that dancing instructresses in ROLLING AMERICA evidently were small fry." "What did Laurel say to the unpleasant person?" "What could she say? There they were circling in the gloom where faulty details were hard to detect. And she can not keep peppering away at Stevens, making a constant nuisance of herself. She holds him in reserve for deeper trouble. 

Yolanda is incredulous and tells Laurel she's a fool to put up with it. They had dinner together in Trenton the other night. Yolanda is stationed in the Trenton rink." "Does she suggest remedy?" "No, because she can't even visualize herself in such a situation. She just scolds Laurel and tells her it's her own fault. "Tact, Laurel, that's all you need to use.' "Is it?" Margaret asked. 9 99 "Wee-ll, Laurel isn't tactful. She's so direct. Besides,

Yolanda's conception of tact would be for a girl to butter up Brennan with smiles, sweetness, glamour, or to ignore him completely, disobey him, flout him, making Stevens choose between the two. If the Great God Stevens chose 

Brennan, Yolanda would simply laugh, resign, and dance away on her pretty legs to another outfit. Or she would go

Margaret gathered up her things. "We'd better run along to the rink. I want to catch Laurel for ten minutes of business talk."

They walked the half-mile to the building that loomed in the clear light of the October evening. Margaret, vigorous, eager to swing freely along, noticed with some concern 

Charles' tendency to lag. "He is going to swallow the pills I send," she stormed to herself. "He fusses like an old hen over his precious daughter and ignores the plainest evidence of his own need." She was down the rink, smoothly stroking, in a flash of speed, and presently Margaret changed her face, form, manner, even her thought, to those of Camille La Tourette, as whom she was introduced to Jim Brennan. And Laurel's maneuver said to Margaret, "You can do what I can't. This dope would turn down sheer gold if the offer came from me. 

So Camille La Tourette gave the manager her brief, business smile of courtesy, and said, "I wanted to interest you and Miss Easton in what is, without question, an advantageous stroke of business. I am a business woman who buys from the theater and sells to the public. It is highly specialized work. I buy women's clothes that are used in modern plays. Only technically second-hand, only technically costumes. I've seen and enjoyed two shows that Miss Easton has directed in your rink and with these in mind I stepped out of my territory and purchased a dozen ballet costumes which I can sell to ROLLING AMERICA at unbelievable bargain. Would you be interested?"

"No," said Jim, "ROLLING AMERICA rents its show costumes from Brooks. We don't buy."

With an intended shade of impatience Camille La Tourette said, "Of course I understand that. Costumes for reviews are not bought for single performances, or even for a week of performance. But you see, Mr. Brennan,” her

voice softened, "I do not rent. You can have these beautiful things for the cost of renting. And they can be used in shows at all the rinks time after time whenever ballets are produced. I am willing to sell them so cheap because they are, as I said, out of my line. I like your rink, I skate here when my work permits, and when I thought of what these costumes would mean to one of your shows I bought them. My motive was impulsive, but I can assure you my valuation was coldly statistical. I can easily sell them elsewhere for a higher price. But, well, you know how it is with amateur skaters. They grow fond of a rink, actually loyal to it, and they like to help. I've admired Miss Easton's productions and this seemed, in a sense, a sort of contribution." "That's smart salesmanship, Miss La Tourette." 

She smiled pleasantly. "It is. And I can tell you that if I were really trying to put over a business deal for accustomed profit, I'd use this exact line with you. As you say, it is smart.

Margaret paused, watching Laurel's face suddenly grow very sober. "What are you thinking about?" "A newspaper route, a girl in pants, all the rest of my fight. Last year's room at Mrs. Green's. Then she gave a mad laugh. “Laurel, while I've been describing to you the delight and necessity of soft, cheerful environment I've forgotten something. But

I remember in college an economics prof who lectured on the soundness of investments and who lost his small savings in a swindle which a child should have suspected.
