The organ, hidden by the crimson curtains, breathed one deep warning. Laurel skated out for the opening formation in the empty arena.

THE WINTER skating season brought few changes to the rink personnel. The floor men Harry, Pete and Jerry were taken into the army; Ben, the door man, was transferred to the New Brunswick rink. Margaret Murphy, the former 

Rockette with the heart-shaped face and magnificent skating legs, was brought from Trenton to replace Alice, who was sent to the Newark rink. Minor ripples on the smooth glis tening surface of ROLLING AMERICA. 

On December seventh Laurel was teaching young Beatrice the Stag Jump when Jim Brennan grasped her arm. "Pearl Harbor's been bombed by the Japs! Just came over the radio in my office." "That's war, isn't it, Mr. Brennan? Oh, Beatrice, faster on the first jump." "Of course it's war. Want to listen in the office? There'll be more coming in." "No." "Aren't you interested? You're sure a cold gal.” "Yes. I'm interested. In the huge fact. The rest will come later in deluges. I'm to skate at a naval training station Monday night in New York. I've worked up an act with Margaret. Yes. I'm interested. You know what our days off mean, and mine are almost dated solid for camps. That's not coldness, is it, Mr. Brennan?” 253

So this tidal wave in turbulent America was thinned down in the backwaters of the nation. Race-tracks, the theater, golf tournaments, all the amusements of the country continued for the common good, and gladly, step by step, adjusted the war harness of taxes, benefit performances, war bonds and stamps. Laurel, Margaret Murphy, and Charles rode midnight trains and buses and tumbled into their beds with the wild applause, the yells and whistles of soldiers, sailors, marines, echoing in their ears. On one such night, a few days before Christmas when she had gone alone to

Camp Dix and skated to the music of an army band, Laurel stopped at the rink just before its closing to leave her skates to be ground by the skate mechanic. She found a letter from Eddie in her dressing-room. 

Laurel Sweetheart, November 30, An Emerald Isle in a Sapphire Sea. (I got this out of a book.)

You know why I don't write more. I'm just no good at it, and 

I'm not so sure the letters get through. If your writing to me it's waste of ink and paper for I'm not getting the letters. But keep it up anyway, for I want to hear from you so badly and they'll catch up with me somewhere....

Laurel, alone in the dressing-room, flushed. Only two letters to Eddie. Written to addresses he had probably left even before she mailed the sketchy lines. .... In spite of war talk this seems such a dreamy place. Of course we work like hell, but when the day is over, I just have moonlight and harbor water and no Laurel. I'm sorry I'm in such a tame place. Gosh, it's a regular Riveera like in a book. 

The men get edgy. I think fighting would be better. After all,

that's what a army's for. Weak beer, shooting a game of pool, walking with a man, smelling tropical flowers, well, that ain't what young Eddie likes. If I got to be a soldier, well, bring on something to shoot at. I was seasick on the way here. Now I'm feeling fine, full of panther sweat. Oh, what I'd give tonight to be on skates rolling down the floor with you, and we'd be married too. I love you, Laurel. My outfit is a swell gang. Best in the army. Regular guys. Rarin' and ready to go. Bring on the bloody Germans and the bragging Japs! Uncle Sam's ready. So's your Eddie with his blasted truck.

But I'm tired already of this place. Too much moonlight and hot nights and no you to kiss if you'd let me. Everything quiet.... 

Laurel whispered, "I'd let you, Eddie, oh, I'd let you.” ....

This is a long letter for me. I've just read it over as far as 

I've went, and I find I haven't even said where I am. Well, this emerald isle in a sapphire sea is Hawaii and the station is at 

Pearl Harbor....

The letter fell to the floor, was whisked up, and read to the end, but everything after "the station is at Pearl Harbor" was, to Laurel, inconsequential. Her eyes were sad. 

That mad brilliant skating boy. All impulse, steel muscles, warmth. Pearl Harbor. Missing? Wounded? Dead? Alive?

No casualty lists were appearing beyond blunt horrible numbers. Perhaps already his next of kin had been notified. He had mentioned, that night so long ago in June, his brief visit to an aunt in Connecticut, his only close relative. Perhaps she had been notified by the government. Eddie hadn't even given her name.... 

That night in June. Her drab bedroom. Eddie in uniform tired out, lying on the bed, eager for her. "Come on, Laurel, lie down just a few minutes. It'll be like old times. A little petting. We've never gone further. God knows when I'll see

you again." The clearly remembered words! And then his whisper, "Please, Laurel.” The blue eyes in the tanned face, filled with excitement and desire. How vivid the blue and excitement had been to her! How deadening to him had been the cool green of her eyes in her pale face, filled with wise and considered denial! Yes, that night in June. Now she was going to her new bedroom, a warm room, pretty, dainty, so different from the cold lair at Mrs. Green's. But which place would she prefer now, if that June night could be relived? The lonesome apartment filled with warmth? 

The bare room filled with Eddie's love? Which? the persistent question nagged her....

She shivered, crammed the letter into the pocket of her gray fur coat, and the shiver came from the breath of coldness expressing the answer of controlled mind: if the sad 

June night could be relived she would prefer the same outcome-denial. How cold she was! Desperately she asked herself one more question. If, when Eddie had called her to the bed, she had imagined a Pearl Harbor, would she then have yielded and given to him? Laurel stood still, as though a voice would give an answer in physical sound, but when the answer came it was mere realization that, in all probability, she would not have given. And, the answer accepted, she was conscious of self-contempt and self-admiration hopelessly mixed. Poor Eddie. She did not love him. He was the man who had skated so close, so very close to her. And that was all.

Laurel pulled the fur tighter around her, switched off the light in the dressing-room, and hurried through the passage, to be stopped by her father's voice made loud in the microphone beside the organ. "Ladies and Gentlemen, our National Anthem."

The big flag descended slowly from the ceiling of the stage, and to the unfolded color of its stars and stripes, while the standing hundreds faced it, she heard with throbbing sadness her father play the Star-Spangled Banner. "That's for you, Eddie,” she muttered. “Alive or wounded, missing, or dead. That's for you. Play it, Dad, while all over the country, millions stand and think of their own Eddies." 

The last deep chord flung out, and with it the greatness of the instant passed, and again there was a skating rink and noisy youngsters taking off their skates.

The bathroom was steamy, scented with lilac salts, and in the deep tub Laurel lay, idly running her hands along her soapy thighs, lazily watching the suds tide seep slowly between her rather small round breasts made pink and glistening by the hardly endurable-but doubly enjoyable-hot water. “Lassitude deluxe," she said. “For the first time in my life. And it's disgraceful that I should enjoy it an hour after reading Eddie's letter. How can I enjoy it? How can I enjoy anything? But I'm so tired and so wonderfully hot and so clean."

Later, standing, adjusting the shower for the rinse, she smiled at her vague form in the steam-blurred mirror. She wasn't as thin as she had been last summer. A slight gauntness had vanished from her square shoulders, hollows from her cheeks. And, because she knew that she was working harder than ever, she realized that the comfort of the apartment was responsible. The soft, perfectly constructed bed gave to her body night rest she had never known before.

The healthful lazing before getting into bed, the lazing before dressing for the day. A vigorously used body absorbed such luxury, put it to its own uses, and flesh replaced the

gauntness and the hollows of overwork. Gone the winter icebox of a room.

The warm shower stripped her body of lather and in the slightly clearing mirror Laurel took further pleasure. She thought of Slade, now in his Princeton glade. If he could see her now would he think so much of painting Yolanda? 

Wouldn't these strong legs, supple waist, nice breasts, wellmuscled arms, impress him professionally? 

From artist her thought flashed in another direction. Men.

In cloud of fragrant powder she wondered for a little while about her life without men. What, at twenty-four, was she losing? In this yellow light, in the now almost cleared mirror, her naked body seemed to possess a genuine loveliness of white. Widely set eyes were soft, warm, her dusky hair a lure. Men craved all this that was her reflection in the mirror. How easy it would be for her to smile at a man she found attractive! A smile, a gesture, both slight, and that body limber, strong, strangely sweet to-night, would be warmed, loved beyond the rather bleak excitements she had guardedly permitted at narrow quarter-hours and in awkward places. Was she living half a life? In cold shadow while young women like Yolanda romped in brilliant sunlight or in the deceiving silver of a moon. No man's lips had kissed her breasts, no man's furious will had leaped- 

Laurel, paling, thrust up from the perfumed sweetness to stare into the mirror, to search for, and instantly find, the stable level of her career. Her legs strong for skating. Her body tireless for work. Her eyes calm and filled with judgment for the improvement of young Beatrice, little Jean, the rest of her youngsters at the rink. To-morrow morning she would practise free style in the empty rink, hang carefully in the storeroom the ballet costumes Margaret had at

last sold to Brennan. Ah! This was better. Distrusted dazzling mood was banished. 

She slipped her pajamas on, pulled a dark-blue robe around her, and passed through the living-room, intending a quiet good-night to Charles nodding over a new mystery novel. But something in the worn-coin quality of his slanted face, something in the hunched shoulders, changed her habitual course. She hesitated a moment, then moved to him and, bending swiftly, kissed his cheek. "Go to bed, Dad. It's late." "Why, Laurel!"

She took with her to the very brink of sleep his expression of complete and delighted surprise.

Margaret Peters surveyed with approval the small Christmas tree in the corner of the Eastons' living-room. Angels, stars, reindeer, balls and candles in all the tinsel gaiety of tradition were enchantingly suspended in its green depths; icicles, snow, added the touch of frozen glitter. Margaret's swift thought surveyed, too, the turkey in the oven, the potatoes she had mashed, the dressing, gravy, turnips, celery, olives, ice-cream, coffee. She surveyed, too, the divan in the room on which Laurel, insistent, would sleep on this 

Christmas Eve while Margaret slept in Laurel's bed. She glanced at the clock on the mantel, and the twenty minutes past midnight brought added approval of the whole intimate situation. She smiled. What an hour for a Christmas dinner!

Laurel and Charles would be home in a few minutes, after the prolonged Christmas Eve skating session. She knew they had been in the rink since early morning helping

with decoration, working out special music, snatching a bite at noon, a sandwich for supper. To-morrow the rink would be thronged afternoon and night. And so on, all through the holidays. So, Christmas dinner at one in the morning.

A single anxiety disturbed Margaret's approval of all she had accomplished. Would this, strange to her and somehow very dear to her, father and daughter come in too tired for an elaborate hour? She frowned. The daughter, no. The father, perhaps. But the tree, the frivolous dining-table, the holly at the windows, would pick him up. Afterward he would sleep the 'better for it. 

The door clicked in the vestibule. There they were!

And there, presently, on the table was the turkey and what went so superbly with it. But the table for a while seemed strange for Christmas. War. And in the middle of the dinner a fourth voice came: five minutes of broadcast news. Then, quite suddenly, the subject was deliberately tamped and quiet celebration resumed. Skating, the organ, Margaret's latest business accomplishment, sustained their interest. Sipping a cup of coffee she declared must be her last, Margaret said, "Has either of you noticed an element of this Christmas dinner common to the three of us? You, Laurel?" "No." "Charles?" "No. Except pleasure. And the three opinions that a marvelous cook." you are "It is more serious than that, and at the same time, happier. Laurel, what were you doing about this hour last year?" 

Laurel replied promptly, "Opening a florist's box filled with roses I didn't know were from you. Returning un-

opened my father's gift. Later, cutting out paper dolls for later costume suggestion." "And for several Christmases before that?” "I don't remember clearly." "But usually you were alone?" "Yes."

Margaret's eyes were softer than the others had ever seen them. "That's enough for you, Laurel. Now, Charles." 

He drew deeply on his pipe. "A comfortable night. Like any other in my recent years. Nothing out of the ordinary." "You were alone?" 

Charles nodded. "That's enough for you, Charles. Now I'll give a thumbbox sketch of my Christmas Eve of last year and of many of the years before. I was coming back to my hotel from a restaurant or night club after the theater. Companions had been brittle, sufficiently drunken and gay. You notice I say companions, not friends. So I was really alone."

Charles stared at her across the littered table, said quietly, "I catch your common element. Each of us-alone, on 

Christmas Eves before to-night. This common element makes the magic hour richer." "I think so," Margaret said, and rose abruptly. “Now, Laurel, the presents. You give them out to your elderly inferiors."

Gay forms of white and green and crimson paper were unwrapped, exclaimed over. Thrillers in gruesome jackets for Charles, Caron's sweetpea in graceful bottle, large bag in red morocco, for Margaret, a green corduroy skating dress for Laurel from Margaret, and from her father almost a duplicate in wine color. 

Laurel cried, "My costumes have always been made

with eyes close to cost. These two are the kind I've never, well, just gone out and bought.” She waved her arm widely, taking in the warmth, light, the decorated table, "All this is being rich for me." 

Charles smiled. "All this is being rich for me. It's taken fifty-six years for riches to come." 

Laurel gave a mocking laugh. "And you've chucked your work, your certainty of comfortable retirement. Just tossed 'em overboard." "I said rich. I meant real riches. They're here in this apartment. Yes, and they are up on that stage at the rink even when I'm banging out Pop-eye, the Sailor Man, Whoop, Whoop!" "Oh, if you are going to get serious, sentimental, then I can say that I've been rich for the years I've had eight little wheels instead of two feet."

Charles started to speak, then moved to answer the doorbell. "More sentiment,” he said, holding out to Laurel the yellow telegram. She read the florid greeting from Princeton. "It's as if his hoarse voice were in the room, Dad. What a monster! You must run over to see him sometime. I-I guess he's rich, too, according to your preposterous standards." "You know they are true, Laurel." "Yes." 

Margaret moaned. "And what of me? Poor old woman from New York, permitted for one gala night to visit the wealthy." "You're a big slice of Easton riches," Charles said. "Isn't that enough for you? You aren't the adjective, you're the noun. You can't be everything in grammar." "Now, look here, Charles my lad, don't come it over me

with any of your professorial bilge. You're now and forever an organist in a rink. Your fancy days are over. You are

Charlie of the rink, not Mr. Charles Easton of the Neville faculty. Oh, don't grin so fatuously. You're an idiot wherever you are. But a nice one. Isn't he, Laurel?" "But definitely," Laurel said, and then instantly veered to her own world. "I think I'll wear the green corduroy tomorrow afternoon, the wine color at night.” "And pajamas within twenty minutes. We're all half asleep. You two tired from the rink, I from your blasted kitchen. Come, Laurel, we'll icebox the remnants, and leave the clearing for the morning."

So it was ordered, and after a little while, one by one, the room doors were closed, but over the two beds and the made-up divan, single lights burned on while three occupants read with Christmas Eve forgotten. Margaret in

Laurel's bed studied the dramatic notices of a play she had seen the night before and marked down for failure; Laurel, propped on one elbow, followed the intricate, printed designs of a new dance step; and Charles peered with some anxiety at the figures in his bank statement. His was the first light out. Then Margaret's. And it was almost three o'clock when Laurel's bare arm reached up to turn the switch.

The febrile rush of skaters lessened after the holidays were concluded with a New Year's Eve party in a decorated and tumultuous rink. From that night's explosion Charles literally swayed his way home to bed. Even Laurel admitted exhaustion and omitted the removal of red from her lips, the application of cream to her face. The new year fell into its

pattern of afternoon and night sessions with, for Laurel, the usual mornings of instruction. Her exhibitions for the armed forces were continued. Yolanda on two occasions joined her in a pair act at Camp Dix near Trenton, but Charles was kept at the rink. 

One night toward the end of January Henry Stevens was waiting for competition skating in his New Brunswick rink.

It was a preliminary contest, an elimination affair for amateur skaters of this rink only, who if they gained first, second or third place, would qualify for the finals among the seven rinks. Mr. Stevens was angry and presently a more responsive victim than a chewed black cigar was necessary to his continuance on earth. All the better, he thought, when the victim was the culprit. So presently from the local manager's office the general manager growled into a telephone, “Hello, Stevens talking. Who's this? Ugh. Get Laurel. What's that? 

I don't care what she's doing, or who she's skating with. Put her on the wire." He waited, his choleric eyes bulging, stomach bulging, short fat legs bulging. "That you, Laurel? 

I'm talkin' from New Brunswick. Are you enjoying yerself to-night? I said, are you enjoyin' yerself?... Why ain't you here?... Oh, nothin' at all. Just dance competition going on. 

That's all. And Yolanda judging alone.... What's that?... 

You didn't know nothin' about coming over to judge?... 

Didn't Brennan tell you?... Put Brennan on.' 

Mr. Stevens waited, in one ear the empty hum of the wire, in the other the grind of the rink beyond the door, and between the two the roar of anger in his head. “That you, Jim? Stevens. Why ain't Laurel here judgin' these dances? ... You guess you forgot to tell her! Suppose Blake had forgot to tell Yolanda to come over from Trenton! Maybe the skate mechanic would have judged. Is Laurel head instruc-

tress at Elizabeth or is she the ladies'-room attendant? Now listen! Get her in a cab an' over here! I'll have Joe Clark hold the competition back for exactly three-quarters of an hour. Step on it!" He set the instrument down with a smash and stamped out to the arena. "Laurel's on her way," he growled to Yolanda. "Tell Joe to hold up the competition until she gets here. Then come in the office." 

When Yolanda, very lovely in a dark brown winter dress, was seated opposite him at the table, all the activity left Mr. Stevens as quickly as it had come. He subsided to his customary staccato speech with punctuating grunts. "Hear yer gettin' yerself married," he said calmly.

Yolanda half rose from her chair, but he waved her down. "When, Yolanda?” "How do you know? I've told no one!" "Know the lucky man. Saw him yesterday in New York. 

On his way West. Why're you marryin' him?” 

Almost recovered, she managed, "He's my big moment, Mr. Stevens." "Yes. An' he owns three rinks in Nevada. That anythin' to do with it?” 

Yolanda laughed. "You manage seven; he owns three-if you insist on the commercial side." "Leavin' me flat. Just goin' to walk out on me. No warning, no nothing. Just go, eh?" "That's not true. And you know it. I'd planned to give you plenty of notice. I'm not eloping, you know. Sam, the darling, isn't the eloping kind." "It's a rotten trick. You was goin' to draw yer money up to the last day an' then walk out." His face was blotched with blood and returning anger. "I know yer kind. All sparkle an' front. No heart. No loyalty."

Yolanda laughed. "For forty-five a week and extras!

ROLLING AMERICA is the meanest, tightest outfit I ever worked for." 

Mr. Stevens' fist crashed on the table, lay with whitened knuckles.

At once she reached out and rubbed it gently, and presently held it tight. "Listen, you old tyrant. You couldn't make me hate you if you really tried. All this huffing and puffing doesn't mean a thing. You're a big, bluffing baby. And what is this nonsense about my letting you down?" "Leavin' sometime in the middle of the winter season, ain't you? Do you call that not letting me down? Final competitions coming up in a month. Shows comin' up in six weeks. What in Christ's name is that, if it ain't stabbin' me in the back?”

Gently Yolanda began again to soothe the trembling hand on the table. When it was quiet she raised blue limpid eyes to his and was dismayed to find that actual, incredible tears were rolling down his flaccid cheeks. "I'm done in," he muttered. "I guess I can't take it any more. The whole racket's beginnin' to get me. I've lost my grip." "Henry, that decides me. I'm quitting tonight, after judging." "Go ahead. Maybe I'm crazy all of a sudden. Maybe I ain't hearing things right. You're quittin' tonight, you, the head instructress of all my rinks?" "Yes. And I'm going to do you a favor before I go.'

Gently, unaffectedly, almost without consciousness of it, she kept on rubbing the puffy hand. "Will you turn the rink over to me, the management of it and you, for just

three minutes of telephone time? I'm going to do something for you you'll never forget. There's only one condition I make.' 

He said nothing, staring blurredly at their clasped hands. "You're tired, Henry Stevens.' 

"You're God-damned right I am," he said slowly.

She got up quickly, went to the door, opened it an inch. "Listen." 

The grind of hundreds of skates filled the room, and singing voices, and, under all, the deep diapason of the organ. 

Singing Foursomes. Yolanda closed the door, came back and covered his hand with hers. "Hear that racket out there? That's the country letting its hair down, laughing, singing, all over the place. These people will be working to-morrow in shipyards and munition plants, and the soldiers and gobs are having a night ashore. And the school kids who buy war stamps at your ticket booth. This rink is your part in war. You're like a baseball manager. Like a theater manager in Moscow. Sure, Henry, that's you. No, you aren't crazy or done in. You aren't even tired. You're a pretty big little guy. That outside the door depends on you. Going to quit? Not you. 

When Laurel and I skate at the camps on our own time, without a dime of pay, we're in the thing ourselves. But you're our boss and so you even come barging into that. 

Humph. Fat little Henry Stevens whose rinks are-to coin a phrase, darling-the apple of his eye. So you're going to quit, eh? To rat on the whole works?" "Yolanda."

She whipped out a wisp of lace and leaned across the table and carefully dabbed his cheeks. Then she kissed him on the mouth.

She said, “Sam wouldn't object. He would know it was because I admire you for the way you run your rinks. He's a rink man all the way. So are you. Now get Brennan on the wire. I'm taking over for the three minutes you promised me. 

"I didn't." "But you do. Get Brennan. And bark, Henry, and grunt the things I whisper, and rumble your throat so he won't hear the whisper. "That you, Jim?... Stevens.... Laurel left yet?...

Tell her old man to turn on the Clarovox, and to come here on the double... an' to bring a skating costume for her, the silver one... and her skates.... Now, God damn it, don't bother me. I'm sending Helen from here. She'll take over yer organ. Keep the damned juke-box screamin' till she gets there.... G'by." 

He turned to Yolanda. "Any more orders?" 

She smiled demurely. "Once again I'm all froth and champagne, Mr. Stevens, as they say around the rinks. And, as you said, 'All front and sparkle. No heart. No loyalty." "You are a God-damned liar." "And you?" “Oh, I'm just an old son-of-a-bitch." "That's quite true. They're usually nice. Sam's one too. 

By the way, when Charles Easton arrives, breathless and bewildered, I'd suggest-suggest, you notice-that you say nothing to him beyond a grumbled order to play, when the proper moment comes, the loudest fanfare ever heard in any rink. And nothing to Laurel. Not a word." "O-oh!" He rose heavily. “So that's it! So- Get out o' here, Yolanda. Get out! An' extra cabs to take Helen to Elizabeth, an' Charlie here. Couldn't Helen play a fanfare?”

"Not quite as madly as Charles Easton will play it-for his daughter. It's long overdue for that girl. Long, long overdue. She's something, Mr. Manager. Quite something." “Get out. I gotta write a official announcement. And what about you? Regret about losin' you? I gotta get that in. 

Yolanda laughed. "Forget that to-night. It can come later.

I had my night. This one is Laurel's." She moved to the door, laughed at him again with delight in the laugh, stood very straight and held a white arm high above her blond wave of hair. Then she pronounced gravely, 

"Ladies and Gentlemen, ROLLING AMERICA takes great pride and pleasure in announcing the appointment of its new head instructress of all the rinks, its own talented skater who has come up from the ranks and earned the position.' Here Joe pauses, Mr. Stevens. Then again, 'Ladies and Gentlemen, give welcome to our own-Laurel Easton.' "And then, Mr. Stevens, though her father's fanfare on the organ will be loud, terribly loud, maybe his hands will falter a bit on the keys. But you won't notice it, for maybe for the second time to-night you'll be crying a little, and

I won't notice it, for I know damned well I'll be shaky, and 

Laurel?-she won't even see the rink. Charles Easton? He won't need a cab back to Elizabeth. He'll need an ambulance."

The car sped through the winter night, its windows frosted. Laurel was nervous, fretful, and at the same time, in some way she did not fully understand, stimulated. This matter of being sent for on a minute's notice was usual enough. And on the depressing side was the fact that, though it was Brennan's fault, she had nevertheless missed a fun-

damentally important engagement. Except teaching, nothing in rink life was placed above judging. And Mr. Stevens' fury could be accurately gaged by the crestfallen manner and appearance of Brennan. No. A pleasant atmosphere was not at the end of this careering cab ride. 

But the strange, faint stimulation remained and the best Laurel could make of it was that she was realizing again that she had made something of herself. Mr. Stevens, the head of it all, the man who had ignored her, pushed her aside from the coveted and earned position of ROLLING AMERICA's premier instructress, was postponing a contest until she should arrive. She. The little waif of newspaper route, dime store, cleaning and dyeing plant. This was a thought to launch her spirits flying out of the cab window and on to the great rink in New Brunswick, where-her eyes caught again the expression of worry-the chief would grunt at her, and growl something like, “Yolanda never misses. If we can't depend on our girls—yes, I know Brennan didn't tell you, but maybe it's your fault too fer not gettin' along with him better...."

Also there was Eddie. No word from him since that letter postmarked November thirtieth. Pearl Harbor. Eddie's "Emerald Isle in a Sapphire Sea." His too dreamy place. 

Tame. His "Riveera." Moonlight and water and no Laurel. 

Then the vile breath of Japan. And silence...

Emotions in the jolting cab were badly mixed, and the frost on the windows, shutting out even the white shadows of fields, exaggerated her loneliness and anxiety. Laurel, impatient at this mood, reminded herself that presently, pad and pencil in hand, she would be out in the arena, jotting down the detailed technicalities of the Ice Polka, the Fourteen Step, the Tango and the Mohawk Waltz, putting her

dark head close to Yolanda's gold as they whispered and judged. Old Hank could chew his cigar and scowl and grunt and the devil take him! 

But when at last the cab stopped in front of the blaze of neon and revolving wheels of light and she had hurried in, and the door man had told her to go straight to the office, she began to dread the basilisk stare of the employer in a non-unionized industry. The harsh words-basiliskemployer-industry-rang in her thought, undermining her cool self-confidence in skates, training, professional skill. 

Hesitating outside the office door she fought this uneasiness. 

Down with it! At the other side of that door, plumped at the table, sat the man who had introduced her so long agoalmost a year-to the shoe manufacturer who made the Sue 

Holle skates. And Stevens had led her to believe that, after 

Sue's departure, there would be the Laurel Easton skates.

He had led her straight to the outpost of the citadel and when, later, she was actually on the floor in front of the stage, certain of the announcement, he had produced in a cruel flashing instant the strange girl from the West, Yolanda, who had floated out to capture, on one appearance, without effort, the hearts of the skaters. Laurel stiffened. 

Yolanda was a friend. But that brute- She knocked, opened the door. 

Yes, the basilisk stare was there, all right. And the grunts came thick and fast. "Well, Laurel. I'm beginnin' to think you Eastons are all the same. Has your father hired a hearse to get here in? 

I told Brennan to send him over in a hurry. But, Lord God-" "My father, Mr. Stevens, is never late when he is given time to-"

"Never mind about your father," Mr. Stevens snarled. "Now listen, Laurel. When he gets here, which ought to be quick, he's got yer skates and a costume with him. You wait out at the entrance for him, an' the minute he comes in you grab what he's got and get into 'em and out on the floor. Tell your father to switch off the Clarovox an' stand by at the organ. Now beat it. Come here."

Bewildered she stood before him. He rose, caught her shoulder roughly. "Yer a good kid, Laurel. And I know just how good. Now get the hell out of here.”

She gave her own smile that came and vanished with as little warmth as the sun's glint on a shallow stream overhung with a lace of foliage, and hurried to the entrance of the rink to stand composed, talking with late arrivals. When 

Charles came, immaculately groomed and dressed as he always was, quite composed, only mildly curious, she snatched skate box, costume and hurried to her dressing-room. There 

Yolanda found her stripped in a cloud of powder, struggling into the tight silver costume that gleamed and flickered in the bright light. When she stood up at last the brief silver skirt seemed a line of metal on the rounding white flesh of her thighs. 

Yolanda said, "You'll do." "For what? Perhaps you'll tell me. Street dresses are always worn by competition judges. You have one on. Well, I'm to judge too, yet there's all this business." She waved at her silvered body. "What's up?" "It's all blank to me." "And old Hank grabbed me by the shoulder and informed me that I was a good kid. That's equivalent to a kiss from 

His Royal Roughness." "Rape, no less."

Laurel grinned. "I could swear he was ready to weep at something." "Sentimental old ass. If there's anything I hate in life it's a droopy, emotional employer. I prefer them tough." "Why, Yolanda! They don't come tougher than Hank.

He's built his whole seven-rink circuit on grunts and grumbles.' "That's just a front. A protection against a natural reservoir of slops and tears. His method is a good bit like yours. Oh, yes, don't stare so innocently! Your frost, distance, are your covering too. 

"Covering against what?" "Against getting hurt since you were a kid. Say, Laurel, before they bang on this door there's one thing I'd like to say. Since you and I have become friends-we are, you know -I've felt rotten once in a while about-oh, about getting earned. But you know how life is, and-" the job you "Every girl for herself. That's the rule. Remember the flowers you gave me in Seaside City? What little resentment --and it was little-I had for you, vanished then. Envy was left, Yolanda. Of course that stayed. I envy you now. And 

I'm frightened too. The future doesn't look too bright. Prestige is a queer thing. I'd really built up a lot of it in my time with ROLLING AMERICA. But a lot of it was lost the moment you were given the top. You can understand that." "Of course. From skate boy up the whole ladder the backbiters had their chance. And they took it, Laurel." "I've never been popular. Not like Yolanda. Except my skating kids, and others here and there, no one goes much beyond the admission that I can skate and teach. 

When you came my stock dropped. It gave the chance for for you,

them to say, 'Laurel isn't up to the lead. Old Hank had to look outside to replace Sue Holle.' And of course,” Laurel bent to lace her shoe, "the old turtle will never see me as more than a dull fixture in a single rink. So it's the future that gets me down a little." She straightened, rose to look gravely at the lovely girl who, strangely, seemed to be studying her. "Dab a little on my back. High, between the shoulders." "There. You'll do," Yolanda said. "You said that before. Tell me, what's up? What's my father over here for? On the face of it, I'm to skate a solo show, but the face is so peculiar, for the simple reason that 

Mr. Stevens ordinarily would just grunt, 'You're to do your stuff.' Instead-" "I'm tempted, Laurel-so, au revoir!" Her extremely high heels clicked as she walked from the room. 

Laurel opened the door in answer to a violent knock. Joe 

Clark, the rink manager, grinned at her and said, “Hiya, Laurel. Ready? You've a couple of minutes to line up in formation. Old Hank seems to be putting on some sort of private show. He grunted out a few orders. Maybe he's got a friend here to-night, some big shot, and he's out to impress him with show. Otherwise what for he got the best organist in the chain? A short All-Skate is to follow this line-up. Immediately. Then the competition." "Then I'm not giving a show?” "No." "Why the costume for judging? Yolanda's in street clothes." 

Joe shrugged. "No idea what's in the boss's head. My orders are to line up the floor men, and at their head you and Yolanda. She won't be on skates."

"Perhaps it's a new stunt to introduce the competition. To dress it up a bit. Oh, well, what's the difference?" "Just another night in the rinks," Joe agreed. "Come on, let's go and get it over with."

They went out together, and at the rail before the organ Laurel joined Yolanda and the eight floor men in their blue and gray uniforms, blue caps, white gloves. "Yolanda," Joe said, "when the music begins, walk out at the head of the parade. Laurel on skates next. Then you guys. Dress it up. Slow precision. Hank is on the sideline. 

Then stand in the usual formation, with Yolanda and Laurel side by side in front. O. K.?” "Yolanda," Laurel began, "this thing is all crazy, you without skates-" "You've seen circuses and-" "I never saw a circus.' "9 

Yolanda's eyes opened wide. "An American kid never saw—well, listen. The funniest thing to me in any circus is this. Elephant entrances into the arena are formal as the grand march at a state ball. Whether it's a poor little onering circus or the Big Show at Madison Square Garden, the elephants always follow the leader, each one holding tight with his trunk the silly thin tail of the elephant in front. It's an unbreakable rule. And every once in a while an elephant switches his tail and it flies out of the next fellow's trunk. Laurel, it's a scream. The poor devil behind has a fit until he can catch that tail again. He knows the rule! Something has gone wrong! So he fishes around as he lumbers along, desperately, despairingly grabbing here, there, eyes rolling, until at last he gets a firm grip on the straying tail. Then, quite happy and placid, he is his old self again and along he goes according to law and order.

Well, Laurel, you're just like that elephant. You're so used to the rules! And now you are uneasy. Because I'm not on skates. Because we're lining up for something special. Because Charles is at the organ. Elephant tails, darling, nothing more than that. And don't be an anxious pachyderm." 

Laurel laughed. "I'm not. It's curiosity." “Then it will soon be satisfied. There goes Charles. Now follow my cute little tail. It really is a cute one."

The slow march. The floor men in perfect precision extremely difficult to maintain behind Yolanda's walking pace.

Two thousand skaters lining the rail three-deep around the vast empty skating floor on which the white blaze of floodlights pours. The formation standing at stiff attention. The organ silent now....

Charles at the console spoke to Clark, who was standing ready to switch on the microphone. "What next?” "I got an announcement to make. After that a lot of noise from you. What is called the fanfare. All stops out. The whole business from drums to fake brass with the pedals driven into the floor. That's Hank's orders." "They'll be followed." Charles looked down at the formed group. How lovely that figure in silver! Laurel's back was so straight, her legs so young, so disciplined now into white marble. "I'd like to see her face. Her eyes must be looking coolly out on level distance. Lord God, I suppose this is some ceremony lifting that wench standing beside my kid into further glory in this skaters' paradise. That's my guess. 

Up, up, Yolanda, fly up. Plod on, Laurel. God damn it, I like Yolanda, she's a swell girl, but that's my poor little kid out there, and-and she's lived on bitter medicine too long. 

Too long."

The microphone rasped into current at the side of the organ.

Then Charles, leaden-hearted, settled back on the bench to listen. "Ladies and Gentlemen, attention, please. Many announcements are made from our stages in the course of a year, but never is a more important one made than those rare ones that concern the highest skating appointment in 

ROLLING AMERICA. Mr. Stevens and the entire organization, and any of the public that have enjoyed our rinks since they were first built, know that the greatest care has been taken to name only the best as the head dancing instructress of ROLLING AMERICA. We lost a fine one in Sue Holle. We gained a fine one in Yolanda Juarez-" the manager paused for what he knew would come. It came in thunder of voices, pounded skates, whistles, hands. "And now we are losing 

Yolanda. She requested of Mr. Stevens that she should not be mentioned to-night. That's Yolanda. She didn't want her successor to lose a gleam of the spotlight. But she must be spoken of, because she has been since last April a brilliant and popular performer. ROLLING AMERICA Wishes her luck."

The voice beside Charles stopped again. The good wishes of the crowd came up in another wave. And Charles tried to stifle the blaze of hope so suddenly kindled in his heart. 

No. Not again would he hope. The fall to follow would be too cruel. He had gone through it once before. That cold 

Stevens now, as he had done last year, would have combed the rinks of the country to find another show girl. One showier, more glittering, than Laurel. Charles, with bitter strength watched his hope die. 

Joe's augmented rasp resumed. "ROLLING AMERICA has

never taken more pleasure and pride in an appointment than it now takes in naming as head instructress a girl who has earned the post. Ladies and Gentlemen, our own, our very own-Laurel Easton!"

Charles, blind, shaken, actually strained to the point of nausea, raised his hands and brought them down with a crash on the keys and the fanfare was drowned in the yelled tribute behind the circling rail. It came in successive waves, rolling from end to end of the rink, from side to side. Then the voice of some child shrilled above all, "Hi, Laurel!" And

Charles lifted his head to see his daughter skate slowly from Yolanda's side out to the center of the floor. Alone there, like motion frozen, she stood in the beat of acclaim. Then 

Charles saw her arm go up in recognition of the welcome and in another instant the freezing melt and the white and silver figure glide swiftly backward in silken arabesque. 

All-Skate was signaled. 

Charles felt Joe Clark's hand slap his shoulder. Then he was alone on the stage, and the maelstrom of pity and excitement whirled out of his heart. Blindness and nausea vanished. There was the current medley of war tunes to play for All-Skate, beginning with Let's Remember Pearl Harbor, and a few moments later there was a girl in silver vaulting to the stage, and a rush to his side, and a white arm around his bent neck, and fresh young lips in a quick kiss. 

And far down the rink, standing at the rail, Henry Stevens grunted, "God damn it, Easton is murdering this number!" 

And the lovely, gold Yolanda at his side laughed. "Henry," she said, "I love you. If it wasn't for Sam I'd marry you.

YOLANDA's self-effacement on the occasion of Laurel's appointment was balanced a few days later by her final departure after the night session. Cheers, tears, flowers, crowds milling around, lights going on and off, photographers from the skating magazines, reporters from daily newspapers that ran skating columns, all painted a marvelous withdrawal for the lovely blond girl who was to take a midnight express for Nevada. And Yolanda was thorough. She was no aloof performer. Her good-bys covered the rink. Lavatory attendants, skate mechanics, first aid man, checkroom girl, floor crews, were given words, bright smiles, handclasps. 

She ran up to the stage to kiss Charles, she whimpered on

Henry Stevens' shoulder, and, at the door in burst of photographers' flashlights, flung her arms around Laurel. "You are America's best on wheels, Laurel. I'm going to stand Nevada fans on their heads and lots of my show will be things I've learned from you. Write to me. You and your father and old Hank are my only real friends. All this boomboom is just business and-Yolanda." 

The girls kissed in the final flash of cameras. 

The rink was quiet under the clattering removal of skates. 

At last it was empty of the public. 

Laurel, still in gold and purple uniform, was standing at 279

the refreshment bar with her father when Jim Brennan came, ordered Coca-Cola and insisted on paying for the drinks they were sipping. "Seen the late papers?” he asked. “Post, final edition?" “No,” Laurel said. “More about Yolanda? What a girl for publicity! She's a wonder. I mean that sincerely. She really puts roller-skating into the class of the theater, national sports. Skaters owe her a lot. And as for rink magnateswell!" "It's not about Yolanda. The first big lists of the boys at 

Pearl Harbor are given. Eddie's among them. Killed in action. Hell! I feel bad. He was a skater and a nice guy." 

Charles saw Laurel stare at the rink manager. Her bottle with the limp straw toppled over on the bar. "Did it say-anything about him?" "No. Just the name among the dead," Jim muttered. "Those Jap bastards!" 

Charles took his daughter's arm. "Come on, Laurel,” he said quietly, "we'll go home. Hurry into your clothes."

Stripping off her brief blue tights in the cooling dressingroom Laurel was shocked at her heart's reception. "Poor 

Eddie," and a sigh were all she had to give. "I ought to weep, at least feel like weeping. That boy. It's horrible that he seems now lost in the thousands I've skated for in the camps." But in the passage where Eddie had so often waited for a stolen kiss the sudden impact of the news lost its dulling effect and she leaned against the stained, scratched wall and wept. He had loved her, her and her skating and his own skating. Eddie flashing down a rink floor, whirling, leaping, landing on skates with the gracefulness and power of a wildcat. Eddie who had carried the hope of her to Pearl 

Harbor, the hope to marry her at the end of his military

service, Eddie who had died at the first burst of war. At the first stab from those vile Japanese. Death for that boy and skates somewhere that were rusting. For a terrible instant she thought of his body. Probably there was enough left of that to decay-the rust of skates, the gangrene- She shivered, thrust the picture into dark, and thoroughly wiping her eyes, returned to the dressing-room, switched on the lights, and made up her face. 

Then she hurried to the office. "Mr. Brennan, while I've skated often at the camps and naval stations, still I've had to turn down many requests. 

From now on none will be refused. At any hour within possible traveling distance from the rink I'll skate a show. My work in the rinks won't suffer, for I'll teach our professionals in my free hours or on my days off." "Laurel goes to war, eh?" "What little she can do. Good night."

She was silent at Charles' side on the short bus ride. In the lamplight of the living-room she stood facing him, offering a stammered good night. 

Charles forced a few words. "You know, Laurel, war, the country, patriotism, have sort of gone over my impractical head. During the first World War I was in a daze of alcohol and illness. In the few weeks of this one, since December seventh, I've continued in a daze of delight. You, Laurel, keep me in that daze. But now, Eddie. The war strikes close. 

I want to do something besides buying a few stamps and playing at camps." "Good night, Dad." "Good night. I'm sorry about Eddie."

She turned abruptly, rushed into her room and closed the door.

Charles slept fitfully on this and several following nights, but one came that brought return to realized comfort and refreshment. Laurel had come in very late from New Brunswick, where she had been teaching professionals. The weariness of her body was apparent, but her face was vividly alive. She sank into a deep chair. "The delight in teaching professionals is beyond anything for me. My kids, amateurs, are grand, enthusiastic, but the professionals are sheer joy. Even the ones that resent me! 

You see, they understand every bit of polish I give. I don't have to stop for fundamentals. I talk, demonstrate, and they take it away for practise. That makes it marvelous for a teacher." "You are happy!" "Yes. I'm flying high. Dad, to-day in a huge factory labels are being stamped on shoes. Soon there'll be a rush to buy the 'Laurel Easton Skating Shoes"." "That's grand." "Isn't it!" She stared at him. "What's the matter? You look queer. 

He laughed. "Not a thing."

And, he thought, there was not, for he was indifferent to waves of slight dizziness that followed periods of extreme weariness. What importance could such things hold, when he had won the only prize he wanted in life-his daughter? 

And she was happy, triumphant. Eddie. Ah, she was sad for him. But for herself it was a case of missing a nice boy, a partner. Not a lover. "She's married to her skates." "Dad, what are you muttering about?"

His evasion was urbane. "I was just telling myself that while rink dust is bad for your lungs, the irregular meals not good, still the hard skating seems to build up resistance to

illness more serious than coughs and colds. And there isn't an ounce of fat on you. All skated off without slavery to any diet. Maybe you're a lucky young kid at that." Well, that had gone over. And the touch of dizziness had passed.

Charles fell into talk with a man seated beside him on a bench. They juggled the weather, Charles with amiable indifference to the subject, the man morosely, and after the dull juggling the man said, "Why do you come here?" 

Charles smiled. "To give a pint of blood to the plasma bank." "Of course, of course. So do all these people,” indicating the long wide room of the parish house, “but-well, I guess it was a silly question. I'm a newspaper man and curious about motives. Maybe inquisitive-oh, hell, just nosey." "Why are you here?" "Lost a son on Bataan." "Ah. I'm sorry. My daughter lost a friend at Pearl Harbor." "But these others? They haven't all had personal losses. 

And this same scene all over the country. No. I'll at last admit it's patriotism." "Why a grudging admission?" "It's not exactly that. I'm glad to admit it. America awake. 

Going to sign up for regular blood donations?"

Charles nodded. "There's a directness about this contribution that appeals to me. I mean directness of application of the gift. To know that my dried blood will be injected straight into a desperately wounded fighter, will straightway give him life, strength-well, it appeals to me in such a personal way. Money contributions we all give, Red Cross,

USO, war bonds, the whole list, but this red pint of me will go straight to navy hospital or army field hospital. A man's body will drink it in. A wounded man will get another lease in borrowed time. That's a good thought.' 

"It is. I wish Ronny could see his old man. He'd grin. He was the greatest grinner I ever knew. Grinned his way through prep school at Pomfret, then Princeton, grinned his way into the marines, grinned his way to being a corporal, and, friend, I reckon if MacArthur was around when the boy was killed-and MacArthur probably was-the General saw Ronny grin his way into death. Jesus! I hope my old blood goes into some guy who'll go back and kill those bastards!" "I hope so," Charles said gently. "Great descriptive word, bastard. I've never heard it in such general use as in these days. Jap bastards. Nazi bastards. Seems to fit, regardless of dictionary irrelevance." "S' long. Here goes." The man slouched up to a table. 

And presently it was Charles' turn and he spoke to a woman in uniform who consulted a card index, and she directed him to another table where a young nurse pricked his finger, matching the drop of blood with color dots on a chart. She took his pulse. Then he was led behind curtains, told to strip to the waist. He looked down a long wide room where women and men of all ages were lying in white robes on beds, at their sides pint containers slowly filling with the fluid that would restore the dying to life, to battle, to the march toward ultimate victory, not only, Charles thought, to the victory of America, Great Britain, Russia, China, but to the victory of hope and freedom, of free idea, over tyranny, enslavement, and the loathsome darks of existence.

"Good morning. Sit here, please," said the examining doctor. "Now, then, Mr. Easton, I'll take your blood-pressure."

The wrapping grew tight on his arm, the peculiar sensation of the pumping increased. Serene in his complete ignorance of all the significance of blood-pressure knowledge and investigation, an ignorance fostered through a life of personal indifference, the years of sequestered, cloistered security at Neville, he waited. Far down the room an elderly woman was assisted from a bed, and led on a nurse's arm to an alcove where those who had given their blood were being strengthened, refreshed, with coffee, soup, sandwiches. He watched the elderly woman seat herself among the younger donors, and he smiled when he noticed that she was given a jigger of whisky. “Good old gal," he thought. "Hitler”he grinned-"that Nazi bastard-won't ever whip you or your grandsons." 

The doctor's low whisper was in his ear. "Sorry, Mr. 

Easton. The Red Cross can not use you. Your pressure is up."

A lifetime's suavity vanished from Charles' manner, countenance. "What!" he gasped. "Blood-pressure, rather seriously high. I'm sorry, but you see we don't take chances in these blood donations." "You mean I might die?" Charles snapped his fingers. "Like that?" "It's not a question of immediate alarm-if you take care of yourself. But I suggest that you see your regular doctor." "I have none." "Then you'd better get one. This condition needs corrective attention."

Charles stared into the kind tired face so close to his under the bright green-shaded table lamp.

"What is your occupation, Mr. Easton?" "Organist in a roller-skating rink." "Whew! You'd be wise to take up some sort of quieter work."

Charles rose. "I mustn't take up your time. This-this is a shock." He forced a smile. "Perhaps a heavier one than I'd have gone through with the blood-giving." "Under different circumstances I'd have cushioned this information, but-you see how it is. Now, don't get it into your thought that you'll collapse or die the next time you make hard effort at anything. That isn't so, at all. But your pressure is one hundred and eighty-five. That is, as I said, rather seriously high. No insurance company would consider you even if it were fifteen or twenty points lower. 

Now, good-by, and do go to a doctor for a check-up.”

As he put on his shirt he gazed down the room and was rocked with anger and a queer sense of shame that replaced, for a while, fear. What a derelict! Not even able to give a pint of blood in these desperate times! Bah! the uselessness of an old carcass. He belonged in a bone-yard and he might be in one before long if that decent and condemning doctor was right. He saw the elderly woman laughing, chattering to a young man, recovering quickly from the so-temporary weakness. "She's ten, fifteen years older than I, and she can give what I can't. That's a sour morsel to swallow."

In the street he turned up his collar against flurries of snow, and stood hesitant on the corner. How glad he was that he had told no one of his errand. It was not yet halfpast nine and he was not due at the rink until two. Over four hours to spend in thought and decision. He could stop in at any house displaying the sign of a doctor. But the verdict was clear enough. That chap back there knew his busi-

ness, or he wouldn't be in charge. In a few days, certainly, when he'd perhaps have recovered equilibrium, he would consult authority for the purpose of correction of his condition, but not this morning. The lump of depression had developed too suddenly. What could a few days matter?

On impulse he boarded a bus that stopped almost beside him. Harold. Good old Slade. That monument of insanity and intelligence and gruff friendliness. A long talk with a man of his own age. That would be valuable to a shocked heart, mind. He would have to gamble that Harold would be at home. The chance was worth taking in these four empty morning hours, for the living-room at home would be intolerable. And if Laurel had not already left for the rink, she might find things in his face. Laurel must not know. This bitterness was his and his alone. “Hah! And I thought I had won in life. My daughter and I. And now-I may die."

The ride to Princeton passed in a gloomy study. 

White magic had stolen over the campus, and Charles, standing on Nassau Street below his old rooms, surrendered for a little while to the ghostly charm of Princeton's quiet. 

The familiar peace of this winter composition whispered messages to his disturbed heart. Students running on the duckboards, snowballs and, quite suddenly on the hour, the tolling of the bell on Nassau Hall. He was again a music teacher, portfolio under arm, walking briskly to the Neville

Conservatory of Music. Then he was returning to his rooms, to coffee, to the high window-seat with its Christmas-card view of street and campus. There was quiet in his heart and snow, gently falling, placed silent touch on the windowpanes. Charles Easton in the harbor the years had rolled him toward with such undemanding, effortless assistance on his own part. White morning of peace in the graying years

of his life. A loveliness of air, a deep hollow of tranquillity. 

Here he could perch on the window-seat, mystery yarn in hand, and drowse away the generous free hours of his years. 

Here he could rest, obey the orders a physician would give, and extend the life that the tired machine grudged. How restful it was. Even his eyes were grateful for the soft light. 

He shivered. Life! Life was everything, and that kind, wise Red Cross doctor had told him how to stave off the dark oblivion of too early death. “I can walk half a mile, enter the campus and, in all probability, with appeal on lips gain reappointment in the school. A dependable faculty member who has wandered, repented, returned. Laurel will visit me when she can, I will lounge at her ringside when I can. Peace. Rest. And years gained by cautious acceptance of the harsh intelligence of science. Yes, I can walk that halfmile. Or I can hail that taxi and ride out to Slade's caravan and talk with my friend whose every breath will be contemptuous of the cloistered calm. I don't want his advice. 

I'll make my own decision. But I'm alone and frightened and I need to talk. Charles gave a twisted grin. "The proof that I need to talk is that I am standing on a street corner talking to myself." He lifted his arm, "Hi, there, taxi!”

The arrangement was made. Three dollars to take him out, to wait, to bring him back. He rolled westward from 

Princeton. The sun burst through clouds to glaze instantly the flanking fields with glitter and flash.

The door of the trailer opened to his knock and the monstrosity of paintbrush and sensuality puffed and rumbled in delighted welcome. For an instant Charles forgot worry, that peculiar shame of helpless exclusion from America's effort. What had the fear of death to do with the warm and amazingly successful blend of working spirit and carnal

pleasure that informed the very air of Harold's ménage? No change in this room on wheels, no change in the painter, no change in the slatternly, pretty, blond girl-woman of forty. 

Helen's heavily rouged lips smiled vaguely and then she returned to the stove and preparation of a meal that would be breakfast and lunch.

Charles told quietly of the morning and was not interrupted. At the end his nervousness broke through. "You must understand, Slade, that I've not come here for advice. A man must form his own decision. But I will not bother Laurel with it, and I wanted to talk. I'd like to know your opinion. That's not asking for advice. Whether I go ahead with my new life, or creep back into my old, will be decided to-day-by me. By no one else. So let's have your slant. Suppose it was you." "2 

Harold's laugh was grim. "And you think it isn't?" "You!" "Oh, I've seen no doctor. I'm as remiss as you've been until to-day. Moreover, I'm not going to one." He turned to his wife. "Pull on a sweater, Helen, and go out for five minutes. I want to talk to Easton."

She smiled, obeyed, and when the door had closed behind her Charles thought, "If bombs were falling and Slade told her to go, she'd step out in just that way. How the devil does he remain contentedly so close to a crippled mind? Ah, he loves this one who is lost in the twilight of her sensual engagement. 

But the mountain of flesh was rumbling. "Take me. God! Judging from color, fat, breathlessness, everything, my blood-pressure is probably a horror. But I close my eyes to it. What the hell? I just go along Gusto

Road, painting to excess, eating to excess, loving that woman to excess. Now, don't mistake me. I'm not a fatalist, talking about when my number comes up and all that God-damned rot, but safely or in danger I'd rather go along as I do. If

I die-" The beef and bone of his shoulders rose and sagged. 

Charles said, "But Helen? What of her? You are her God, master, foundation rock.” "She has present happiness. If I fall sick or die, yes, she would go to an institution. And be miserable. But until I drop, she's on the crest of the wave. Would she be better off if I should-warned by the medical profession-turn into a timid, irritable, starch-gaging counter of food ounces? And a measurer of doled-out physical love? Teetering on the tightwire of caution. Refusing the good red meat she cooks, the bed she makes tropical. Lord God, I'd even turn to painting in misty colors! Hah! Not I! And don't forget, my friend, what the doctors know about this pressure business isn't infallible. Not by a mile. They admit that. No. I'll live on the way I want to live. And to hell with it! It's too bad that doctor told you about your damned blood-pressure. I'd ignore it if I were you." 

Charles laughed. "I believe I shall."

But on the way back to Princeton the Slade tonic drained away, and Charles' chin sank deep into the wool scarf. “Decision. Decision. How in hell does a man arrive at decision?"

Charles shut his eyes. Those towers ahead. His to be close to once more if he cared to lift a hand. Before the Elizabeth bus came and left he could go out to good old Saunders and make his plea. The school might even give him three or four weeks of rest. The sane and sensible path into old age. 

In the opposite scale the happiness of his life with Laurel in

the rink. Was the balance even? Which would go down in the next five minutes? There was no time to fiddle about.

Against the swing to the rink was the hard fact that there was no such thing as possible rest. The rink paid and the organist played. It was as simple and stark as that. One did not stall with Hank Stevens. So in little more than an hour he would either enter the ELIZABETH ROLLER ARENA as permanent organist, or he would enter to agree to play a few final sessions until he could be replaced. If the latter he would, of course, be forced to tell Laurel something of his condition. She was too sharp for easy deception.

When the cab turned into Nassau Street, depositing him on the walk, once more before his old rooms, it seemed as though a light hand dropped onto his shoulder, and a faint voice whispered: "This is Princeton. You know me as I am now in winter, you know me in tender spring, in lush hot summer and in the brisk fires of autumn." And another voice: "I am Neville. I give you moderate work and hours of ease, long vacations. The voices joined, sharpened, “But if you go to that honkytonk of your daughter's, you may break down, collapse into invalidism that her strength must support for years.' 

Charles shuddered, and then was suddenly angry. “An invalid! On her shoulders. But I remember a quarter of a century ago sending a bullet into my forehead. A bullet that bungled its errand. If Laurel has to carry a broken, paralyzed man there will be no bungling. No letter of good-by.

No conclusion to be drawn. There'll be just a stumble in a 

New York subway station when a train comes roaring through and in the papers a paragraph of an accident. The train won't bungle." 

The bell on Nassau Hall commenced its solemn song.

Deep musical strokes. Come-back. Come-back. Comeback. 

Charles saw beyond the library the massive white form of the university chapel that seemed a cathedral. There was an organ waiting for quiet hands, and there were stained glass and the hush of stone and gold. The solemnity. That organ and the measured voice of the choir. The comforting dusk of his life. The lengthened years of borrowed time. But on that organ he had played on a starred night in spring music he had composed for a daughter restored to him, and she had sealed the restoration with a kiss.

The tolling of the bell ceased, the last stroke lingering in the thin cold air. The ghostly hand on his shoulder slipped off.

Charles grinned as the Elizabeth bus lumbered to a stop.... He left the bus a few blocks away from the rink and, at a newly opened food stand, bought the advertised specialty, half a small broiled chicken, fried potatoes, appetizingly arranged on a cardboard platter. One for Laurel, one for himself, wrapped in thick paper against the cold. She would have munched a sandwich, or forgotten luncheon. 

Reckless young idiot. Trudging to the rink he thought of the hot, tasty food she would enjoy. You just couldn't tell a kid, no matter how sensible she might be in most things, that a great degree of her skating for the next few hours would be affected by the food in her stomach. He glanced at his wrist. One-thirty. Time to spare. He entered the building by a side door and saw on benches the gathering personnel. 

Maizie, rolls of tickets under her arm, said, “Hi, Charlie." 

The floor manager called, "Oh, Charlie, good news for you. Old Hank's dug his hand into his pocket and your organ at last will be tuned."

A checkroom girl shifted the chewing-gum from one side of her mouth to the other, and stuck her tongue out at him. 

He smiled at this equivalent of friendly salute. 

And as he walked past the benches others shouted, muttered, or grinned their Charlies. Charles thought of his lifelong sense of "outsidedness." Well, that had been banished forever when he had found Laurel. True, in this land of whirling wheels there were resentments, jealousies, minor treacheries, along with the good warmths and generosities and loyalties, but the point was that-of good and bad he was a part. An insider. He was Charlie. He walked on toward Laurel, who was standing at the main entrance talking to Jim Brennan, and he answered her waved arm as she skated toward him. "Have you eaten?” he asked anxiously. "Yes; breakfast of coffee, toast. Hours ago." "Good! Sink your teeth into this." The tender chicken, the crisp potatoes appeared. "Oh, Dad, it's a miracle!"

She ate greedily and so delight was a condiment added to his food. He thought, "How far away is that doctor's pronouncement of my impending doom! And have I ever sat down to cozier luncheon than this? Cozy!" "What are you chuckling at?" she demanded. "This luncheon for two. I mean this snug little room with its perfectly appointed table, close warm walls and intimate floor." His glance swept the acre or more of hardwood floor, the cold high roof, the distant walls of concrete, steel and glass. "Yes. Cozy, isn't it?" "This is my home," she said. "And some of my best sleeping is done right on these benches, between sessions."

"It's my home too, Laurel. The best I've ever had. I doubt if the face of death itself could make me leave it."

She looked at him sharply, the last morsel of chicken arrested at her lips, but his face was so innocent, so bland, so characteristically urbane, that she popped the sliver of white meat into her mouth, wiped her fingers on the paper napkin, and, after gathering up the cardboard platters, skated toward a trash receptacle. The group on the far-off bench separated, went to their posts for the afternoon session. 

Charles strolled to the stage, entered the passage in the rear and presently, behind the closed crimson curtains, began to play the overture. The curtains were drawn slowly aside and he looked out at his selected world. He saw the impatient crowds behind the restricting rail of the vast oblong arena, he saw the unvarying, rigid formation of the floor crew, guards, instructors, instructresses, and at the head of the group he saw the strong, slender form of his daughter, her skin like white marble in contrast with the royal blue and gold satin of the rink uniform. 

And, playing with the required crash and power, Charles spoke. "I may die to-night, next month, or twenty years from this hour, but now I'm happy under this echoing cavernous roof, for I am not alone. There on the floor, gravely attentive to her work, stands the one person I love in all the world. I'm lucky, lucky. Skate on, Laurel. Skate on, my daughter."

His hand pressed down on the emphasized final bar; the flood-lights poured onto the arena; the crowds streamed through the openings in the rail; and Charles saw Laurel glide toward the inner oblong, her body balanced in that incomparable Laurel Easton arabesque. (3)
